Verify Cantor's Theorem in the finite case by showing that
if $S$ is finite then the cardinality of its power set is 
$\sizeof{\powerset(S)}=2^{\sizeof{S}}$.
\begin{answer}\verified
Before we do the proof, consider $S=\set{0,1,2,3}$.
These are the elements of $\powerset(S)$.
\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{l}
  \set{}, \set{0}, \set{1}, \set{2}, \set{0,1}. \set{0,2}, \set{1,2}, \set{0,1,2} \\ 
  \quad \set{3}, \set{0,3}, \set{1,3}, \set{2,3}, \set{0,1,3}. \set{0,2,3}, \set{1,2,3}, \set{0,1,2,3} \\ 
\end{array}
\end{equation*}
So, fixing $\hat{s}=3\in S$ gives two classes of
subsets, those sets containing~$\hat{s}$ and those not containing~$\hat{s}$,
and the two have the same number of members. 

Our proof uses induction on $\sizeof{S}$.
The base case is that $\sizeof{S}=0$, so $S$ is empty.
Then $\powerset(S)=\set{\emptyset}$ and $\sizeof{\powerset(S)}=1$,
as required.

For the inductive step fix $k\in\N^+$, assume that the statement is 
true for $n=0$, $n=1$, \ldots $n=k$, and consider a set $S$ where 
$\sizeof{S}=k+1$.
Because $\sizeof{S}=k+1$ the set has an element, $\hat{s}\in S$.
The inductive hypothesis applies to $S-\set{\hat{s}}$, so 
$\powerset(S-\set{\hat{s}})$ has $2^k$-many elements.
As illustrated in the example above, 
the elements of $\powerset(S-\set{\hat{s}})$, which do not contain $\hat{s}$, 
are in correspondence with the elements of $\powerset(S)$ that do 
contain~$\hat{s}$.
Thus the total number of elements of~$\powerset(S)$
is $2\cdot 2^k$, which equals $2^{k+1}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \href{https://viterbi-web.usc.edu/~mjneely/docs/infinity.pdf}{Michael J~Neely }
\end{credit}
The definition $R=\set{s\suchthat s\notin f(s)}$ is the key to the proof of 
Cantor's Theorem, \cref{th:RealsNotCountable}.
This story illustrates the idea:~a high school yearbook asks 
each graduating student~$s_i$ make a list $f(s_i)$ of class
members that they predict will someday be famous. 
Define the set of humble students~$H$ to be those who are not on their
own list.
Show that no student's list equals~$H$.
\begin{answer}\verified
Suppose that $f(s_i)=H$.
There are two possibilities,~either $s_i\in H$ or else $s_i\notin H$.
If $s_i\in H$ then student~$s_i$ is a member of their own list, so 
$s_i$ is not humble, so this violates its case assumption.
On the other hand, if $s_i\notin H$ then $s_i$ is not a member of their own
list, so $s_i$ is humble, which also violates this case assumption.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The proof of \cref{th:RealsNotCountable} works around the fact that
some numbers have more than one base ten representation.
Base two also has the property that 
some numbers have more than one representation; 
an example is $0.01000\ldots$ and $0.00111\ldots$.
% But in a base~two argument, when building~$z$ there is no way to avoid
% the digits~$0$ and~$1$.
How could you make the argument work in base~two?
\begin{answer}\verified
One way is to take the diagonal bits two at a time; 
for instance changing $01$ to $10$, and changing any other two-bit
sequence to $01$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Answer from Stack Exchange member
  \href{https://math.stackexchange.com/a/271126}{Alex Becker}.
\end{credit}
The discussion after the statement of \cref{th:RealsNotCountable}
includes that the real number~$1$ has two different
decimal representations, $1.000\ldots=0.999\ldots$\sentencespace
\begin{exparts}
\item
Verify this equality using the formula for an infinite geometric series, 
$a+ar+ar^2+ar^3+\cdots =a\cdot 1/(1-r)$.
\item 
Show that if a number has two different decimal representations then
in the leftmost decimal place where they differ, they differ by~$1$.
\hint~that is the biggest difference that the remaining decimal places
can make up. 
\item In addition show that, for the one with the larger digit in
that first differing place, all of the digits to its right are~$0$,
while the other representation has that all of the remaining digits are $9$'s. 
\hint~this is similar to the prior item.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
In $a+ar+ar^2+ar^3+\cdots =a\cdot 1/(1-r)$,
take $a=9$ and $r=0.1$.
The left side is $9+0.9+0.09+0.009+\cdots=9.999\,99\ldots$\sentencespace
The right side is $9\cdot 1/(1-(0.1))=9/0.9=1/0.1=1/(1/10)=10$.
Subtract~$9$ from both sides to get $0.999\,99\ldots=1$.
\item
Consider a real number with two representations.
Write the representations
as $\vec{x}=\sequence{x_N,x_{N-1},\ldots}$ and 
$\vec{y}=\sequence{y_N,y_{N-1},\ldots}$,
subject to $x_i,y_i\in\set{0,1,\ldots 9}$ and to this condition.
\begin{equation*} 
  \sum_{N\geq i}x_i\cdot 10^i=\sum_{N\geq i}y_i\cdot 10^i
  \tag{*}
\end{equation*} 
(The number $N\in\Z$ is the furthest left nonzero decimal place among 
the two representions.)  
For instance, for $1.000\ldots=0.999\ldots$ we have
$x_0=1$, $x_{-1}=0$, $x_{-2}=0$, \ldots, 
giving $1\cdot 10^{0}+0\cdot 10^{-1}+0\cdot 10^{-2}+\cdots$,
and
$y_0=0$, $y_{-1}=9$, $y_{-2}=9$, \ldots,
giving $0\cdot 10^{0}+9\cdot 10^{-1}+9\cdot 10^{-2}+\cdots$\,.

Let the leftmost decimal place where the two differ be~$M$,
so that $x_M\neq y_M$ and $x_i=y_i$ for $i\in\set{N,N-1,\ldots M}$.
One of $x_M$ or~$y_M$ is larger; without loss of generality
suppose that $x_M>y_M$.
We must show that $x_M=y_M+1$ 
(as it does for the example $\sequence{1,0,0\ldots}$ and
$\sequence{0,9,9\ldots}$).

The reason is that the remaining digits can add to a number no larger than
$\sum_{M>i}9\cdot 10^{i}$.
This adds to $10^{M}$ by the geometric series formula.
If $x_M-y_M>1$ then
$(\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}x_i10^i)-(\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}y_i10^i)=(x_M-y_M)\cdot 10^M$
is at least $2\cdot 10^M$, and the remaining digits cannot make up the 
difference to satisfy condition~($*$). 
\item
Continuing with the notation from the prior item, 
we want to show that $x_{j}=0$ and $y_{j}=9$ for 
$j\in\set{M-1,M-2,\ldots}$\sentencespace
The argument is similar to the prior one and we will do only $j=M-1$.
We have that the representations differ by one in decimal place~$M$.
\begin{equation*}
\big(\!\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}x_i10^i\big)
  -\big(\!\sum_{N\leq i\leq M}y_i10^i\big)
  = 10^{M}
\end{equation*}
Suppose that $y_{M-1}-x_{M-1}\leq 8$.
Then
\begin{equation*}
\big(\!\sum_{N\leq i\leq M-1}x_i10^i\big)
  -\big(\!\sum_{N\leq i\leq M-1}y_i10^i\big)
  \geq 10^{M}-8\cdot 10^{M-1}
\end{equation*}
and the remaining digits can add to a number no larger than
$\sum_{M-1>i}9\cdot 10^{i}=1\cdot 10^{M-1}$,
again by the geometric series formula.
Adding that into the prior equation is not enough to satisfy~($*$).
(The cases of $j=M-2$, $j=M-3$, etc.\@ are similar.)
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that there is no set of all sets.
\hint~use \cref{th:CantorsThm}.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Suppose otherwise, that $\mathcal{C}$ is the set of all sets.
  Then $\powerset(\mathcal{C})$ would be a collection of sets so we would have
  $\powerset(\mathcal{C})\subseteq \mathcal{C}$.
  That would give $\sizeof{\powerset(\mathcal{C})}\leq\sizeof{\mathcal{C}}$,
  which would contradict Cantor's Theorem.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:SchroderBernstein}
\Cref{def:CardLessThanOrEqual} extends the definition of equal cardinality 
to say that $\sizeof{A}\leq \sizeof{B}$ if there is a one-to-one 
function from $A$ to~$B$.
The \definend{Schröder–Bernstein theorem} is that if both 
$\sizeof{S}\leq \sizeof{T}$ and $\sizeof{T}\leq \sizeof{S}$ 
then $\sizeof{S}=\sizeof{T}$.
We will walk through the proof.
It depends on finding chains of images:~for 
any $s\in S$ we form the associated chain by iterating application of the
two functions, both to the right 
and the left, as here. 
\begin{equation*}
  \ldots{}\,f^{-1}(g^{-1}(s)),g^{-1}(s),s,f(s),g(f(s)),f(g(f(s)))\, \ldots{}
  % \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
(Starting with $s$ the chain to the right is 
$s,f(s),g(f(s)),f(g(f(s))), \ldots$ while the chain to the left 
is $\ldots{}\,f^{-1}(g^{-1}(s)),g^{-1}(s),s$.)
For any $t\in T$ define the associated chain similarly. 

An example is to take a set of integers $S=\set{0,1,2}$ and 
a set of characters $T=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$,
and consider the two 
one-to-one functions $\map{f}{S}{T}$ and $\map{g}{T}{S}$
shown here.
\begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{c|c}
    $s$    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$f(s)$}  \\ \hline
    $0$    &$\str{b}$  \\
    $1$    &$\str{c}$  \\
    $2$    &$\str{a}$  
  \end{tabular}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/maps/maps03.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \begin{tabular}{c|c}
    $t$       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$g(t)$}  \\ \hline
    $\str{a}$ &$0$   \\
    $\str{b}$ &$1$    \\
    $\str{c}$ &$2$     
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
Starting at $0\in S$ gives 
a single chain that is cyclic,
$\ldots{}\,0,\str{b},1,\str{c},2,\str{a},0\,\ldots$\sentencespace
\begin{exparts}
\item 
  Consider $S=\set{0,1,2,3}$ and $T=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}$.
  Let $f$ associate $0\mapsto\str{a}$, $1\mapsto\str{b}$, $2\mapsto\str{d}$
  and $3\mapsto\str{c}$.
  Let $g$ associate $\str{a}\mapsto 0$, $\str{b}\mapsto 1$, $\str{c}\mapsto 2$
  and $\str{d}\mapsto 3$.
  Check that these maps are one-to-one.
  List the chain associated with each element of $S$ and the 
  chain associated with each element of $T$.
\item For infinite sets a chain can have a first element, an 
   element without any preimage.  
   Let $S$ be the even numbers and let $T$ be the odds.
   Let $\map{f}{S}{T}$ be $f(x)=x+1$ and let $\map{g}{T}{S}$ be $g(x)=x+1$.
   Show each map is one-to-one.
   Show there is a single chain and that it has a first element.
\item Argue that we can assume without loss of generality that $S$ 
  and~$T$ are disjoint sets.
\item Assume that $S$ and $T$ are disjoint and that $\map{f}{S}{T}$
  and $\map{g}{T}{S}$ are one-to-one.
  Show that every element of either set is in a unique chain, 
  and that each chain is of one of four kinds: (i)~those that
  repeat after some number of terms
  (ii)~those that continue infinitely in both directions without repeating
  (iii)~those that continue infinitely to the right but stop on the left
  at some element of~$S$, 
  and (iv)~those that continue infinitely to the right but stop on the left
  at some element of~$T$.
\item Show that for any chain the function below 
  is a correspondence between the 
  chain's elements from~$S$ and its elements from~$T$.
  \begin{equation*}
    h(s)=
    \begin{cases}
      f(s) &\case{if $s$ is in a sequence of type~(i), (ii), or~(iii)}  \\
      g^{-1}(s) &\case{if $s$ is in a sequence of type~(iv)}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item These are the chains for~$S$.
\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{r|c}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{s\in S}
  &\text{\makebox[2in][c]{\tabulartext{Chain}}}  \\ \hline
  0 &  \ldots\,0,\str{a},0,\str{a}\,\ldots   \\
  1 &  \ldots\,1,\str{b},1,\str{b}\,\ldots    \\
  2 &  \ldots\,2,\str{d},3,\str{c},2,\str{d}\,\ldots\\
  3 &  \text{--\, same as the chain for $2$\,--}
\end{array}
\end{equation*}
These are the chains for~$T$.
\begin{equation*}
\begin{array}{r|c}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{t\in T}
  &\text{\makebox[2in][c]{\tabulartext{Chain}}}  \\ \hline
  \str{a} &  \ldots\,\str{a},0,\str{a},0\,\ldots   \\
  \str{b} &  \ldots\,\str{b},1,\str{b},1\,\ldots    \\
  \str{c} &  \ldots\,\str{c},2,\str{d},3,\str{c},2\,\ldots\\
  \str{d} &  \text{--\, same as the chain for \str{c}\,--}
\end{array}
\end{equation*}
\item Let $S=\set{0,2,4,6,\ldots}$, let $T=\set{1,3,5,\ldots}$,
and take $\map{f}{S}{T}$ to be $f(s)=s+1$, and 
$\map{g}{T}{S}$ to be $g(t)=t+1$.
The function~$f$ is one-to-one because if $f(s)=f(\hat{s})$ then
$s+1=\hat{s}+1$, and so $s=\hat{s}$.
The function~$g$ is one-to-one in the same way.

The chain associated with~$0\in S$ is
$0,1,2,3\ldots$\sentencespace
It contains all the elements of both sets, and so the chain is unique.
It also has a first element, that is, there is no element of~$T$ 
that is~$g^{-1}(0)$.
\item If they are not already disjoint, if $S\cap T\neq\emptyset$,
then we can just color all the elements of~$S$ purple and all the elements 
of~$T$ gold and that will make them disjoint for sure.
More precisely, consider the set $\set{0}\times S$ of pairs
$\sequence{0,s}$ along with the set $\set{1}\times T$ of pairs
$\sequence{1,t}$.
These are disjoint sets because any two elements differ in the
first entry.
Obviously they correspond to the original sets $S$ and~$T$.
\item First we show that every element is in a unique chain.
Fix some $x\in S\cup T$.
Because we assume that the two sets are disjoint, the function operation that 
applies to $x$ is determined, meaning that if $x\in S$ then for the chain
we next consider $f(x)$ while if $x\in T$ then we are next doing~$g$.
With that, all the elements of the chain to the right of~$x$ are determined.
And, because the two functions are one-to-one, all of the
element to the left are likewise determined also.

As to the four possibilities, either a chain is finite, that is, it repeats, 
or it is infinite.
If it repeats then it is case~(i).
If it is infinite then it either does not have an initial element, and
so is case~(ii), or else it does.
If it does have an initial element then either that element is from 
the set~$S$, as in case~(iii), or else that element is from the
set~$T$, as in case~(iv).
\item  
We will consider the correspondence for each chain type below.
But first note that because each case is a restriction of a function, each is
well-defined.
That is, never does any of these maps associate two different outputs
with the same input.

A type~(i) chain
repeats after some number of elements 
$s_0,t_0,s_1,t_1,\ldots,s_{n-1},t_{n-1},s_n=s_0$
(because the sets are disjoint it cannot repeat after a single element, and
we can thus take the first element to be a member of~$S$).
% So this is the chain, with $s=s_0$.
% \begin{equation*}
%   s,f(s),g(f(s)),f(g(f(s))),\ldots{} g(f(\,\cdots f(s)))=s
% \end{equation*}
The function~$f$ makes these associations.
\begin{equation*}
  s_0\mapsto t_0\quad s_1\mapsto t_1,\quad\ldots\quad s_{n-1}\mapsto t_{n-1}
\end{equation*}
This is clearly a correspondence among the elements of the chain.
It is manifestly onto the set 
$\set{t_0,t_1,\ldots t_{n-1}}$ and
it is one-to-one because the~$t_j$'s are 
distinct, or else the chain would be shorter.

For a type~(ii) chain 
\begin{equation*}
  \ldots{}\,f^{-1}(g^{-1}(s)),\,g^{-1}(s),\,s,\,f(s),\,g(f(s)),\,f(g(f(s))),\, \ldots{}
  % \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
the function~$f$ associates these.
\begin{equation*}
  \ldots\quad
  f^{-1}(g^{-1}(s))\mapsto g^{-1}(s)\quad
  s\mapsto f(s)\quad 
  g(f(s))\mapsto f(g(f(s))),\quad\ldots
\end{equation*}
This also is clearly a correspondence.

A type~(iii) chain 
\begin{equation*}
  s,\,f(s),\,g(f(s)),\,f(g(f(s))),\, \ldots{}
\end{equation*}
has these associations.
\begin{equation*}
  s\mapsto f(s)\quad 
  g(f(s))\mapsto f(g(f(s))),\quad\ldots
\end{equation*}
Again, clearly a correspondence.

The one that takes some thought is a chain of type~(iv).
\begin{equation*}
  t,\,g(t),\,f(g(t)),\,g(f(g(t)),\, \ldots{}
\end{equation*}
We want a correspondence function from $S$ to~$T$.
Consider these associations.
\begin{equation*}
  g(t)\mapsto t\quad 
  g(f(t))\mapsto f(t),\quad\ldots
\end{equation*}
Because the function~$g$ is one-to-one its inverse is defined,
over its range. 
So this association is a well-defined function.
Like the chain types above, this also is clearly a correspondence.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{cardinality|)}\index{infinity|)}

\end{exercises}
\index{diagonalization|)}












%================================================
\section{Universality}\index{universality|(}

We have seen a number of Turing machines:~one whose behavior is that its 
output is the successor of its input, 
one that interprets its input as two numbers and adds them, etc.
These are single-purpose devices, where to get different input-output behavior
we needed to get a new machine, that is, new hardware.
This was what we meant by
saying that a good first take on Turing machines is that they 
are more like a modern computer program than a modern computer.

The picture below shows
programmers of an early electronic computer. 
They are changing its behavior by 
changing its circuits, using the patch cords.
\begin{center}  \small
  \includegraphics[width=.53\textwidth]{background/pix/rewiring.jpg} \\ 
  \begin{credit}
    \textit{ENIAC Programmers, 1946} \href{https://artsandculture.google.com/asset/eniac-programmers-united-states-army-united-states-army/4AFgcMwMqg1mRw}{U. S. Army Photo from Army Research Labs Technical Library}
    % (US government works not subject to copyright.)
  \end{credit}
  \captionof{figure}{\protect\citetext{ENIAC, reconfigure by rewiring.}{Jean Jennings (left), Marlyn Wescoff (center), and Ruth Lichterman program the ENIAC, circa 1946. U. S. Army Photo}}
\end{center}
Imagine having a phone where  
to change from running a browser to taking a call you must 
pull one chip and replace it with another. 
The patch cords are an improvement over a soldering iron but are 
not a final answer.



\subsection{Universal Turing machine}\index{Universal Turing machine|(}\index{Turing machine!universal|(}
\citetext{A pattern in technology is for jobs done in hardware to migrate to software}{%
  One story that illustrates the naturalness of this involves
  the English mathematician C~Babbage, and his protogee A~Lovelace.
  In 1812 Babbage was developing tables of logarithms.
  These were calculated by computers\Dash the word then current
  for the people who computed them by hand.
  To check the accuracy he had two people do the same table and compared.
  He was annoyed at the number of discrepencies and 
  had the idea to build a machine to do the computing.
  He got a government grant to design and construct a
  machine called the difference engine, 
  which he started in 1822. 
  This was a single-purpose device, what we today would call a calculator.
  One person who became interested in the computations was an
  acquaintance of his, Lovelace (who at the time was named Byron, as
  she was the daughter of the poet Lord Byron).  
  \protect\begin{center}
  \protect\small
  \protect\begin{tabular}[t]{c}
  \protect\includegraphics[height=1in]{background/pix/babbage.jpg} \\
  \protect\label{fig:babbage}\protect\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Babbage}{Charles Babbage, 1791--1871} % wikipedia
  \protect\end{tabular} 
  \protect\hspace{3em}
  \protect\begin{tabular}[t]{c}
  \protect\includegraphics[height=1in]{background/pix/adabyron.jpg} \\
  \protect\label{fig:adabyron}\protect\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ada_Lovelace}{Ada Lovelace (nee Byron), 1815--1852} % 
  \protect\end{tabular}
  \protect\end{center}
  However, this machine was never finished because
  Babbage had the thought to make a device that would be programmable,
  and that was too much of a temptation.
  Lovelace contributed an extensive set of notes on a proposed
  new machine, the analytical engine, and has become known as the
  first programmer.}.
The classic example is weaving.
\begin{center}
  % http://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Loomwork.jpg
  \includegraphics[width=.33\textwidth]{background/pix/loom.jpg}
  \hspace*{2em}
  % http://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Deutsches_Technikmuseum_Berlin_February_2008_0013.JPG 
  \includegraphics[width=.33\textwidth]{background/pix/jaquardpunchedcards.jpg}
\end{center}
Weaving by hand, as the loom operator on the left is doing,
is intricate and slow.
We can make a machine to reproduce her pattern.
But what if we want a different pattern; do we need another machine? 
In 1801 
\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacquard_weaving}{J~Jacquard} 
built a loom like the one on the right, 
\citetext{controlled by paper cards}{%
  It weaves with hooks whose positions, raised or lowered, are determined by 
  holes punched in the cards}.
Getting a different pattern does not require a new loom, it only requires 
swapping cards.
Turing introduced the analog for computing devices.

%<*th:UniversalTM>
\begin{theorem}[Turing, 1936] \label{th:UTM}
There is Turing machine that when
given the input $e,x$ will have the same   
output behavior as does $\TM_e$ on input~$x$.
\end{theorem}
%</th:UniversalTM>

%<*discussion:UniversalTM> 
So there is a single Turing machine that we can tell 
``Act like this other machine with this input'' and it can do that.
We don't need infinitely many different machines, we just need this one.
This is a 
\definend{Universal Turing Machine}.\footnote{%
  Another often-used way to define a Universal Turing machine that is to 
  have it take the single-number input $\cantor(e,x)$.}
\index{Universal Turing machine}\index{Turing machine!universal}
% We denote such a machine with~$\UTM$.
%</discussion:UniversalTM> 

``Have the same output behavior'' means that if 
$\TM_e$ on input~$x$ halts then the universal machine gives the same output 
and if $\TM_e$ on input~$x$ does not halt then the universal machine also 
does not halt.\footnote{%
  A technical point:~Turing machines have a tape alphabet.
  So a universal machine's input or output can only
  involve symbols that it is defined as able to take. 
  If another machine, machine~$e$, has a different tape alphabet
  then how can the universal machine simulate it?
  As usual, we define things so that the universal machine
  manipulates representations of machine~$e$'s alphabet.
  This is like the way an everyday computer uses binary
  to represent decimals.}

\margingraphic{0.2\textwidth}{background/pix/snake.png}{\label{fig:ouroboros}\href{https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?curid=36915535}{An ouroboros, a snake swallowing its own tail}}\index{ouroboros}
Before we give the argument for the existence such a machine, 
we will first address a question.
A universal machine may seem to present a 
chicken and egg problem:~how can we give a Turing machine as input to 
a Turing machine?
In particular, since the universal machine is itself a Turing machine,
the theorem seems to allow the possibility of giving it
to itself\Dash won't feeding a machine to itself lead to infinite regress?

\label{page:RepresentingTM}
% We don't feed a machine to itself.
We run Turing machines by loading symbols on the tape
and pressing \texttt{Start}.
True, we can think of a universal machine's input pair of numbers 
as computationally equivalent to 
the source of a machine and that machine's input.
But even so,
the universe won't explode\Dash
you can absolutely use a text editor to edit its own source,
or write a compiler and use it to compile itself.
Similarly, you can feed a universal machine its own number. 
This is not to say that nothing interesting happens as a result, 
but rather to say that there is no inherent impossibility.

We next observe that, in some ways, universal machines are familiar from 
everyday computing work.

\margingraphicnomag*[l]{0.2\textwidth}{background/asy/hp/hp14.pdf}
% Universal machines
% change hardware behavior in software.
% \citetext{This is what a computer's operating
% system does}{%
% }.
Consider your computer and its operating system, 
along with some program that will run on it.\footnote{% 
  The figure is a 
  \citetext{flow chart}{%
    Flowcharts are widely used to sketch algorithms; here is one from XKCD.
    \protect\begin{center}
    \protect\small
    \protect\begin{tabular}{c}
      \protect\includegraphics[width=0.25\textwidth]{background/pix/xkcdcalc.png} \\
      \protect\label{fig:xkcdcalc}\protect\href{https://xkcd.com/2117/}{Courtesy xkcd} % wikipedia
    \protect\end{tabular}
    \protect\end{center}
    See also~\protect\url{http://archive.computerhistory.org/resources/text/Remington_Rand/Univac.Flowmatic.1957.102646140.pdf}.}, 
  which gives a high level outline of a routine, here of an operating system 
  or of a Universal Turing machine.
  We use three types of boxes.
  Rectangles are for the ordinary flow of control.
  Round corner boxes are for \protect\texttt{Start} and 
  \protect\texttt{End}.
  Diamond boxes, which appear in later flow charts, 
  are for decisions, \protect\texttt{if} statements.
}\index{flow chart}
Think of the program 
as like some Turing machine, $\TM_e$. 
It may take input, a string of \str{0}'s and \str{1}'s, that we can 
interpret as the number~$x$.
When asked, the operating system loads the program into 
memory and then feeds the input to that program.
That is, when asked, the operating system arranges that 
your computer will 
behave like the stored program, machine~$e$ with input~$x$. 
Universal Turing machines
change hardware behavior in software,
as does the operating system.
No patch chords.

Another everyday computer experience that is like a universal
machine is an interpreter.
Below is a session with a Scheme interpreter.
After the interpreter's command line invocation, 
in line~$1$ the system gets the source of a program that takes in 
\codeinline!i! and 
sum the first \codeinline!i!~numbers.
In line~$2$ the interpreter 
runs that source with the input~$i=4$. 
and returns the output of~$10$.
\begin{lstlisting}
$ csi

CHICKEN
(c) 2008-2013, The Chicken Team
(c) 2000-2007, Felix L. Winkelmann
Version 4.8.0.5 (stability/4.8.0) (rev 5bd53ac)
linux-unix-gnu-x86-64 [ 64bit manyargs dload ptables ]
compiled 2013-10-03 on aeryn.xorinia.dim (Darwin)

#;1> (define sum
  (lambda (i tot)
    (if (= i 0)
	tot
	(sum (- i 1) (+ tot i)))))
#;2> (sum 4 0)
10
#;3> (<@\color{highlightcolor}(lambda (i) (if (= i 0) 1 0))@> 1)
0
\end{lstlisting}  % $
Illustrating this even more directly, in line~$3$ 
the interpreter gets as a single expression
both the source of a routine (it is shown highlighted) and the 
input, and it returns the result of applying the source to the input.
That is, like the loom's punched cards, our mechanism
allows us to swap behaviors in and out,
at will.

\label{page:UTM}
The most direct example of our everyday experience with 
computing systems that act as universal machines 
is a programming language's \codeinline!eval! statement.\index{eval}
\begin{lstlisting}
#;1> (define (utm s)
  (eval s (scheme-report-environment 5)))
#;2> (define TEST '(lambda (i) (if (= i 0) 1 0)))
#;3> TEST
(lambda (i) (if (= i 0) 1 0))
#;4> ((utm TEST) 5)
0
#;5> ((utm TEST) 0)
1
\end{lstlisting}
In line~$1$ the \codeinline{utm} is defined to evaluate its input
(the \codeinline!scheme-report-environment!
ensures that the routines defined in the specification for 
Scheme~$5$ are available to \codeinline!eval!).\footnote{%
  Writing a program that allows general users to  
  evaluate arbitrary code
  is powerful but not safe,
  especially if these users just surf in from the Internet.
  Restricting which commands the user can evaluate,
  known as sandboxing, forms part of
  being careful with that power.
  For us, however, the software engineering issues are not relevant.}
In line~$2$ the \codeinline!(lambda (i) (if (= i 0) 1 0))!
is quoted so that Scheme will keep it as
a string and not execute it.
Lines~$4$ and~$5$ have that string fed to \codeinline!utm!,
which runs \codeinline!eval! on it, so the first entry in the
list is now a routine. 
This routine acts on the argument, here $5$ or~$0$, producing the output.

To finish, to justify \cref{th:UTM},
we have already exhibited what amounts to a Universal Turing machine.
In the Turing Machine simulator section on
page~\pageref{extra:TuringMachineSimilator} 
we gave code that reads an arbitrary Turing machine from a file,
and then simulates it.
The code is in Scheme but 
by Church's Thesis we could write this as a Turing machine.
\index{Universal Turing machine|)}\index{Turing machine!universal|)}




\subsection{Uniformity}\index{uniformity|(}
We've argued that there are functions that no program can perform.
Consider this job:~given a real number $r\in\R$,
write a program to produce its digits.
More precisely, the job is to produce a family of machines,
a $\TM_r$ for each~$r\in\R$,
such that when given $n\in\N$ as input, $\TM_r$ returns the
$n$-th decimal place of~$r$ (for~$n=0$,
it returns the integer to the left of the decimal point).

We know that because of cardinality 
there is no such family;~there
are countably many Turing machines
but uncountably many reals.
But why not?
One of the enjoyable things about coding is the feeling of being able to
get the machine to do anything\Dash what's stopping us from producing
whatever digits we like?  

There are some real number $r$'s 
for which there indeed is such a program.
An easy one is~$r=1/4$.
For a more generic number, some kind of $r=0.703\ldots$\,,
we might momentarily imagine brute-forcing it.
\begin{lstlisting}
read n
if n==0:
    print 0
if n==1:
    print 7
elif n==2:
    print 0
  ...
\end{lstlisting}
But that's silly,
because programs have finite length.
We can have \codeinline{if .. elif ..} branches for a few
cases but past some point we need code that 
handles the remaining input~$n$'s uniformly.

Thus, the fact that Turing machines have only finitely many instructions
imposes on what we can do a requirement of uniformity.

\begin{example} \label{ex:NineInPi}
This requirement can have surprising consequences.
Consider~$\pi=3.14159\ldots$ and
consider the problem of writing 
a program that inputs a number~$n$ and  
decides whether somewhere in $\pi$'s decimal expansion
there is a sequence of 
\citetext{consecutive nines}{%
  At the $762$-nd decimal point there are 
  six nines in a row.  
  This is call the Feynman point; see 
  \protect\url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feynman_point}.
  Most experts guess that for any~$n$ the decimal 
  expansion contains a sequence of 
  $n$ consecutive nines but no one has proved or disproved that.}
of length~$n$.    

Here is an answer:~there are two possibilities.
Either for all lengths such a sequence exists or else there is some
number $n_0$ where a sequence of $9$'s exists for lengths less than~$n_0$ 
and no such sequence exists for $n\geq n_0$.
Therefore one of these two programs solves the problem\Dash we don't know
which one, but one of the two does.
\begin{center}
\vspace*{-4ex}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
read n
print 1
\end{lstlisting}%
\end{minipage}%
\hspace*{2em}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
read n
if n < n0:
    print 1
else:
    print 1
\end{lstlisting}%
\end{minipage}%
\vspace*{-2ex}
\end{center}
What's surprising is that the answer, whichever of the two it is,
does not to have much to do with $\pi$.
Also surprising, and perhaps unsettling, is that
we have shown that the problem is mechanically solvable
without giving any information as to how to solve it.
That is, 
\citetext{there is a difference between defining this function}{%
  This is a little like  
  Schr\"odinger's cat paradox
  (see \protect\url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schr\%C3\%B6dinger's_cat}) 
  in that it seems that one of the two is right 
  but we just don't know which.
  }
and giving an algorithm to compute it.
\begin{equation*}
  f(n)=
  \begin{cases}
    1 &\case{if there are $n$ consecutive $9$'s in $\pi$}  \\
    0 &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}

In contrast, consider a subroutine that inputs~$i\in\N$ and outputs 
\citetext{$\pi$'s $i$-th decimal place}{%
  As we have noted, some real numbers have two decimal
  representations, one ending in $0$'s and one ending in $9$'s.
  But every such number is rational (as ``ending in $0$'s'' implies)
  and $\pi$ is not rational, so $\pi$ is not one of these numbers.}.
With it, we can write a program that takes in~$n$ and
looks through~$\pi$ for~$n$ consecutive $9$'s by searching the 
digits.
This approach is constructive in that we are constructing the answer,
not just saying that it exists.
It is also uniform in the sense that we could 
modify it to take other subroutines as input
and thus look for strings of $9$'s in other numbers. 
However, this approach 
has the disadvantage that if $n_0$ is such that for $n\geq n_0$
never does $\pi$ have $n$ consecutive $9$'s then this program will
just search forever, without printing~$0$.
\end{example}
\index{uniformity|)}




\subsection{Parametrization}\index{parametrization|(}
Universality says that there is a Turing machine that 
takes in inputs $e$ and~$x$  and returns the same value as
we would get by running $\TM_e$ on input~$x$
(including not halting, if that machine does not halt).
That is, there is a computable
function $\map{\phi}{\N^2}{\N}$ such that
$\phi(e,x)=\TMfcn_e(x)$ if $\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$ and
$\phi(e,x)\diverges$ if $\TMfcn_e(x)\diverges$.

There, the letter~$e$ travels from the function's argument to an index.
We now generalize this observation.

Start with a program that takes two inputs
such as this one.
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (P x y)
  (+ x y))
\end{lstlisting}
Freeze the first argument, that is, lock $x=a$ for some $a\in\N$.
The result is a one-input program;~this shows~$a=7$.
\begin{lstlisting}
(define (P_7 b)
  (P 7 b))
\end{lstlisting}
This is \citetext{\definend{partial application}}{%
  See \autocite{wiki:PartialApplication}.}.

Obviously the second program is related to the first. 
Denoting the function computed by the starting program~\codeinline{P} 
as $\psi(x,y)=x+y$,
partial application gives a family of programs and functions:
$\psi_0(y)=y$, $\psi_1(y)=1+y$, $\psi_2(y)=2+y$, \ldots\sentencespace
The next result is that from the index of the starting program or function, and
from the values that are frozen, we can effectively compute the family
members.

%<*th:smn>
\begin{theorem}[\smn{} theorem, or Parameter theorem] \label{th:smn}
\index{Parameter theorem}\index{s-m-n theorem@$s$-$m$-$n$ theorem}
For every $m,n\in\N$
there is a computable total function $\map{s_{m,n}}{\N^{1+m}}{\N}$  
such that for the $m+n$-ary function
$\TMfcn_e(x_0,\ldots x_{m-1},x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1})$,
freezing the initial $m$~variables at $a_0,\ldots a_{m-1}\in\N$
gives an $n$-ary function equal to
$\TMfcn_{s(e,a_0,\ldots a_{m-1})}(x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1})$.
\end{theorem}
%</th:smn>
The function $\TMfcn_e(x_0,\ldots x_{m-1},x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1})$ could be
partial, that is, it could be that the Turing machine
$\TM_e$ fails to halt on some inputs $x_0,\ldots x_{m-1},x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1}$.

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:smni>
We will produce the function~$s$ to satisfy three 
requirements:~\citetext{it must be effective}{%
  In fact, careful analysis shows that it is primitive recursive.}, 
it must input an index~$e$ and an $m$-tuple $a_0,\ldots a_{m-1}$,
and it must output the index of a machine~$\hat{\TM}$ that,
when given the input $x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1}$,
will return the value $\TMfcn_e(a_0,\ldots a_{m-1},x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1})$, 
or diverge if that function diverges.

The idea is that the machine that computes~$s$
will construct the instructions for~$\hat{\TM}$.
We can get from the instruction set to the index
using Cantor's encoding, so with that we will be done.

Below on the left is
the flowchart for the machine that computes~$s$ 
and on the right is the flowchart for $\hat{\TM}$.
%</pf:smni>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts00.pdf}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts01.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*pf:smnii>
Recall that we are being flexible about
the convention for input and output representations for Turing machines
but to be precise
in this argument we assume that input
is encoded in unary, that
multiple inputs are separated with a single blank, and that
when the machine is started the head should be under the input's
left-most \str{1}.

With that, we construct the machine $\hat{\TM}$ so that the first thing it
does is not read its inputs $x_m,\ldots x_{m+n-1}$.
Instead, $\hat{\TM}$ first moves left and puts $a_0,\ldots a_{m-1}$ on the tape,
in unary and separated by blanks, and with a blank between $a_{m-1}$ 
and~$x_m$.
Then, using universality, $\hat{P}$ simulates
Turing machine~$P_e$, and lets it run on that input list.
%</pf:smnii>
\end{proof}

% That argument uses $a$'s for the values that the $x$~variables are frozen to.
% In the future we won't maintain that convention, as we will be thinking of the $x$'s as parameters and
% so sometimes varying them. 

In the notation $s_{m,n}$, 
the subscript~$m$ is the number of inputs being frozen
while~$n$ is the number of inputs left free.
As the prior example suggests, they can sometimes be a bother and 
we usually omit them.

\begin{example}
Consider the two-input routine sketched by this flowchart.
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1007.pdf}}
  \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine that fills in the sketch,
and computes the function $\psi(x,y)=x\cdot y$.
let that machine have index~$e$.
We can use the \smn{} theorem to freeze the value of~$x$ to~$0$.
On the left below is the flowchart sketching the machine $\TM_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}$.
It computes the function $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(y)=0$;
for example, $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(5)=0$.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1008.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1009.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1010.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}\ldots
\end{center}
Similarly the other two are flowcharts summarizing $\TM_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}$ 
and~$\TM_{s_{1,1}(e,2)}$, 
which freeze the value of $x$ at $1$ and~$2$.
The machine sketched in the center computes $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(y)=y$,
so for instance $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(5)=5$.
On the right the machine computes $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,2)}(y)=2y$,
and an example is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,2)}(5)=10$.

In general, this is the flowchart for $\TM_{s_{1,1}(e,x)}$.
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1011.pdf}}
  \tag{**}
\end{equation*}
Compare this to the flowchart in~($*$) above. 
The difference is that this machine does not read~$x$.  
Rather, as in the three charts above, 
$x$ is hard-coded into the program body.
That is, $\TM_{s_{1,1}(e,x)}$ is a family of Turing machines, 
the first three of which are in the prior paragraph.
This family is parametrized by~$x$, and the indices are
uniformily computable from $e$ and~$x$, using the function~$s$.
\end{example}

The \smn{} Theorem says that we can hard code the values of parameters into
the machine's source.
But it says more.
It also says that the resulting 
family of functions is uniformily computable;~there 
is a single computable function, $s$, going from 
the index~$e$ and the parameter value~$x$ to the index of the
result in~($**$).
So, the \smn{} Theorem is about uniformity.
\index{parametrization|)}




% ..............................................................
\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Started on\href{https://cs.stackexchange.com/questions/69197/difference-between-turing-machine-and-universal-turing-machine}{Stack Exchange}
\end{credit}
Your friend asks,
``What can a Universal Turing machine do that a regular Turing machine 
cannot?''
Help them out.
\begin{answer}\verified
  A Universal Turing machine is a regular machine, in that it is just another
  machine in the list $\TM_0$, $\TM_1$, \ldots\sentencespace
  Perhaps your friend meant something more like, ``What does a 
  Universal Turing machine
  do that a non-universal machine does not do?''
  Every machine does a job.
  Some machines expect a single input and then double it, 
  some interpret their input as two numbers and then add them.
  A Universal Turing machine expects two inputs, $e$ and~$x$,
  and the output of this machine is the same as the output
  of the machine $\TM_e$ on input~$x$,
  including failing to halt if that machine fails to halt.
  It is universal in that any input/output behavior that can be mechanically 
  produced can be produced by this one device.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Has anyone ever built a Universal Turing machine, 
or a machine equivalent to one?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Every general purpose computer, including any standard laptop or cell phone,
  is equivalent in computable power to a Universal Turing machine.

  A person can object that a standard laptop for instance
  does not com from the store with unboundedly much memory.
  That's true.
  But such a machine can store things in the Cloud and so is not restricted to
  the installed memory, and in this sense its memory is unbounded.

  A person can go on to worry that there are only so many bits available in the
  physical universe and thus memory is not in the end 
  unbounded.
  Perhaps that is true\Dash although we could worry back that we have not 
  got enough time
  to exhaust what bits that there are before the Big Bang becomes a Big Crunch,
  or whatever it will become\Dash 
  but at the least we can take
  general purpose devices to be so very close to 
  Universal Turing machines that it is quite hard to tell them apart.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Can a Universal Turing machine similate another Universal Turing machine,
or for that matter can it simulate itself?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Yes.
  If $\TM_{e_0}$ and $\TM_{e_1}$ are universal then giving the first one
  the inputs $e_1$ and~$x$ will result in an input/output behavior
  exactly like the second.
  Likewise, giving  $\TM_{e_0}$ the inputs $e_0$ and~$x$ will result in 
  the machine behaving just like itself. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  From a 
  \href{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/41854/67754}{Stack Exchange question}.
\end{credit}
Your class has a jerk who keeps throwing out pronouncements
that the prof must patiently correct.
This time its, 
``Universal Turing machines make no sense.
How could a machine simulate another that had a different alphabet?
And how could it simulate another machine that has more states?
Obviously it can't.''
Clue this chucklehead in.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The answer is that it does those things in software.
  A laptop uses the binary alphabet but simulates working with
  any language with any alphabet.
  For instance, it may simulate the symbol \str{a} with \str{111101}.
  A Universal Turing machine with alphabet 
  $\Sigma=\set{\str{B},\str{1}}$ can simulate a Turing machine with
  alphabet  $\hat{\Sigma}=\set{\str{B},\str{a},\str{b}}$ by having
  codes for each symbol.
  Likewise, the universal machine does not use its states to simulate the states
  of the simulated machine.
  Rather, it has codes for those states that it manipulates on the tape.

  Chucklehead.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is there more than one Universal Turing machine?
\begin{answer}\verified
Yes, every computable function has infinitely many 
indices by~\cref{lem:EveryCompFcnHasInfManyIndices}.
So if $\TM_e$ is universal, then the associated function~$\TMfcn_e$
has infinitely many unequal indices so that 
$\TMfcn_e=\TMfcn_{e_0}=\TMfcn_{e_1}=\cdots\,$.
The machines 
$\TM_{e_0}$, $\TM_{e_1}$, \ldots{} are all universal.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
What happens if we feed a Universal Turing machine to itself?
For instance, where the index~$e_0$ is such that 
$\TMfcn_{e_0}(e,x)=\TMfcn_e(x)$ for all~$x$,
what is the value of 
$\TMfcn_{e_0}(e_0,5)$?
\begin{answer}
We know that $5$ equals $\cantor(2,0)$, that is, $\pair(5)=\sequence{2,0}$.
So in $\TMfcn_{e_0}(e_0,5)$ the argument to $\TMfcn_{e_0}$ is the
value of $\TMfcn_2(0)$.
So, where $\pair(\TMfcn_2(0))=\sequence{a,b}$, the value of 
$\TMfcn_{e_0}(e_0,5)$ is $\TMfcn_a(b)$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider the function $f(x_0,x_1)=3x_0+x_0\cdot x_1$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Freeze $x_0$ to have the value~$4$.
  What is the resulting one-variable function?
\item Freeze $x_0$ at~$5$.
  What is the resulting one-variable function?
\item Freeeze $x_1$ to be~$0$.
  What is the resulting function?  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
We write $f_{i,a}$ for the function produced by freezing $x_i$
to the value~$a$.
\begin{exparts}
\item The resulting one-variable function is $f_{0,4}(x_1)=12+4x_1$. 
\item The one-variable function is $f_{0,5}(x_1)=15+5x_1$. 
\item The one-variable function is $f_{1,0}(x_0)=3x_0$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider
$f(x_0,x_1,x_2)=x_0+2x_1+3x_2$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Freeze $x_0$ to have the value~$1$.
  What is the resulting two-variable function?
\item What two-variable function results from fixing $x_0$ to be $2$?
\item Let $a$ be a natural number.
  What two-variable function results from fixing $x_0$ to be $a$?
\item Freeze $x_0$ at~$5$ and $x_1$ at~$3$.
  What is the resulting one-variable function?
\item What one-variable function results from fixing $x_0$ to be~$a$
  and $x_1$ to be~$b$, for $a,b\in\N$?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
We use $\hat{f}$ for the name of each function.
\begin{exparts}
\item The resulting two-variable function is 
  $\hat{f}(x_1,x_2)=1+2x_1+3x_2$.
\item The result is the two-variable function  
  $\hat{f}(x_1,x_2)=2+2x_1+3x_2$.
\item This gives 
  $\hat{f}(x_1,x_2)=a+2x_1+3x_2$.
\item This results in the one-variable function  
  $\hat{f}(x_2)=11+3x_2$.
\item The result is   
  $\hat{f}(x_2)=a+2b+3x_2$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Suppose that the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart has index~$e$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1000.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts}
\item Describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}$.
\item What are the values of $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(0)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(1)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(2)$?
\item Describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}$.
\item What are the values of $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(0)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(1)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,0}e,0}(2)$?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The two subscripts on $s_{1,1}$ mean that
  we will freeze the first input and leaves the second one as
  a variable.
  So $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}$ will be a one-input function.
  The arguments $e$ and~$1$ indicate that we are working on Turing machine~$e$,
  the one whose flow chart is given in the problem statement,
  and that the input is frozen to be $x_0=1$.
  This flowchart gives a sense of the result.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1001.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The function is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(x_1)=1+x_1$.
\item The values are $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(0)=1$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(1)=2$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(2)=3$.
\item The notation $s_{1,1}(e,0)$ indicates that we work on Turing machine~$e$,
  that we freeze the first input to $x_0=0$, and 
  that we leave the second input as
  a variable.
  This flowchart gives the idea.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1002.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The function is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(x_1)=0+x_1=x_1$.
\item We have $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(0)=0$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(1)=1$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(2)=2$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart have index~$e$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1003.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts}
\item Describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}$.
\item Find $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(0,1)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(1,0)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(2,3)$
\item Describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}$.
\item Find $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(0)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(1)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(2)$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The notation $s_{1,2}(e,1)$ indicates that
  we work on $\TM_e$, 
  that we freeze the first input to $x_0=1$, 
  and that we leave the second and third inputs as variables
  This flowchart sketches the behavior.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1004.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The function is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(x_1,x_2)=1+x_1\cdot x_2$.
\item We have $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(0,1)=1$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(1,0)=1$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(2,3)=7$.
\item The $s_{2,1}(e,1,2)$ says that we start with $\TM_e$,
  that we freeze the first input to $x_0=1$ and the second input to 
  $x_1=2$, 
  and leave the third input as a variable.
  This sketches the result.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1005.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The function is $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(x_2)=1+2\cdot x_2$.
\item The values are $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(0)=1$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(1)=3$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(2)=5$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Suppose that the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart has index~$e$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1006.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Describe $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}$.
\item What is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(5)$?
\item Describe $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}$.
\item What is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(5)$?
\item Describe $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,2)}$.
\item What is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,2)}(5)$?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(x_1)=x_1$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(5)=5$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(x_1)=x_1$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(5)=5$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,2)}(x_1)\diverges$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,2)}(5)\diverges$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Suppose that the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart has index~$e$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1012.pdf}
\end{center}
We will describe the family of functions parametrized by the arguments~$x_0$
and~$x_1$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Use \cref{th:smn}, the \smn{} theorem, to fix $x_0=0$ and~$x_1=3$.
  Describe $\TMfcn_{s(e,0,3)}$.
  What is  $\TMfcn_{s(e,0,3)}(5)$?
\item Use the \smn{} theorem to fix $x_0=1$.
  Describe $\TMfcn_{s(e,1,3)}$.
  What is  $\TMfcn_{s(e,1,3)}(5)$?
\item Describe $\TMfcn_{s(e,a,b)}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This flowchart sketches $\TM_{s(e,0,3)}$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1013.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The function is $\TMfcn_{s(e,0,3)}(y)=3y$.
  Consequently,  $\TMfcn_{s(e,0,3)}(5)=15$.
\item This is the flowchart sketching $\TM_{s(e,1,3)}$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1014.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The computed function is $\TMfcn_{s(e,1,3)}(y)=3+y$,
  and consequently  $\TMfcn_{s(e,1,3)}(5)=8$.
\item This is the flowchart sketching $\TM_{s(e,a,b)}$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1015.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The distinction between this and the flowchart in the question statement
  is that here the machine does not read in~$x_0$ or~$x_1$.  
  Those two are hard-coded, as $a$ and~$b$, 
  into the program body.
  This is a family of functions
  parametrized by~$a$ and~$b$, and the indices of these functions are
  uniformily computable from $e$, $a$, and~$b$, using the 
  \smn{} function~$s$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Argue that the function $\map{\psi}{\N^2}{\N}$ given by $\psi(x,y)=3x+y$
  is computable. 
\item Show that there 
  is a family of functions $\psi_n$ parameterized by~$n$ such
  that $\psi_n(y)=3n+y$. 
  \hint~take $e\in\N$ such that $\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$, and apply the
  \smn{} theorem. 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The function  $\psi(x,y)=3x+y$ is computable by Church's Thesis,
  as sketched in this flowchart. 
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1018.pdf}
  \end{center}
\item  By the prior item there is a Turing machine that 
  computes the function~$\psi$.
  Suppose that it is~$\TM_e$.
  The \smn{} theorem gives that 
  $\TM_{s_{1,1}(e,n)}$ computes the function $y\mapsto 3n+y$.
  Take $\psi_n=\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,n)}$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that there is a total computable function $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$
such that Turing machine $\TM_{g(n)}$ computes the function 
$y\mapsto y+n^2$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Start with the function $\map{\psi}{\N^2}{\N}$ defined by $\psi(x,y)=y+x^2$. 
  By Church's Thesis it is computable, since it is computed by the
  machine sketched by this flowchart.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1016.pdf}
  \end{center}
  Suppose that machine has index~$e$, that is, 
  suppose that $\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$.
  
  By the \smn{} theorem there is a family of computable functions
  parametrized by~$n$, $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(n)}$,
  with the desired behavior, $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(n)}(y)=y+n^2$.  
  Take $g(n)$ to be~$s_{1,1}(n)$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that there is a total computable function $\map{g}{\N^2}{\N}$
such that Turing machine $\TM_{g(m,b)}$ computes
$x\mapsto mx+b$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Start with the function $\psi(u,v,x)=ux+v$. 
  Church's Thesis gives that it is computable, by the
  machine sketched by this flowchart.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1017.pdf}
  \end{center}
  Let that machine have index~$e$, that is, 
  suppose that $\psi(u,v,x)=\TMfcn_e(u,v,x)$.

  The \smn{} theorem gives a family of computable functions
  $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(m,b)}$
  parametrized by~$m$ and~$b$
  with the desired behavior, $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(m,b)}(x)=m\cdot x+b$. 
  Take $g(m,b)$ to be $s_{2,1}(m,b)$. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Suppose that $e_0$ is such that $\TMfcn_{e_0}$ is a 
Universal Turing machine, in that if given the input $\cantor(e,x)$
then it returns the same value as $\TMfcn_e(x)$.
Suppose also that~$e_1$ is such that $\TMfcn_{e_1}(x)=4x$ for all~$x\in\N$.
Determine, if possible, the value of these.
If it is not possible, briefly describe why not.
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor(e_1,5))$
\item $\TMfcn_{e_1}(\cantor(e_0,5))$
\item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor(e_0,\cantor(e_1,5)))$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item 
  This gives the same result as $\TMfcn_{e_1}(5)$, that is, it converges
  and gives~$20$.
\item
  This returns $4$ times the the value of
  $\cantor(e_0,5)$.
\item This returns $\TMfcn_{e_1}(5)=20$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Suppose that $e_0$ is such that if $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor(e,x))$
returns the same value as $\TMfcn_e(x)$ (or does not converge if
that function does not converge).
Suppose also that $\TMfcn_{e_1}(x)=x+2$
and that $\TMfcn_{e_2}(x)=x^2$, for all~$x\in\N$.
If possible determine the value of these
(if it is not possible, say why not).
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor(e_1,4))$
\item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor(4,e_1))$
\item $\TMfcn_{e_1}(\cantor(e_0,\cantor(e_2,3)))$
\item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor{e_0,\cantor(e_0,4)})$
% \item $\TMfcn_{e_0}(\cantor{e_0,\cantor{e_2,e_1}})$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item 
  This gives the same result as $\TMfcn_{e_1}(4)$, that is, it gives~$6$.
\item
  This returns the value of $\phi_4(e_1)$, whataever that is.
\item This returns $\TMfcn_{e_2}(3)$ plus~$2$, which is~$11$.
\item Because $\pair(4)=\sequence{1,1}$, this returns the same as 
  $\TMfcn_1(1)$, whatever that is.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% We can write programs that process the source code of programs.
% \begin{exparts}
% \item
% Sketch a program that, when given the source code $\mathcal{S}$ of a
% program, will return the first character in that source (or some
% nominal value if the source is empty).
% \item 
% Sketch a program that, when given the source code $\mathcal{S}$ of a
% program, will return the final character in that source, or some nominal
% value.
% \item What happens when you feed the program in the prior part
% its own source?
% \end{exparts}
% \end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% We can write programs that process the source code of programs.
% \begin{exparts}
% \item
% Sketch a program that, when given the source code $\mathcal{S}$ of a program,
% will return the number of times the character \str{a} appears in that source.
% \item
% What happens when you feed your program's source to itself?
% \item
% Sketch two program that perform the same task as each other, that is, 
% their input-output
% behavior is the same, but the program in the first item gives different
% results for those two.
% \end{exparts}
% \end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% On \cpageref{page:UTM} is the definition of the Scheme routine \codeinline{utm}
% that acts as a Universal Turing machine.
% What happens if you give this machine its own source?
% \end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{universality|)}





%================================================
\section{Unsolvability}\index{unsolvability|(}\index{unsolvable problem|(}\index{problems!unsolvable|(} \label{sec:Unsolvability}
We've showed that
there are functions that are not mechanically computable.
We gave a counting argument, that there are countably many Turing machines but 
uncountably many functions and so there are functions with no
associated machine.
While knowing what's true is great,
even better is to exhibit a specific 
function that is unsolvable.
We will now do that.



\subsection{The \HP}\index{Halting problem|(}\index{problem!Halting|(}
The natural approach to producing such a function is to go through 
Cantor's Theorem and effectivize it,
to turn the proof into a construction.

Here is an illustrative table adapted from the discussion of
Cantor's Theorem on page~\pageref{ex:DiagonalizationTable}.\index{diagonalization!effectivized}
Imagine that this table's rows are the computable functions and 
its columns are the inputs.
For instance, this table lists $\phi_2(3)=5$.
\begin{center} \small \label{HPDiagonalizationTable}
  %<*table:CantorThmEffectivization>
  \setlength{\arraycolsep}{0.75\arraycolsep}
  \begin{tabular}{r|ccccccc@{\hspace*{1em}$\ldots$}}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ } 
    &\multicolumn{7}{c}{\text{\tabulartext{Input~$x$}}}  \\
     % \multicolumn{1}{c}{\text{\tabulartext{Function}}}
     \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
    &$0$ &$1$ &$2$ &$3$ &$4$ &$5$ &$6$ \\ 
   \cline{2-8}
   \rule{0em}{2.15ex}% bit more vertical room 
   $\phi_0$  &$3$  &$1$  &$2$  &$7$  &$7$  &$0$  &$4$  \\  
   $\phi_1$  &$0$  &$5$  &$0$  &$0$  &$0$  &$0$  &$0$  \\  
   $\phi_2$  &$1$  &$4$  &$1$  &$5$  &$9$  &$2$  &$6$  \\  
   $\phi_3$  &$9$  &$1$  &$9$  &$1$  &$9$  &$1$  &$9$  \\  
   $\phi_4$  &$1$  &$0$  &$1$  &$0$  &$0$  &$1$  &$0$  \\
   $\phi_5$  &$6$  &$2$  &$5$  &$5$  &$4$  &$1$  &$8$  \\
   \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\vdotswithin{0}$} 
    &\multicolumn{6}{c}{$\vdotswithin{0}$}  
  \end{tabular}
  %</table:CantorThmEffectivization>
  \hspace*{2em plus 0.75fil}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp02.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*discussion:HP>
Diagonalizing would mean considering the machine on the right.
However, applying it yields a puzzle, an apparent contradiction.
It appears that, in the usual diagonalization way, 
this machine's output does not equal any of the rows.
But the puzzle is that the flowchart outlines
an effective procedure;~we can write this 
by using a Universal Turing machine, so its output should be one of the
table's rows.

So what's the resolution?
The program's first, second, fourth, and fifth boxes are trivial, so the
issue must involve getting through the box in the middle.

The issue is halting\Dash among the computable functions 
there are ones that do not converge on some inputs.  
In particular, the apparent contradiction shows that there must be
an $e\in\N$ so that $\phi_e(e)\diverges$.
With that insight the difficulty dissolves, because taking such 
a number~$e$
as input in the flowchart means that the machine will never leave 
the middle box, and so will never get to the output box and its contradiction.

So, 
to avoid a contradiction the above table must contain $\uparrow$'s.
This brings out that the fact that some computations fail to halt on 
some inputs is central to the nature of computation.
%</discussion:HP>

%<*def:K>
\begin{definition}\index{K@$K$, the Halting problem set}
  $\definend{K}=\set{e\in\N \suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$, that is, Turing machine $\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$}}$
\end{definition}
%</def:K>

%<*prob:HP>
\begin{problem}[Halting problem]\index{Halting problem}\index{problem!Halting}  \label{prob:HaltingProblem}
Given~$e\in\N$, determine whether $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$, that is,
whether Turing machine~$\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$.
\end{problem}
%</prob:HP>

For any particular $e\in\N$, obviously either $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$ or
$\TMfcn_e(e)\diverges$.
Thus the function defined by 
$K(e)=1$ if $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$ and $K(e)=0$ otherwise
is well-defined.
So the \HP{} is not about whether the function exists.
Instead, we want to determine the values of that function;
we want to compute the characteristic function of~$K$.

%<*th:HPIsUnsolvable>
\begin{theorem}[Unsolvability of the \HP]\index{Halting problem!unsolvability}
The \HP{} is mechanically unsolvable.
\end{theorem}
%</th:HPIsUnsolvable>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:HPIsUnsolvablei>
Assume otherwise, that there is a Turing machine whose behavior is this.
\begin{equation*}
  K(e)=
  \computablefunction{halt\_decider}(e)=
  \begin{cases}
  1  &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$}  \\
  0  &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\diverges$} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Then the function below is also mechanically computable.
The flowchart illustrates how $f$ is constructed;
it uses the above function in its decision box.
%</pf:HPIsUnsolvablei>
\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.3\textwidth}
  \begin{equation*}
  f(e)=
    \begin{cases}
    42    &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\diverges$}  \\
    \uparrow  &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
\end{minipage}
\hspace{2.5em}
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts02.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*pf:HPIsUnsolvableii>
(\citetext{In $f$'s top case the output value doesn't matter}{%
  Sometimes we use $42$ when we need an arbitrary output value,
  because of its connection with 
  \protect\textit{The Hitchhiker's Guide to the Galaxy},
  \autocite{Adams}.
  See also \autocite{wiki:foobar}.},
all that matters is that~$f$ converges.)
Since this function is mechanically computable it has an index.
Let that index be~$\hat{e}$, so that
$f(x)=\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(x)$ for all inputs~$x$.

Consider $f(\hat{e})=\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(\hat{e})$,
that is, feed the machine the input~$\hat{e}$.
If it diverges then the first clause in the definition of~$f$ means that
$f(\hat{e})\converges$,
which contradicts divergence.
If it converges then $f$'s second clause 
means that $f(\hat{e})\diverges$, also impossible.
Since assuming that $\computablefunction{halt\_decider}$ is 
mechanically computable leads to a contradiction,
it is not mechanically computable.
%</pf:HPIsUnsolvableii>
\end{proof}

With Church's Thesis in mind will say that a problem is 
\definend{unsolvable}\index{unsolvable problem}\index{problem!unsolvable} 
if it is mechanically unsolvable, if no Turing machine 
computes that task.
If the problem is to compute the answer to `yes' or `no' questions,
so it is the problem of determining membership in a set,
then we will say that the set is 
\citetext{\definend{undecidable}}{%
  The word `undecidable' is used in mathematics in two different ways.
  The definition here of course applies to the Theory of Computation.
  In relation to G\"{o}del's theorems, it means that a statement is 
  cannot be proved true or proved false within a particular formal system.}.\index{undecidable}\index{set!undecidable}\index{Halting problem|)}\index{problem!Halting|)}



\subsection{Discussion}\index{Halting problem!discussion|(}
The fact that the Halting Problem is unsolvable does not mean that 
we cannot tell if any program halts. 
This program obviously adds~$1$ to its input and then halts  for every input.
\begin{lstlisting}
#;1> (define (prompt/read prompt)
--->     (display prompt)
--->     (read-line))
#;2> 
#;2> (+ 1
--->    (string->number (prompt/read "Enter n")))
Enter n---> 4
5  
\end{lstlisting}
Nor does the unsolvability of the \HP{} mean that we cannot tell if a program 
does not halt.
Consider this one.
\begin{lstlisting}
#;1> (define (f x)
--->   (+ 1 (f x)))
\end{lstlisting}
This obviously does not halt;~once started, it just keeps going.
\begin{lstlisting}
#;2> (f 0)
^C
	Call history:

	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	readline.scm:392: print-call-chain	  	<--

*** user interrupt ***
\end{lstlisting}
\noindent Instead, the unsolvability of the Halting Problem 
says that there is no single program that, for all~$e$, correctly decides 
in a finite time whether $\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$.

That has the qualifier `finite time' 
because we could perfectly well write source code to 
read an input~$e$, simulate $\TM_e$ on input~$e$, 
and then print some nominal output such as~$42$,
but if $\TM_e$ on input~$e$ fails to halt then we would not get the output 
in a finite time.

The `single program' qualifier is there because for any index~$e$,
either $\TM_e$ halts on~$e$ or else it does not.
That is, for any~$e$ one of these two programs
gives the right answer.
\begin{center}
% \vspace*{-4ex}
\vspace*{-\ht\strutbox}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
read e
print 0
\end{lstlisting}%
\end{minipage}%
\hspace*{2em plus 0.5fil}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
read e
print 1
\end{lstlisting}%
\end{minipage}%
% \vspace*{-2ex}
\end{center}
Of course, guessing which one applies is not what we had in mind
in solving the \HP{}.
We had in mind a program, an effective procedure, that inputs~$e$
and outputs the right answer.

Thus, the unsolvability of the Halting Problem is about 
the non-existence of a single program that works across all
indices.
It speaks to uniformity,
or rather, the impossibility of uniformity.
\index{Halting problem!discussion|)}



\subsection{Significance}\index{Halting problem!significance|(}  \label{subsec:Significance}
A beginning programming class could leave the 
impression that if a program doesn't halt then
it just has a bug, something fixable.
So the \HP{} could seem to be not very interesting. 
That impression is wrong.

Imagine 
a utility for programmers, $\computablefunction{always\_halt}$,
to patch non-halting programs.
After writing a program~$\TM$ we run it through the utility, which
modifies the source so that for any input where $\TM$ fails to halt,
the modified program will halt (and output $0$), but the utility
does not change any outputs where $\TM$ does halt.
That would give rise to a 
list of total functions like the one on \pageref{HPDiagonalizationTable},
and diagonalization gives a contradiction.

Thus, halting, or rather failure to halt, is central
to the nature of computation.
In any general computational scheme
there must be some computations that halt on all inputs, 
some that halt on no inputs, 
and some that \citetext{halt on some inputs but not on others}{A 
  Turing machine could fail to halt because it has an infinite loop.
  The Turing machine
  $\TM_0=\set{\tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{0},
    \tminstruction{0}{1}{1}{0}}$
  never halts, cycling forever in state $q_0$.
  We could patch this problem; we could write a program
  $\computablefunction{inf\_loop\_decider}$ that at each
  step checks whether a machine has ever before in this computation
  had the same configuration as it has now.
  This program will detect infinite loops like the prior one.
  
  However, note that there are machines that fail to halt but
  do not have loops, in the sense that they never repeat a configuration.
  One is $\TM_1=\set{\tminstruction{0}{B}{1}{1},
        \tminstruction{1}{1}{R}{0}}$
  which when started on a blank tape will endlessly move to the right,
  writing \str{1}'s.}.

That alone is enough to justify study of the \HP{} but we will give a 
second reason.
If $\computablefunction{halt\_decider}$ were a computable function
then we could solve many problems that we currently don't know how to solve. 

\margingraphicnomag*[l]{.425\textwidth}{background/asy/hp/hp00.pdf}
For instance, a natural number is \textit{perfect}\index{perfect number} 
if it is the 
sum of its proper positive divisors.
Thus $6$ is perfect because $6=1+2+3$.
\citetext{Similarly, $28 = 1 + 2 + 4 + 7 + 14$ is perfect}{%
  After $6$ and $28$ comes $496$ and~$8128$.
  No one knows if there are infinitely many.}.
These have been studied since Euclid and
today we 
\citetext{understand the form of all even perfect numbers}{%
  A number is an even perfect number if and only if it has the form 
  $(2^p-1)\cdot 2^{p-1}$ where $2^p-1$ is prime.}.
But no one knows if there are any odd perfect numbers.

With a solution to the Halting Problem we could settle this question.
The
program shown here searches for an odd perfect number.\footnote{%
  It takes an
  input $x$ but ignores it; in this book we have the habit of having
  all of the machines that we use to take an input and give an output.}
If it finds one then it halts.
If not then it does not halt.
So if we had a $\computablefunction{halt\_decider}$ and we gave it 
the index of this program, then that
would settle whether there exists any odd perfect numbers.
There are many open questions 
\citetext{involving an unbounded search}{%
  A computer program that solved 
  the Halting Problem, if one existed, could be very slow. 
  So this might not be a feasible way to settle this question.  
  But at the moment we are studying what can be done in principle.} 
that would fall to this approach.
(Just to name one more:~no one knows if there is any $n>4$ such that
$2^{(2^n)}+1$ is prime.
We could answer the question by writing $\TM$ to search for such an~$n$,
 and give the index of $\TM$ to 
$\computablefunction{halt\_decider}$.)
 
Before moving on, note the appearance here of the theme of unbounded search.
It does not just appear in looking for perfect numbers;
we have seen unbounded search before, in defining $\mu$-recursion.
And it is at the heart of the \HP{} since
the obvious way to test whether $\phi_e(e)\converges$
is to run a brute force computation, an unbounded
search for a stage at which the computation halts.    
\index{Halting problem!significance|)}







%================================================
\subsection{General unsolvabilty}\index{unsolvability!general|(}
The \HP{} is part of a larger unsolvability phenomenon.
% We will consider a number of problems and for each
% show that if we could solve it then we
% could solve the \HP, and consequently 
% the given problem is also unsolvable.

\begin{example} \label{ex:HaltOnThree}
Consider the problem of determining whether a given Turing machine halts 
on the input~$3$.
That is, given~$x$, does $\TMfcn_x(3)\converges$?
\begin{equation*}
  \computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(3)\converges$}  \\
    0 &\case{otherwise} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
We will show 
that if $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}$ 
were a computable function then we could compute the solution of the \HP.
That's impossible, so we will then know that 
$\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}$ is also not effectively
computable.
% \margingraphicnomag*[r]{.3\textwidth}{background/asy/hp/hp06.pdf}

The plan is to create a scheme where being able to determine
whether a machine halts on~$3$ allows us to settle \HP{} questions.
Consider the machine sketched on the right below.
It reads the input~$y$, ignores it, and gives a nominal output.
The action is in the middle box, where it 
simulates running $\TM_x$ on input~$x$.
If that simulation halts then the machine as a whole halts.
If that simulation does not halt then this simulation as a whole
does not halt.
Thus, the machine on the right halts on input~$y=3$ if and only if 
$\TM_x$ halts on~$x$.
So, using this flowchart, we can leverage being able
to answer questions about
halting on~$3$ to answer
questions about whether $\TM_x$ halts on~$x$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp06.pdf}
  \hspace*{4em}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp07.pdf}
\end{center}

With that motivation we are ready for the argument.
For contradiction, assume that 
$\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}$ is mechanically
computable.
Consider this function.
\begin{equation*}
  \psi(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    42 &\case{if $\phi_x(x)\converges$} \\
    \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Observe that~$\psi$ is mechanically computable, because it
is computed by the flowchart above on the left.
So by Church's Thesis
there is a Turing machine whose input-output behavior is $\psi$.
That Turing machine has some index,~$e$, meaning that $\psi=\phi_e$.

Use the \smn{} theorem to parametrize~$x$, giving $\phi_{s(e,x)}$.
This is a family of functions, one for~$x=0$, one for~$x=1$, etc.\sentencespace
Below is the family of associated machines.
Notice in particular the one on the right.
It is a repeat of the one on the right above.
Notice also that it has a `Read~$y$' but no `Read~$x$'. 
For each of these machines, 
the value used in the middle box is hard-coded into its source.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp15.pdf}}
  \hspace*{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp16.pdf}}
  \hspace*{2em}\ldots{}\hspace*{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp07.pdf}}
  \hspace*{2em}\ldots{}
\end{center}

As planned, for all $x\in\N$ we have this.
\begin{equation*}
 \phi_x(x)\converges 
  \quad\text{if and only if}\quad
 \computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}(\,s(e,x)\,)=1
  \tag{*}
\end{equation*}
The function~$s$ is computable so 
the supposition that $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}$ is 
also computable gives that the right side is effectively computable,
which in turn implies that the \HP{} is effectively solvable, 
which it isn't.
This contradiction means that $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}$
is not mechanically computable.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
We emphasize that $s(e,x)$ gives a 
family of infinitely many machines and computable functions
to make the point that while $e$ is constant (it is the index of the 
machine that computes~$\psi$), $x$ varies.
We need that for~($*$).
\end{remark}

\begin{example} \label{ex:OutputsSeven}
We will show that this function is not mechanically computable: given~$x$,
determine whether $\TM_x$ outputs a~$7$ for any input.
\begin{equation*}
  \computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(y)=7$ for some~$y$}  \\
    0 &\case{otherwise} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Assume otherwise, that $\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}$ 
is computable. 
Consider this.
\begin{equation*}
  \psi(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    7 &\case{if $\phi_x(x)\converges$} \\
    \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
The flowchart on the left below outlines how to compute~$\psi$.
Because it is intuitively mechanically computable, Church's Thesis says that
there is a Turing machine whose input-output behavior is $\psi$.
That Turing machine has an index,~$e$, so that~$\psi=\phi_e$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp08.pdf}
  \hspace*{2em plus 0.5fil}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp09.pdf}
\end{center}
The \smn{} theorem gives 
a family of functions $\phi_{s(e,x)}$ parametrized by $x$.
On the right is a flowchart for an associated machine. 
As in the prior example, note that this is a family of infinitely many
different machines, one with $x=0$, one with $x=1$, etc.\sentencespace 
Each machine in the family has its~$x$ hard-coded in its source.

Then, $\phi_x(x)\converges$ if and only if
$\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}(s(e,x))=1$.
If, as we supposed, $\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}$ is 
computable then the composition of two computable functions
$\composed{\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}}{s}$
is computable, so the \HP{} is computably solvable, 
which is not right.
Therefore $\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}$ is not computable.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:Doubles}
We next show that this problem is unsolvable:~given $x$,
determine whether $\TMfcn_x$
doubles its input, that is, whether $\TMfcn_x(y)=2y$ for all~$y$. 

We want to show that this function is not mechanically computable.
\begin{equation*}
  \computablefunction{doubler\_decider}(e)=
  \begin{cases}
    1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(y)=2y$ for all~$y$}  \\
    0 &\case{otherwise} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Assume that it is computable. 
This function
\begin{equation*}
  \psi(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    2y &\case{if $\phi_x(x)\converges$} \\
    \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
is intutitively mechanically computable by the flowchart on the left below.
So by Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine that computes it.
It has some index,~$e$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp10.pdf}
  \hspace*{2em plus 0.5fil}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp11.pdf}
\end{center}
Apply the \smn{} theorem to get 
a family of functions $\phi_{s(e,x)}$ parametrized by $x$. 
The machine $\TM_{s(e,x)}$ is sketched by the flowchart on the right.
Then $\phi_x(x)\converges$ if and only if
$\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_decider}(s(e,x))=1$.
So the supposition that $\computablefunction{doubler\_decider}$ is 
computable gives that the \HP{} is computable, 
which is wrong.
\end{example}

These examples show that one of the roles that the \HP{}
plays is that it is a touchstone for demonstrating algorithmic unsolvability.
Often we show that something is unsolvable by showing that
if we could solve it then we could solve
the \HP{}.
We say that the given problem was 
\definend{reduced to the \HP{}}.\index{reduced!to the Halting problem}\index{Halting problem!reduction to}

Before the next subsection, two things.
First,  to reiterate, when we say that a problem is
unsolvable we mean that it is unsolvable
by a mechanism, that there  
is no Turing machine that can compute the solution to the problem.
There can be a \citetext{function that solves it}{%
  \autocite{wiki:ouija}}
but no computable function that solves it.

The other thing is some intuition.
There certainly are problems that start with ``Given an index~$x$'' 
that we can solve.
Just to list one: given~$e$ decide if one instruction in $\TM_e$ is 
$\tminstruction{0}{B}{L}{1}$.
But that problem has a different character than the ones above.
The problems above are about the behavior of the computed function\Dash
each is about $\phi_e$ rather than~$\TM_e$.
This echos the opening of the first chapter,
which says that we are more
interested in the input-output behavior of the machines than in their
implementation details. 
The next subsection expands. 
\index{unsolvability!general|)}



\subsection{Rice's Theorem}\index{Rice's theorem|(}
The intuition from the unsolvability examples is that
we cannot mechanically analyze the behavior of a mechanism.
