These two definitions make precise the word `behavior'.

%<*def:SameBehavior>
\begin{definition}
Two computable functions
\definend{have the same behavior $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$}\index{same behavior, functions with}\index{functions!same behavior}\index{behavior, functions}
if they converge on the same inputs~$x\in\N$
and when they do converge, they have the same outputs.\footnotemark[1] 
\end{definition}\footnotetext[1]{%
  Strictly speaking we don't need the symbol~$\samebehavior$. 
  A function is a set of ordered pairs, 
  So if 
  $\TMfcn_e(0)\converges$ while~$\TMfcn_e(1)\diverges$, then the set $\TMfcn_e$
  contains a pair starting with~$0$ but no pair with first entry~$1$.
  Thus for partial functions, if they converge on the same inputs
  and when they do converge they have the same outputs, then we can simply say 
  that the two are equal, $\TMfcn=\hat{\TMfcn}$, as sets.
  We use $\samebehavior$ as a reminder that the
  functions may be partial.}
%</def:SameBehavior>

%<*def:IndexSet>
\begin{definition}
A set $\mathcal{I}$ of natural numbers is an 
\definend{index set}\footnotemark[2]\index{index set}\index{set!index} 
when for all 
indices~$e,\hat{e}\in\N$,
if $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ 
then also~$\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$. 
\end{definition}\footnotetext[2]{%
  It is called an index set because it is a set of indices.}
%</def:IndexSet>

\begin{example}
The set 
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\phi_e(x)=2x$ for all $x$}}$
is an index set. 
Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that 
$\phi_e\samebehavior \phi_{\hat{e}}$. 
Then the behavior of $\phi_{\hat{e}}$ is also to double its input: 
$\phi_{\hat{e}}(x)=2x$ for all~$x$.
Thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ also.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
The set
$\mathcal{J}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat
      \text{$\phi_e(x)=3x$ for all $x$, or $\phi_e(x)=x^3$ for all~$x$}}
$
is an index set.
For, suppose that $e\in\mathcal{J}$ and that  
$\phi_e\samebehavior \phi_{\hat{e}}$  where $\hat{e}\in\N$.
Because $e\in\mathcal{J}$, either 
$\phi_e(x)=3x$ for all $x$ or $\phi_e(x)=x^3$ for all~$x$.
Because $\phi_e\samebehavior \phi_{\hat{e}}$ we know that
either $\phi_e(x)=3x$ for all $x$ or $\phi_e(x)=x^3$ for all~$x$, and  
thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{J}$.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:NotIndexSet}
The set $\set{e\in\N\suchthat
      \text{$\TM_e$ contains an instruction starting with $q_{10}$}}$ 
is not an index set.
We can easily produce two Turing machines having the same behavior
where one machine contains such an instruction while the other does not.
\end{example}

%<*th:RicesTheorem>
\begin{theorem}[Rice's theorem]\index{Rice's theorem}   \label{thm:RicesThm}
Every index set that is not trivial, that is not empty and not 
all of $\N$, is not mechanically computable.
\end{theorem}
%</th:RicesTheorem>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:RicesTheoremi>
Let $\mathcal{I}$ be an index set.
Choose an~$e_0\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_{e_0}(y)\diverges$ for all~$y$.
Then either $e_0\in\mathcal{I}$ or $e_0\notin\mathcal{I}$.
We shall show that in the second case $\mathcal{I}$ is not computable. 
The first case is similar and is \cref{exer:OtherHalfRicesThm}.

So assume $e_0\notin\mathcal{I}$.
Since $\mathcal{I}$ is not empty there is an index~$e_1\in\mathcal{I}$.
Because $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, $\TMfcn_{e_0}\not\simeq\TMfcn_{e_1}$.
Thus there is an input~$y$ such that $\TMfcn_{e_1}(y)\converges$.

Consider the flowchart on the left below.
Note that $e_1$ is not an input, it is hard-coded into the source.
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior,
let it be~$\TM_e$.
Apply the \smn{} theorem to paramatrize~$x$,
resulting in the uniformily computable 
family of functions $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$, whose computation is 
outlined on the right.
%</pf:RicesTheoremi>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp12.pdf}}
  \hspace{4em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp13.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*pf:RicesTheoremii>
We've constructed the machine sketched on the right so that if 
$\TMfcn_x(x)\diverges$ then $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{e_0}$
and thus $s(e,x)\notin\mathcal{I}$. 
Further,
if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$ then $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{e_1}$
and thus $s(e,x)\in\mathcal{I}$.
Therefore if $\mathcal{I}$ were mechanically computable, so that
we could effectively check whether $s(e,x)\in\mathcal{I}$,
then we could solve the \HP.
%</pf:RicesTheoremii>
\end{proof}


\begin{example}
We use Rice's Theorem to show that this problem is unsolvable: 
given $e$, decide if $\TMfcn_e(3)\converges$.    

Consider the set
$
  \mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N \suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}
$.
To apply Rice's Theorem we must show that this set is not empty, that 
it is not all of~$\N$, and that it is an index set.
The set~$\mathcal{I}$ is not empty because we can write a Turing machine that 
acts as the identity function $\TMfcn(x)=x$, and if $e_0$ is the index of that 
Turing Machine then $e_0\in\mathcal{I}$.
The set~$\mathcal{I}$ is not equal to $\N$ because, 
where $e_1$ is the index of a Turing machine that never halts, 
we have that $e_1\notin\mathcal{I}$.

To finish we will verify that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set. 
Assume that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and 
let $\hat{e}\in\N$ be such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
Then $e\in\mathcal{I}$ gives that  $\TMfcn_e(3)\converges$ and 
$\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ gives that
$\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(3)\converges$ also.
Hence $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$, and $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.  
\end{example}

\begin{example}
We can use Rice's Theorem to show that this problem is unsolvable:
given $e$, decide if $\phi_e(x)=7$ for some~$x$.    

We will show that
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N \suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(x)=7$ for some $x$}}$
is a nontrivial index set.
This set is not empty because, where $e_0$ is the index of a 
Turing Machine that acts as the identity function $\TMfcn_{e_0}(x)=x$,
we have that $e_0\in\mathcal{I}$.
It is not all of $\N$ because, 
where $e_1$ is the index of a Turing Machine that never halts, 
$e_i\notin\mathcal{I}$.
So~$\mathcal{I}$ is nontrivial.

To showing that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set
assume that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and let $\hat{e}\in\N$ be such that
$\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
By the first assumption, $\TMfcn_e(x_0)=7$ for some input~$x_0$.
By the second, the same input gives
$\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(x_0)=7$. 
Consequently, $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.
\end{example}

We close by reflecting on the significance of Rice's Theorem.
This result addresses the properties of computable functions.
It does not speak to the properties of machines
that aren't about the input-output behavior;~for example,~the set of functions
computed by C~programs whose first character
is `k' would not be an index set. 
Again, this ties back to the declaration
in the first paragraph of the first chapter of this book
that we are more interested in what the machines do than in their
internal construction.

At the start of this chapter we saw by a counting argument that 
unsolvable problems exist.
Later the \HP{} showed us that there are some interesting
problems that are unsolvable, some problems
that are significant for the kinds of things
we want to compute in practice.
Here we saw the definition of index set, 
which is the natural way to encapsulate a behavior of interest,
and Rice's Theorem says that for every nontrivial index set,
computing the characteristic function is  unsolvable.
In this sense, 
Rice's Theorem says that \emph{every} interesting problem
is unsolvable.

Of course, that's an overstatement; we've all seen
and written real-world programs with interesting behaviors.
Nonetheless, Rice's Theorem is especially significant
for an understanding what can be done mechanically.
\index{Rice's theorem|)}  



% ...................................................
\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
True of false: there is no function that solves the Halting Problem;
there is no $f$ such that $f(e)=1$ if $\phi_e(e)\converges$ and $f(e)=0$ if
$\phi_e(e)\diverges$.
\begin{answer}\verified
False.  
There is such a function.  
But it is not computable; there is no Turing machine whose behavior is
that function.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Your study partner asks you, ``The Turing machine 
$\TM=\set{\tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{0},\tminstruction{0}{1}{1}{0}}$
fails to halt for all inputs, that's obvious.
But these unsolvability results say that I cannot know that.
Why not?''
Explain what they are missing.
\begin{answer}
The unsolvability results do not say that you cannot prove that a
particular machine does a particular thing.
The given machine does indeed fail to halt on all inputs and it
is indeed obvious.
Instead the results say that no machine can take in the index~$x$ and
correctly determine whether $\TM_x$ does that thing.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
True or false?
\begin{exparts}
\item
The problem of determining, given~$e$, whether $\TMfcn_e(3)\converges$ is
unsolvable because no function
$\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_decider}$
exists.
\item
The existence of unsolvable problems indicates weaknesses in the
models of computation, and we need stronger models.
% \item
% Rice's Theorem says that every nontrivial problem is unsolvable.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
False.
The function exists.
We say that the problem is unsolvable because that function is not
mechanically computable.
\item
False.
Church's Thesis asserts that Turing machines are a maximally-powerful
model of computation.
The existence of unsolvable problems is inherent in effective computation.
% \item
% False.
% Rice's Theorem says that every nontrivial problem of a certain kind
% (about index sets) is unsolvable.
% There are plenty of solvable problems.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set is computable if its characteristic function
is a computable function.
Consider the set consisting of~$1$ if Mallory reached the summit of Everest,
and otherwise consisting of~$0$. 
Is that set computable?
\begin{answer}
  Any finite set is computable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Your friend says, ``According to Rice's Theorem, everything is impossible. 
Every property of a computer program is non-computable.
But I do this supposedly impossible stuff all the time!''
Set them straight.
\begin{credit}
  Question by SE user MathematicalOrchid,
  \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/2811/67754}, 
  and answer by SE user Andrej Bauer.
\end{credit}
\begin{answer}
Briefly, we have to distinguish between a 
machine and the function that it computes. 
Many different machines compute the same function. 
Rice's theorem is about those properties of the machine that extend to be 
properties of the computed functions.
For instance, ``Does this code compute the squaring function?'' is 
unsolvable, but ``Does this code contain the letter K?'' is not. 

A more sophisticated answer is that
two machines `extensionally equal' if they compute the 
same function, so that for every input either both 
machines run forever on that input, 
or they both terminate and output the same value.
An `extensional property' of programs is a property that respects 
extensional equality, i.e., 
if two machines are extensionally equal then they either both have 
the property or both not have it.
Then Rice's Theorem says that a computable extensional property of machines 
either holds of all machines or of none.

Here are some non-extensional properties,
to which Rice's Theorem does not apply:
(i)~the machine halts within $100$ steps (we can always modify a machine 
to an extensionally equal one that runs longer),
(ii)~the machine uses fewer than~$n$ memory cells within the first~$m$ 
steps of execution (we can always modify a machine to an extensionally 
equal one so that it uses extra memory), and
(iii)~the machine source contains state~$q_{10}$ (we can add extra states).
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is $K$ infinite?
\begin{answer}
Yes, any finite set is computable.
You could, for example, compute membership in a finite set with a sequence
of if-then-else statements.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Describe the family of computable functions
that you get by using the \smn{} Theorem 
to parametrize~$x$ in each function.
Also give flowcharts sketching the associated machines
for $x=0$, $x=1$, and $x=2$. 
\begin{exparts*}
\item $f(x,y)=3x+y$
\item $f(x,y)=xy^2$
\item 
  $f(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    x  &\case{if $x$ is odd} \\
    0  &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The function $f(x,y)=3x+y$ is intuitively computable, as sketched
  on the left of
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsThreeXPlusY}.
  Church's Thesis gives that there is a Turing machine with
  that behavior; let that machine have index~$e$, so that
  $f(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$ for all $x,y\in\N$. 
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1008.pdf}}
    \hspace*{3em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1009.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1010.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1011.pdf}}
    \caption{The machine associated with the function $f=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the machines associated with $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$ for $x=0$, $x=1$, and $x=2$.}
    \label{fig:FamilyFcnsThreeXPlusY}
  \end{figure}
  Applying the \smn{} Theorem to parametrize~$x$ gives this computable family of
  one-input computable functions:
  $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=3x+y$.
  Flowcharts sketching some associated machines are on the right side of
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsThreeXPlusY}.  
\item The function $f(x,y)=xy^2$ is intuitively computable, as in
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsXYSquared}.
  Church's Thesis gives that there is a Turing machine~$\TM_e$ with
  that behavior, so that
  $f(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$ for all $x,y\in\N$. 
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1012.pdf}}
    \hspace*{3em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1013.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1014.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1015.pdf}}
    \caption{The machine associated with the function $f=\TMfcn_{e}$ and machines associated with $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$ for $x=0$, $x=1$, and $x=2$.}
    \label{fig:FamilyFcnsXYSquared}
  \end{figure}
  Applying the \smn{} Theorem to parametrize~$x$ gives
  $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=xy^2$, which is a family of one-input functions.
  Flowcharts sketching some associated machines are on the right side of
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsXYSquared}.  
\item The function $f$ is intuitively computable, as sketched on the left of
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsChoiceOnX}.
  Church's Thesis gives that there is a Turing machine~$\TM_e$ with
  that behavior, so that
  $f(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$ for all $x,y\in\N$. 
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1016.pdf}}
    \hspace*{3em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1017.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1018.pdf}}
    \quad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1019.pdf}}
    \caption{A sketch of the machine for the function $f=\TMfcn_{e}$ and of the machines associated with $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$ when $x=0$, $x=1$, and $x=2$.}
    \label{fig:FamilyFcnsChoiceOnX}
  \end{figure}
  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to parametrize~$x$.
  The resulting family of one-input functions is this.
  \begin{equation*}
    \TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=
      \begin{cases}
         x  &\case{if $x$ is odd}  \\
         0  &\case{otherwise}
      \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  Flowcharts sketching associated machines are on the right side of
  \figureref{fig:FamilyFcnsChoiceOnX}.  
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that this is an unsolvable problem by reducing it to the \HP:~given~$x$, 
determine if $\TMfcn_x$ is total, that is,
if it converges on every input.
\begin{answer}\verified
  We will show that this function is not mechanically computable.
  \begin{equation*}
    \computablefunction{total\_decider}(e)=
    \begin{cases}
      1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(y)\converges$ for all~$y$}  \\
      0 &\case{otherwise} 
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  The function
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
      \uparrow   &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  is intuitively computable by the flowchart on the left of 
  \figureref{fig:DetermineTotalNotComputable}.
  Thus Church's Thesis says that there is a
  Turing machine that computes it;
  let that machine have index~$e$, so that $\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1004.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1005.pdf}}
    \caption{The machine associated with the function $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:DetermineTotalNotComputable}
  \end{figure}
  The \smn{}~Theorem gives a family of functions 
  parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
  \figureref{fig:DetermineTotalNotComputable}.
  Notice that 
  $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
  if and only if 
  $\computablefunction{total\_decider}(s(e,x))=1$.

  Since~$s$ is computable, if 
  we can mechanically compute $\computablefunction{total\_decider}$
  then we can mechanically solve the \HP.
  We cannot mechanically solve the \HP, so we cannot mechanically
  compute  $\computablefunction{total\_decider}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:ShowUnsolvableHP}
Show that each of these is an unsolvable problem by reducing it to the \HP.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given an index~$x$, decide if the Turing machine~$\TM_x$ squares its
  input.  
  That is, decide if $\TMfcn_x$ maps $y\mapsto y^2$.
\item Given~$x$, determine if the function~$\TMfcn_x$ returns the same value
  on two consecutive inputs, so that $\phi_x(y)=\phi_x(y+1)$ for some~$y\in\N$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item 
  We will show that this function is not mechanically computable.
  \begin{equation*}
    \computablefunction{square\_decider}(e)=
    \begin{cases}
      1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(y)=y^2$ for all~$y$}  \\
      0 &\case{otherwise} 
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}

  Consider this function.
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      y^2        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
      \uparrow   &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  The flowchart on the left of 
  \figureref{fig:DetermineSquaringFunctionNotComputable} shows that
  $\psi$ is intuitively computable so by Church's Thesis there is a
  Turing machine that computes it.
  Let that machine have index~$e$.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1000.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1001.pdf}}
    \caption{The machine associated with the function $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the machine associated with $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:DetermineSquaringFunctionNotComputable}
  \end{figure}
  Apply the \smn{}~Theorem to get a family of functions 
  parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
  \figureref{fig:DetermineSquaringFunctionNotComputable}.
  Then $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
  if and only if 
  $\computablefunction{square\_decider}(s(e,x))=1$.

  So, since the function~$s$ is computable, if 
  we can mechanically compute $\computablefunction{square\_decider}$
  then we can mechanically solve the \HP.
  But we can't mechanically solve the \HP{} so we cannot mechanically
  compute  $\computablefunction{square\_decider}$.
  \item 
  We will show that this function is not computable.
  \begin{equation*}
    \computablefunction{same\_value\_decider}(e)=
    \begin{cases}
      1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(y)=\TMfcn_e(y+1)$ for some~$y$}  \\
      0 &\case{otherwise} 
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}

  This function
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
      \uparrow   &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  is intuitively computable by the flowchart on the left of 
  \figureref{fig:DetermineSameutoutsConsecInputsNotComputable}.
  So, by Church's Thesis there is a
  Turing machine that computes it;
  let that machine have index~$e$.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1002.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1003.pdf}}
    \caption{The machine associated with the function $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:DetermineSameutoutsConsecInputsNotComputable}
  \end{figure}
  Apply the \smn{}~Theorem to get a family of functions 
  parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
  \figureref{fig:DetermineSameutoutsConsecInputsNotComputable}.
  Observe that these machines produce the function with constant
  output~$42$ if and only if the machine gets through the middle box.
  More precisely stated, 
  $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
  if and only if 
  $\computablefunction{same\_value\_decider}(s(e,x))=1$.

  Since the function~$s$ is computable, if 
  we can mechanically compute $\computablefunction{same\_value\_decider}$
  then we can mechanically solve the \HP,
  which we cannot do.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that each of these is an unsolvable problem by reducing it to the \HP.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given~$x$, determine whether $\TMfcn_x$ fails to converge on input~$5$.
\item Given an index, determine if the computable function with 
  that index fails to converge on any odd number.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item 
    We will show that this function is not computable;~no Turing machine
    has this input-output behavior.
    \begin{equation*}
      \computablefunction{diverges\_on\_five\_decider}(e)=
      \begin{cases}
        1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(5)\diverges$}  \\
        0 &\case{otherwise} 
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    The function
    \begin{equation*}
      \psi(x,y)=
      \begin{cases}
        42        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
        \uparrow   &\case{otherwise}
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    is intuitively computable by the flowchart on the left of 
    \figureref{fig:DivergeOnFiveNotComputable}.
    So Church's Thesis says that there is a
    Turing machine that computes it.
    Let that machine have index~$e$, so that $\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$.
    \begin{figure}
      \centering
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1006.pdf}}
      \qquad
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1007.pdf}}
      \caption{The machine associated with $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
      \label{fig:DivergeOnFiveNotComputable}
    \end{figure}
    The \smn{}~Theorem gives a family of functions 
    parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
    \figureref{fig:DivergeOnFiveNotComputable}.
    Then
    $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
    if and only if 
    $\computablefunction{diverges\_on\_five\_decider}(s(e,x))=1$.
    Because the function~$s$ is mechanically computable, this means that if 
    we could mechanically compute 
    $\computablefunction{diverges\_on\_five\_decider}$
    then we could mechanically solve the \HP.
    But we cannot mechanically solve the \HP, so we cannot mechanically
    compute $\computablefunction{diverges\_on\_five\_decider}$.
  \item
    We want to show that this function is not computable.
    \begin{equation*}
      \computablefunction{diverge\_on\_odds\_decider}(e)=
      \begin{cases}
        1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(y)\diverges$ for all odd $y\in\N$}  \\
        0 &\case{otherwise} 
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    The argument given in the prior item will do again here.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that each of these is an unsolvable problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given an index~$e$, decide if the function~$\phi_e$
computed by machine~$\TM_e$ is the function
$x\mapsto x+1$.
\item Given an index~$e$, decide if the function~$\phi_e$
fails to converge on some inputs $x$ and~$2x$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
    We will show that no Turing machine
    has this input-output behavior.
    \begin{equation*}
      \computablefunction{successor\_decider}(e)=
      \begin{cases}
        1 &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(x)=x+1$ for all $x\in\N$}  \\
        0 &\case{otherwise} 
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    The function
    \begin{equation*}
      \psi(x,y)=
      \begin{cases}
        y+1        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
        \uparrow   &\case{otherwise}
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    is intuitively computable by the flowchart on the left of 
    \figureref{fig:DetermineIfSuccessorNotComputable}.
    So Church's Thesis says that there is a
    Turing machine that computes it.
    Let that machine have index~$e$, so that $\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)$.
    \begin{figure}
      \centering
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1020.pdf}}
      \qquad
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1021.pdf}}
      \caption{Sketches of the machine associated with $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the machine associated with $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
      \label{fig:DetermineIfSuccessorNotComputable}
    \end{figure}
    The \smn{}~Theorem gives a family of functions 
    parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
    \figureref{fig:DetermineIfSuccessorNotComputable}.
    Then
    $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
    if and only if 
    $\computablefunction{successor\_decider}(s(e,x))=1$.
    The function~$s$ is mechanically computable,
    so if 
    we could mechanically compute $\computablefunction{successor\_decider}$
    then we could mechanically solve the \HP.
    But we can't do that, so we cannot mechanically
    compute $\computablefunction{successor\_decider}$.
  \item 
    We will show that this function is not mechanically computable.
    \begin{equation*}
      \computablefunction{converges\_on\_twice\_input\_decider}(e)=
      \begin{cases}
        1 &\case{if there is $x\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$ and $\TMfcn_e(2x)\converges$}  \\
        0 &\case{otherwise} 
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    The function
    \begin{equation*}
      \psi(x,y)=
      \begin{cases}
        42        &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  \\
        \uparrow  &\case{otherwise}
      \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    is intuitively computable by the flowchart on the left of 
    \figureref{fig:ConvergeXAndTwoXNotComputable}.
    So Church's Thesis says that there is a
    Turing machine that computes it.
    Let that machine have index~$e$.
    \begin{figure}
      \centering
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1022.pdf}}
      \qquad
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1023.pdf}}
      \caption{Sketch of the machine for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
      \label{fig:ConvergeXAndTwoXNotComputable}
    \end{figure}
    Apply the \smn{}~Theorem to get a family of functions 
    parametrized by~$x$, whose machines are sketched on the right of  
    \figureref{fig:ConvergeXAndTwoXNotComputable}.
    Then
    $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$
    if and only if 
    $\computablefunction{converges\_on\_twice\_input\_decider}(s(e,x))=1$.
    So if 
    we could mechanically compute 
    $\computablefunction{converges\_on\_twice\_input\_decider}$
    then we could mechanically solve the \HP.
    But we cannot mechanically solve the \HP, so we cannot mechanically
    compute $\computablefunction{converges\_on\_twice\_input\_decider}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each, if it is mechanically solvable
then sketch a program to solve it.
If it is unsolvable then show that.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given $e$, determine the number of states in $\TM_e$.
\item Given $e$, determine whether $\TM_e$ halts when the input is the
  empty string.
\item Given $e$, determine if $\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$ within one
  hundred steps. 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item This is solvable.
  Given the index~$e$, start by translating it to the set of 
  Turing machine instructions, $\TM_e$
  (remember that we are using numberings that are acceptable in 
  the sense defined on page~\pageref{def:AcceptableNumbering}).
  Now count the number of states that appear in that Turing machine.
  \item This is not solvable; $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}$
  is not mechanically computable.
  For, consider this function. 
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      42          &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$} \\
      \uparrow    &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*} 
  It is intuitively mechanically computable, and so by Church's Thesis 
  there is a Turing machine that computes it.
  Let the index of that machine be~$e$.
  Apply the \smn~theorem to parametrize~$x$,
  giving a uniformily computable family of functions~$\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.
  (The $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$'s are functions of no input; that's fine as the
  associated Turing machine simply does not read any input from the tape.)

  Then $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$ if and only if 
  $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}(s(e,x))=1$.
  If $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}$ were mechancially computable
  then we could mechanically solve the \HP,
  so $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}$ is not mechancially computable.
  \item This is solvable.
  From the index~$e$, use a Universal Turing machine to run~$\TM_e$
  on input~$e$ for one hundred steps. 
  By the end either it has halted or it hasn't.     
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is 
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ runs for at least $100$ steps on input~$5$}}$
an index set?
\begin{answer}
  No, it is not an index set.
  We can write two Turing machines with the same behavior, say, 
  on input~$x$ they return output~$x$.
  But one of them takes only a few steps to do this while
  the other takes $101$ steps. 
  The index of the second set is a member of~$\mathcal{I}$ but the
  index of the first set is not a member.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each problem in \cref{exer:ShowUnsolvableHP}, use Rice's Theorem to
show that it is unsolvable.
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Recall the first problem:~given an index~$x$, 
  decide if the Turing machine~$\TM_x$ squares its input so that  
  $\TMfcn_x(y)=y^2$ for all $y\in\N$.
  To apply Rice's Theorem we must show that
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{x\in\N\suchthat
                     \text{$\TMfcn_x(y)=y^2$ for all $y\in\N$}}$
  is a nontrivial index set.
  First, it is not empty because clearly we can write a program that
  squares its input, and then by Church's Thesis there is a Turing
  machine with that behavior.
  That machine's index is a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  Second, $\mathcal{I}$ is not equal to~$\N$ 
  because we can write a program that does not
  square its input, for instance because it does not halt on any input.
  By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior.
  and the machine's index is not a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  To finish we verify that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  Suppose that $x\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{x}$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{x}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}$.
  Because $x\in\mathcal{I}$ we must have that $\TMfcn_x(y)=y^2$ for
  all $y\in\N$.
  But then because the two have the same behavior, 
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}(y)=y^2$ for all~$y$ also, and therefore 
  $\hat{x}\in\mathcal{I}$.
  Thus, $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
\item The problem is:~given~$x$, 
  determine if~$\TMfcn_x$ is such that there is a $y\in\N$
  where $\TMfcn_x(y)=\TMfcn_x(y+1)$.
  For Rice's Theorem we will show that
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{x\in\N\suchthat
                     \text{$\TMfcn_x(y)=\TMfcn_x(y+1)$ for some $y\in\N$}}$
  is a nontrivial index set.
  First, $\mathcal{I}$ is not empty because we can write a program that
  outputs~$1$ for all input, and then by Church's Thesis there is a Turing
  machine with that behavior.
  That machine's index is a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  Second, $\mathcal{I}$ is not all of~$\N$ 
  because we can write a program that does not halt on any input.
  By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior.
  and the machine's index is not a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  We finish by verifying that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  Suppose that $x\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{x}$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{x}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}$.
  Because $x\in\mathcal{I}$ there must exists a $y\in\N$ such that
  $\TMfcn_x(y)=\TMfcn_x(y+1)$.
  Because the two have the same behavior, 
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}(y)=\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}(y+1)$ also, and therefore 
  $\hat{x}\in\mathcal{I}$.
  Thus, $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that each of these is an unsolvable problem by applying
Rice's Theorem.
\begin{exparts}
\item The problem of determining if a function is total, that is,
converges on every input.
\item The problem of determining if a function is partial, that is,
fails to converge on some input.
% \item The problem of deciding if the function ever converges, on any input.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item We first state the problem precisely:~given an index~$x$, 
  decide if~$\TMfcn_x(y)\converges$ for all $y\in\N$.
  To show that this problem is unsolvable we will verify that
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{x\in\N\suchthat
                     \text{$\TMfcn_x(y)\converges$ for all $y\in\N$}}$
  is a nontrivial index set.

  It is not empty because we can write a program that
  returns its input, and then by Church's Thesis there is a Turing
  machine with that behavior.
  That machine's index is a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  It is not equal to~$\N$ 
  because we can write a program that does not halt on any input.
  By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior
  and the machine's index is not a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  We finish verifying that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  Suppose that $x\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{x}$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{x}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}$.
  Because $x\in\mathcal{I}$ we know that $\TMfcn_x(y)\converges$ for
  all $y\in\N$.
  Because the two have the same behavior, 
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}(y)\converges$ for all~$y$ also. 
  Therefore $\hat{x}\in\mathcal{I}$
  and $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
\item This set is the complement of the one in the prior item.
  \Cref{exer:IndexSetsClosedSetOps} shows that the complement of an
  index set is also an index set.
  But we don't have that result in the main section body so we will 
  verify that the given problem is unsolvable without reference to it.

  The problem is:~given an index~$x$, 
  decide if~$\TMfcn_x(y)\diverges$ for some $y\in\N$.
  To show that this is unsolvable we will verify that
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{x\in\N\suchthat
                     \text{$\TMfcn_x(y)\diverges$ for some $y\in\N$}}$
  is a nontrivial index set.

  The set~$\mathcal{I}$ is not empty because we can write a program that
  that does not halt on any input and then by Church's Thesis there is a Turing
  machine with that behavior.
  That machine's index is a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  The set is not equal to~$\N$ 
  because we can write a program that returns its input and then
  by Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior.
  That machine's index is not a member of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  We finish verifying that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  Suppose that $x\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{x}$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{x}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}$.
  Because $x\in\mathcal{I}$ we know that $\TMfcn_x(y)\diverges$ for
  at least one $y\in\N$.
  Because the two have the same behavior, 
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}(y)\diverges$ for at least one~$y$ also. 
  Therefore $\hat{x}\in\mathcal{I}$
  and $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Define that a Turing machine 
\definend{accepts}\index{Turing machine!accept a language}\index{language!accepted by Turing machine} 
a set of bit strings $\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\B}$
if that machine inputs bit strings, and it halts on all inputs, and 
it outputs~$1$ if and only if the input is a member of~$\lang$.
Show that each problem is unsolvable, using Rice's Theorem.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
The problem of deciding, given $x\in\N$, whether $\TM_x$ accepts an
infinite language. 
\item
The problem of deciding, given $e\in\N$, whether $\TM_e$ accepts the string
\str{101}.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  For both items we will use Rice's Theorem.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item 
  We will show that this is a nontrivial index set:
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ accepts an infinite language}}$.

  First, $\mathcal{I}$ is nonempty because the set 
  $\lang=\kleenestar{\B}$ is infinite and
  we can easily write a Turing machine to output~$1$ on all inputs, which
  accepts this subset of $\B$.
  The index of that Turing machine is an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  Second, $\mathcal{I}$ does not equal~$\N$ 
  because we can easily write a Turing machine to output~$0$ on all inputs, which
  accepts the empty subset of~$\B$, which is not infinite.
  The index of that Turing machine is not an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  Finally, to verify that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, suppose that
  $x\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{x}\in\N$ is such that 
  $\TMfcn_x\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}$.
  Because $x\in\mathcal{I}$ there is an infinite $\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\B}$
  so that $\TM_x$ accepts~$\lang$. 
  Because $\TMfcn_x\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}$ the set of bit strings
  for which $\TM_{\hat{x}}$ outputs~$1$ equals the set for which 
  $\TM_x$ outputs~$1$, and so $\TM_{\hat{x}}$ also accepts an infinite
  set of bit strings.
  Thus $\hat{x}\in\mathcal{I}$, and $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  \item 
  We will show that 
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ accepts \str{101}}}$
  is a nontrivial index set.
  First, $\mathcal{I}$ is nonempty because
  we can write a Turing machine to output~$1$ on all inputs, which
  accepts the string~\str{101}.
  The index of that Turing machine is an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.
  Second, $\mathcal{I}$ does not equal~$\N$ 
  because we can write a Turing machine to output~$0$ on all inputs, which
  does not accept the string~\str{101}.
  The index of that Turing machine is not an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.

  Finally, to verify that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, suppose that
  $x\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{x}\in\N$ is such that 
  $\TMfcn_x\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}$.
  Because $x\in\mathcal{I}$ the machine $\TM_x$ accepts~\str{101}. 
  Because $\TMfcn_x\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{x}}$ the 
  machine $\TM_{\hat{x}}$ also accepts~\str{101}.
  Thus $\hat{x}\in\mathcal{I}$, and $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that this problem is mechanically unsolvable: give~$e$, determine if
there is an input~$x$ so that~$\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$.
\begin{answer}
  We will use Rice's Theorem so consider
  $\mathcal{I}=\set{e\suchthat  \text{$\TMfcn_e(x)\converges $ for some $x\in\N$}}$.
  This set is not empty because we can wite a Turing machine that halts on all
  inputs.
  This set is not equal to~$\N$ because we can write a Turing machine that
  fails to halt for any input.

  To show this is an index set suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that
  the number~$\hat{e}$ is such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Because $e\in\mathcal{I}$ there is a number~$x_0$ such that 
  $\TMfcn_{e}(x_0)\converges$.
  Then $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(x_0)\converges$ also, 
  because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ and this is an index set.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Fix integers $a,b,c\in\Z$.
Consider the problem of determining, given $\cantor(x,y)$, whether $ax+by=c$.
Is that problem solvable or unsolvable?
\begin{answer}
  This problem is solvable.
  We can write a program that inputs $\cantor(x,y)$, applies the 
  unpairing function to get $x$ and~$y$, and then 
  plugs them into $ax+by$, to see if the result equals~$c$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} 
\begin{credit}
  \href{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/86660}{SE user Rajesh R} 
  % https://cs.stackexchange.com/users/78255/rajesh-r), Whether TM accepts epsilon?, URL (version: 2018-01-13): 
\end{credit}
Your classmate says, ``Here is a problem that is about the behavior of
machines but is also solvable:~given~$x$, determine whether $\TM_x$ only halts
on an empty input tape.
To solve this problem, give machine~$\TM_e$ an empty input and
see whether it goes on.''
Where are they mistaken?
\begin{answer}
  Briefly, we cannot tell anything by whether the machine `goes on'.
  It can go on to do quite complex things.

  For more, we can reduce this problem to the \HP.
  Consider this $\map{\psi}{\N^2}{\N}$.
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42  &\case{if machine $\TM_x$ halts on~$x$ and $y=\emptystring$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  (We can take this machine to have tape alphabet 
  $\Sigma=\set{\str{B},\str{1}}$.)
  This is intuitively computable so Church's Thesis gives that there is 
  a Turing machine with this behavior, and we can assume it has index~$e$.

  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to parametrize~$x$,
  giving a family of machines and associated computable 
  functions as here.
  \begin{equation*}
     \TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=
     \begin{cases}
       42 &\case{if machine $\TM_x$ halts on~$x$ and $y=\emptystring$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
     \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  Then $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$ if and only if the function
  $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$ halts only on $y=\emptystring$.
  So if we could check the latter mechanically then we could mechanically 
  solve the \HP, which of course we cannot.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a trivial index set:~fill in the blanks
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\suchthat \text{\underline{\makebox[3em]{}}$\TM_e$\underline{\makebox[3em]{}}}}$
so that the set~$\mathcal{I}$ is empty.
\begin{answer}
  There are of course many answers;
  one is $\mathcal{I}_0=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ solves the \HP}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise} 
% As described in \cref{ex:NotIndexSet}, 
% produce two Turing machines 
% giving an output of $0$ for all inputs, and such that
% and one machine contains an instruction starting with
% $q_{10}$ while the other does not.    
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that each of these is an index set.
\begin{exparts}
\item
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{machine $\TM_e$ halts on at least five inputs}}$
\item
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{the function $\TMfcn_e$ is one-to-one}}$
\item
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{the function $\TMfcn_e$ is either total or else $\TMfcn_e(3)\diverges$}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
  We will call each set~$\mathcal{I}$.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and suppose that $\hat{e}$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Because  $e\in\mathcal{I}$ the machine $\TM_e$ halts on at least five inputs,
  that is, the function $\TMfcn_e$ converges on at least five inputs.
  Because $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$, the function
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ also halts on at least five inputs, namely, the 
  same five.
  Thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ also.
  \item Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and suppose also 
  that $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Because  $e\in\mathcal{I}$ the function $\TMfcn_e$ 
  is one-to-one.
  Because $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$, the function
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ is also one-to-one.
  Thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.
  \item Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and also 
  that $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that
  $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  Because  $e\in\mathcal{I}$ the function $\TMfcn_e$ 
  is either total or else $\TMfcn_e(3)\diverges$.
  In the first case, that $\TMfcn_e$ is total,
  because $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$, the function
  $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ is also total.
  In the case that $\TMfcn_e(3)\diverges$,
  because they have the same behavior, $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(3)\diverges$ also.
  In either case, $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Index sets can seem abstract.
Here is an alternate characterization.
The Padding Lemma on 
page~\pageref{lem:EveryCompFcnHasInfManyIndices}
says that every computable function has infinitely many indices.
Thus, there are infinitely many indices for the 
doubling function $f(x)=2x$,
infinitely many for the function that diverges on all inputs, etc.
In the rectangle below imagine the set of all integers and
group them together when they are indices of equal computable functions.
The picture below shows such a partition. 
Select a few parts, such as the ones shown shaded.
Take their union.
That's an index set. 
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/indexsets/indexsets.pdf}}     
\end{center}
More formally stated,
consider the relation $\simeq$ between natural numbers 
given by $e\mathbin{\simeq}\hat{e}$ 
if $\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_{\hat{e}}$.
\begin{exparts*} 
\item
Show that this is an equivalence relation.
\item 
Describe the parts, the equivalence classes.
\item
Show that each index set is the union of some of the equivalence classes.
\hint~show that if an index set contains one element of a class then it contains
them all.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  The relation~$\simeq$ is reflexive because a Turing machine $\TM_e$ 
  has the same behavior as itself:~$\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_e$.
  It is symmetric because if $\TM_e$ has the same 
  behavior as $\TM_{\hat{e}}$ then the converse also holds.
  Transitivity is just as clear.
  \item
  Each equivalence class is a set of integers~$e\in\N$.
  Two integers are together in a class, $e,\hat{e}\in\mathcal{C}$ if 
  the input-output behaviors of the associated Turing machines
  $\TM_e$ and $\TM_{\hat{e}}$ are the
  same,~$\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_{\hat{e}}$.
  (As an example, one class contains the indices of all Turing machines 
  that double the input, 
  $\mathcal{C}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\phi_e(x)=2x$ for all $x$}}$.)
  \item
  We follow the hint.
  Suppose that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set 
  and that $e\in\mathcal{C}$ is an element of~$\mathcal{I}$.
  If $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{C}$ then $\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}\samebehavior\TMfcn_e$
  because $\mathcal{C}$ is an equivalence class for the relation~$\simeq$.
  But~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set so $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ also.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:IndexSetsClosedSetOps}
Because being an index set is a property of a set, we naturally 
consider how it interacts with set operations.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Show that the complement of an index set is also an index set.
\item
Show that the collection of index sets is closed under union.
\item
Is it closed under intersection?
If so prove that and if not then give a counterexample.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Let $\mathcal{I}$ be an index set and consider the 
  complement,~$\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$.
  Suppose that $e\in\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$ and suppose also that 
  $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  We will show that $\hat{e}\in\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$.

  By the definition of set complement, either $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$
  or $\hat{e}\in\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$.
  If $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ then because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$
  and because $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, we have that $e\in\mathcal{I}$
  also.
  This contradicts that $e\in\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$ and
  therefore $\hat{e}\in\setcomp{\mathcal{I}}$. 
  \item 
  Fix a collection $\mathcal{I}_j$ of index sets and consider
  the union $\mathcal{I}=\cup_j\mathcal{I}_j$.
  Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and suppose also that 
  $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  We will show that $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.

  The number~$e$ is an element of the union $\cup_j\mathcal{I}_j=\mathcal{I}$
  so it is an element of some $\mathcal{I}_{j_0}$.
  Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ and because
  $\mathcal{I}_{j_0}$ is an index set, the number~$\hat{e}$ is an element
  of $\mathcal{I}_{j_0}$, and is therefore an element of
  the union~$\mathcal{I}$.
  Thus $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  \item 
  Let $\mathcal{I}_j$ be a collection of index sets and let
  $\mathcal{I}=\cap_j\mathcal{I}_j$.
  Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$, and also that 
  $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$.
  We will show that $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$

  Since~$e$ is an element of the intersection 
  $\cap_j\mathcal{I}_j=\mathcal{I}$, it is an element of each set
  $\mathcal{I}_{j}$.
  Because $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ and because
  $\mathcal{I}_{j}$ is an index set, the number~$\hat{e}$ is an element
  of $\mathcal{I}_{j}$ also.
  As $\hat{e}$ is an element of each $\mathcal{I}_{j}$, 
  it is an element of the intersection~$\mathcal{I}$.
  Thus $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:OtherHalfRicesThm}
Do the $e_0\in\mathcal{I}$ case in the proof of Rice's Theorem, 
\cref{thm:RicesThm}.
\begin{answer}
  The differences between this case and the one in the section are very small.
  The proof there starts by considering an index set~$\mathcal{I}$.
  It fixes an index $e_0\in\N$ so that 
  $\TMfcn_{e_0}(y)\diverges$ for all inputs~$y\in\N$.
  It then does the $e_0\notin\mathcal{I}$ case.
  So what's left is to do $e_0\in\mathcal{I}$.
  Here's a difference:~because $\mathcal{I}$ is nontrivial,
  it does not equal~$\N$ so there is some~$e_1\notin\mathcal{I}$.  
  Because $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, $\TMfcn_{e_0}\not\simeq\TMfcn_{e_1}$.
  Thus there is an input~$y$ such that $\TMfcn_{e_1}(y)\converges$.

  Consider the flowchart on the left of
  \figureref{fig:HalfRicesTheorem}.
  By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior;
  call it~$\TM_e$.
  Apply the \smn{} theorem to paramatrize~$x$,
  resulting in the uniformily computable 
  family of functions $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$, whose computation is 
  outlined on the right of \figureref{fig:HalfRicesTheorem}.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp12.pdf}}
    \hspace{4em}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp13.pdf}}
    \caption{Sketches of machines for the proof of half of Rice's Theorem.}
    \label{fig:HalfRicesTheorem}
  \end{figure}
  We've constructed the machines so that if 
  $\TMfcn_x(x)\diverges$ then $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{e_0}$
  and thus $s(e,x)\in\mathcal{I}$. 
  Further,
  if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$ then $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{e_1}$
  and thus $s(e,x)\notin\mathcal{I}$.
  Therefore if $\mathcal{I}$ were mechanically computable, so that
  we could effectively check whether $s(e,x)\in\mathcal{I}$,
  then we could mechanically solve the \HP.
  (This part of the argument is like the one in the section, but with the 
  cases of $s(e,x)\in\mathcal{I}$ and $s(e,x)\notin\mathcal{I}$
  reversed.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that most problems are unsolvable by showning
that there are uncountably many functions $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$
that are not computed by any Turing machine, while the number of
function that are computable is countable.
\begin{answer}
  The set of functions $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ is the disjoint union of two
  subsets:~the functions that are computable and the functions that are not.
  We know that the set of computable functions is countable.
  Because the union of two countable sets is countable, if the set
  of non-computable functions were also countable then there would be 
  countably many functions overall, which there are not.
  Thus the set of non-computable functions is not countable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give an example of a computable function that is total,
meaning that it converges on all inputs, 
but whose range is not computable.
\begin{answer}
  Fix an element $k\in K$.
  The range of this function is~$K$.
  \begin{equation*}
    f(x,n)=
    \begin{cases}
    x  &\case{if $\TM_x$ with input~$x$ halts in $n$ steps}  \\
    k  &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  Clearly the function is computable and converges on all inputs.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set of bit strings is a 
\definend{decidable language}\index{decidable!language}\index{language!decidable} 
if its
characteristic function is computable.
Prove these.
Show that the collection of decidable languages is closed under these
operations.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
The union of two decidable languages is a decidable language.
\item
The intersection of two decidable languages is a decidable language
\item
The complement of a decidable language is a decidable language.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  Start with decidable languages~$\lang_0$ and~$\lang_1$ and 
  consider the union $\lang=\lang_0\cup\lang_1$.
  Because they are decidable, there are Turing machines $\TM_{e_0}$
  and $\TM_{e_1}$ that decide membership.
  \begin{equation*}
    \TMfcn_{e_0}(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      1  &\case{if $x\in\lang_0$}  \\
      0  &\case{if $x\notin\lang_0$}  
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{e_1}(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      1  &\case{if $x\in\lang_1$}  \\
      0  &\case{if $x\notin\lang_1$}  
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  Note that because they decide membership, these machines halt on all inputs.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1024.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1025.pdf}}
    \caption{Sketches of machines to decide union and intersection of decidable languages.}
    \label{fig:DecidableLangsUnionInt}
  \end{figure}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  A sketch of the machine that decides membership in $\lang_0\cup\lang_1$ 
  is in \figureref{fig:DecidableLangsUnionInt}.
  A number is an element of the union if it is a member of either set.  
  So, given~$x$, this machine first runs $\TM_{e_0}$ on~$x$.
  If that returns~$1$ then $x$ is a member of the union, so the new
  machine outputs~$1$.
  Otherwise the new machine runs~$\TM_{e_1}$ on~$x$ and returns the result.
  \item
  A sketch of the machine that decides membership in $\lang_0\cap\lang_1$ 
  is in \figureref{fig:DecidableLangsUnionInt}.
  A number is an element of the union if it is a member of both sets.  
  So, given~$x$, this machine first runs $\TM_{e_0}$ on~$x$.
  If that returns~$0$ then $x$ is not a member of the intersection, so the new
  machine outputs~$0$.
  Otherwise the new machine runs~$\TM_{e_1}$ on~$x$ and returns the result.
  \item 
  Complement is straightforward.
  For the complement of~$\lang_0$ the new machine just runs $\TM_{e_0}$
  and subtracts the result from~$1$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{unsolvability|)}\index{unsolvable problem|)}\index{problems!unsolvable|)}



% ========================================
\section{Computably enumerable sets}\index{computably enumerable|(}\index{set!computably enumerable|(}\label{sec:ComputablyEnumerableSets}
% Required for the section on oracles, which in turn is required
% for the seciton on barriers to solving P=NP.
 
To attack the \HP{} the natural thing is to 
start by simulating $\TM_0$ on input~$0$ for a single step.
Then simulate $\TM_0$ on input~$0$ for a second step and
also simulate $\TM_1$ on input~$1$ for one step.
After that, run $\TM_0$ on~$0$ for a third step,
followed by $\TM_1$ on~$1$ for a second step,
and then $\TM_2$ on~$2$ for one step.
This process cycles among the $\TM_e$ on~$e$ simulations,
running each for a step.
Eventually you will see some of these halt and 
the elements of $K$ will fill in.
On computer systems this interleaving is called time-slicing 
but in theory discussions it is called 
\citetext{\definend{dovetailing}}{% https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Joinery-throughdovetail.svg
  A dovetail joint is often used in woodworking, 
  for example to hold together the sides of a drawer.
  It weaves the two sides in alternately, as shown here.
  \protect\begin{center}
  \protect\small
  \protect\includegraphics[width=0.25\textwidth]{background/pix/dovetail.png} 
  \protect\end{center}}.\index{dovetailing}

We are listing the elements of~$K$:~first $f(0)$, 
then~$f(1)$, \ldots{}
(the computable function $f$ is such that, for instance, $f(0)=e$ where
it happens that $\TM_e$ on input~$e$ is the first of these to halt). 
\cref{def:Countable} gives the terminology that a function~$f$ with
domain~$\N$ \definend{enumerates} its range. 

Why won't this process of gradual enumeration solve the \HP?
If $e\in K$ then it will tell us that eventually, 
but if $e\notin K$ then it will not.

%<*def:ComputableSet>
\begin{definition}
A set of natural numbers
is \definend{computable}\index{computable!set}\index{set!computable}
(or \definend{decidable})\index{decidable!set}\index{set!decidable}
if its
characteristic function is computable, 
if we can effectively determine both membership and non-membership.
\end{definition}
%</def:ComputableSet>

%<*def:ComputablyEnumerable>
\begin{definition}  \label{def:ComputablyEnumerable}
A set of natural numbers is 
\definend{computably enumerable}\index{computably enumerable set}\index{set!computably enumerable}\index{set!c.e.} 
(or \definend{recursively enumerable}\index{recursively enumerable set}\index{set!recursively enumerable}\index{set!r.e.} 
or \definend{c.e.}\index{c.e. set} or \definend{r.e.})\index{r.e. set}
if it is effectively listable, that is, if it is
the range of a total computable function, or it is the empty set.
\end{definition}
%</def:ComputablyEnumerable>

%<*description:DecidableAndSemidecidable>
So a set~$S$ is computable if there is a
Turing machine that decides membership; this machine
inputs a number~$x$ and 
decides either `yes' or `no' whether $x\in S$.
With computably enumerable sets there is a machine that 
decides `yes' 
but that machine need not address `no'.
% Restated, a set is computable if there is a Turing machine that 
% recognizes both members and nonmembers, while
% a set is computably enumerable if there is a Turing machine that
% recognizes members.
Computably enumerable sets are also called 
\definend{semicomputable}\index{semicomputable set}\index{set!semicomputable}
or \definend{semidecidable}.\index{semidecidable set}\index{set!semidecidable}
%</description:DecidableAndSemidecidable>

This is the natural way to computably produce sets\Dash picture 
a stream of numbers
$\TMfcn_e(0)$, $\TMfcn_e(1)$, $\TMfcn_e(2)$,~\ldots{}\, gradually filling 
out the set.
(This list may contain repeats, and the numbers could appear 
in jumbled up order, that is, not necessarily in ascending order.)

% The definition has that the enumerating function is total, which is 
% natural for an `enumeration'; if for instance
% $f(0)\converges$, $f(1)\diverges$, and~$f(2)\converges$ and we naively try to
% produce the range by running the computable function~$f$ on successive inputs
% then we will never get to the input~$2$.
% But dovetailing solves that problem.
% So we will next show that we get the same 
% collection of sets if we relax the condition to 
% allow partial computable functions. 

%<*lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn>
\begin{lemma} \label{lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}
The following are equivalent for a set of natural numbers.
\begin{thmparts}
\item It is computably enumerable, that is, either it is empty or it is
  the range of a total computable function.
\item It is the domain of a partial computable function.
\item It is the range of a partial computable function.
\end{thmparts}
\end{lemma}
%</lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn>

\begin{proof}
We will show that the first two are equivalent.
That the second and third are equivalent is 
\cref{exer:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}.

Assume first that $S$ is computably enumerable.
If $S$ is empty then it is the domain of the partial computable function that 
diverges on all inputs.
So instead assume that $S$ is the range of a total computable~$f$\!,
and we will describe a computable~$g$ with domain~$S$.
Given the input~$x\in\N$, to compute $g(x)$
enumerate $f(0)$, $f(1)$, \ldots{} 
and wait for~$x$ to appear as one of the values.
If~$x$ does appear then halt the computation 
(and return some nominal value).
If~$x$ never appears then the computation never halts.

For the other direction,
assume that $S$ is the domain of a partial computable function~$g$, 
to show that it is computably enumerable.
If $S$ is empty then it is computably enumerable by definition.
Otherwise we must produce a total computable~$f$ whose range is~$S$. 
If~$S$ is finite but not empty, $S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_m}$,
then such a function is given by
$0\mapsto s_0$, \ldots{} $m\mapsto s_m$, and $n\mapsto s_0$ for $n>m$. 

Finally assume that~$S$ is infinite. 
Fix some $s_0\in S$. 
Given $n\in \N$, run the computations of each of
$g(0)$, $g(1)$, \ldots{} $g(n)$ for $n$-many steps.
Possibly some of these computations halt.
Define $f(n)$ to be the least $k$ where $g(k)$ halts within~$n$ steps, and so
that $k\notin\set{f(0),f(1),\ldots f(n-1)}$.
If no such~$k$ exists then define $f(n)=\hat{s}$; this makes 
$f$ a total function.

If $t\notin S$ then $g(t)$ never converges 
and so~$t$ is never enumerated by~$f$.
If $s\in S$ then eventually $g(s)$ must converge,
in some number of steps,~$n_s$.
The number~$s$ is then is queued for output by~$f$ in the sense that
it will be enumerated by~$f$ as, at most, $f(n_s+s)$. 
\end{proof}

Many authors define computably enumerable sets using the second or third
items.
\cref{def:ComputablyEnumerable} is more natural but also more
technically awkward.

\begin{definition}
\definend{$W_e=\set{y\suchthat \TMfcn_e\converges}$}
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:ComputableAndCE}
\begin{thmparts}
\item \label{lem:SetComputableThenSetCE}
%<*lem:ComputableAndCEi>
  If a set is computable then it is computably enumerable.
%</lem:ComputableAndCEi>
\item \label{lem:SetComputableIffSetAndCompCE}
%<*lem:ComputableAndCEii>
  A set is computable if and only if both it and its
  complement are computably enumerable.
%</lem:ComputableAndCEii>
\end{thmparts}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First, let~$S\subseteq\N$ be computable.
We will produce an effective enumeration~$f$\!.
If $S$ is finite, take $f(0)=s_0$, $f(1)=s_1$, \ldots
$f(n-1)=s_{n-1}$, and set $f(m)\diverges$ for $m\geq n$.
The other case is that $S$ is infinite.
For~$f(0)$, find the smallest element of~$S$ 
by testing whether $0\in S$, then whether $1\in S$, \ldots\sentencespace 
This search is effective because~$S$ is computable,
and it must halt because $S$ is infinite.
Similarly, $f(k)$ will be the $k$-th smallest element in~$S$.

As to the second item, first
suppose that $S$ is computable.
The prior item shows that it is computably enumerable.
The complement of~$S$ is also computable because
its characteristic function is $\charfcn{\setcomp{S}}=1-\charfcn{S}$.
So the prior item shos that
$\setcomp{S}$ is also computably enumerable.

Finally,
suppose that both $S$ and $\setcomp{S}$ are computably enumerable.
Let $S$ be enumerated by~$f$ and let $\setcomp{S}$ be
enumerated by $\bar{f}$\!. 
We must give an effective procedure to determine whether a given~$x\in\N$
is an element of~$S$.
We will dovetail the two enumerations:~first run 
the computation of $f(0)$ for a step
and the computation of $\bar{f}(0)$ for a step,
then run the computations of $f(0)$ and
$\bar{f}(0)$ for a second step, etc.
Eventually $x$ will be enumerated into one or the other.
\end{proof}

%<*lem:HPIsCE>
\begin{corollary} \label{cor:KCompNotCE}
The \HP{} set $K$ is computably enumerable.\index{K, the Halting problem set@$K$, the halting problem set}
Its complement $\setcomp{K}$ is not.
\end{corollary}
%</lem:HPIsCE>

\begin{proof}
The set~$K$ is the domain of the function
$f(x)=\TMfcn_x(x)$,
which is mechanically computable by Church's Thesis.
If the complement~$\setcomp{K}$ were computably enumerable then
\cref{lem:ComputableAndCE} would imply that $K$ is computable,
but it isn't.
\end{proof}

That result gives one reason to be interested in computably
enumerability sets, namely that the
\HP{} set~$K$ falls into the class of computably enumerable sets,
as do sets such as 
$\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$
and $\set{e\suchthat \text{there is an $x$ so that $\TMfcn_e(x)=7$}}$. 
So this collection of sets contains
lots of interesting members.

Another reason that these sets are interesting is 
philosophical:~with Church's Thesis we can think that, in a sense, 
computable sets are the only sets 
that we will ever know,
and semidecidable sets are ones that we at least half know.

% .......................................
\begin{exercises}
\recommended
\begin{exercise}
You got a quiz question to define computably enumerable.
A friend of yours says they answered, 
``A set that can be enumerated by a Turing machine
but that is not computable.''
Is that right?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Not completely right.
  It is right that 
  a set is computably enumerable if it can be enumerated by a Turing machine.
  That is, a Turing machine exists that, when started on a blank tape, will
  print all of the sets members on the tape. 
  (This machine just outputs $f(0)$, $f(1)$, \ldots\@ 
  It may run forever if there are
  infinitely many elements in the set.)
  However, the ``but is not computable'' is wrong.
  Any computable set is computably enumerable (although there are 
  computably enumerable sets that are not computable).
  For instance, the set of even numbers is computably enumerable and also
  computable. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Produce a function that enumerates each set, whose range is the given set.
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\N$
\item the even numbers
\item the perfect squares
\item the set $\set{5,7,11}$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
For each part we will produce a function $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ that is effective. 
\begin{exparts}
  \item The identity function, $f(x)=x$, works.
  \item The  function $f(x)=2x$ comes to mind.
  \item $f(x)=x^2$
  \item This will do.
  \begin{equation*}
     f(x)=
     \begin{cases}
       5  &\case{if $x=0$}  \\
       7  &\case{if $x=1$}  \\
      11  &\case{otherwise}
     \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Produce a function that enumerates each set
\begin{exparts*}
\item the prime numbers
\item the natural numbers whose digits are in non-increasing order
  (e.g., $531$ or $5331$ but not $513$).
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  For each item we produce an effective function $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$. 
  \begin{exparts}
  \item The association $n\mapsto\text{$n$-th prime}$ is a function.
    Clearly we can write a program that computes it, so by Church's Thesis
    it is effective.
  \item To compute this function one approach is brute 
    force:~generate the $n$-th one by 
    searching through the natural numbers, $0$, $1$, \ldots{}\,, checking
    each to see if its digits appear in non-increasing order.
    Output the $n$-th such number.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
One of these two is computable and the other is computably enumerable but
not computable.
Which is which?
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input~$4$ in less than twenty steps}}$
\item $\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input~$4$ in more than twenty steps}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  The first is computable, since to decide if a number~$e$ is a member of 
  this set we can run a simulation for twenty steps.
  The second is computably enumerable but not computable.
  It is computably enumerable because we can dovetail computations for the 
  indices $e\in\N$.
  It is not computable because clearly if we could solve it then we could 
  solve the problem of deciding membership in 
  $\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(y)\converges}$, but we cannot solve that problem
  by Rice's Theorem.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Which of these sets is decidable, which are semidecidable but not decidable, 
% and which are neither?
% Justify in one sentence.
% \begin{exparts}
% \item
% The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes more than $100$ steps
% on input~$7$.
% \item
% The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes less than $100$ steps
% on input~$7$.
% \item
% The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes more than $100$ steps
% on every input.
% \item
% The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes less than $100$ steps
% on all inputs.
% \end{exparts}
% \begin{answer}
% \begin{exparts}
% \item
% This set is computable, since we can just run the machine for a hundred steps.
% Because it is computable, it is also computably enumerable.
% \item 
% This set is also computable, again since we can just run the machine
% for a hundred steps.
% Because it is computable, it is also computably enumerable.
% \item
% This set is neither computable nor computably enumerable.
% Testing all inputs would never halt.
% \item
% As with the prior item, this set is neither computable nor computably 
% enumerable.
% \end{exparts}
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Short answer:~for each set state whether it is computable, 
computably enumerable but not computable, or neither.
\begin{exparts*}
\item The set of indices~$e$ of Turing machines that contain an instruction using
state~$q_4$.
\item The set of indices of Turing machines that halt on input~$3$.
\item The set of indices of Turing machines that halt on input~$3$ in fewer than
 $100$ steps.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Decidable.
  Indices are source-equivalent: there is a program that takes in the index~$e$
  and puts out the set of instructions for~$\TM_e$.  
  With that set of instructions, just scan it for~$q_4$,
  \item Semidecidable but not decidable.
  To show it is computably enumerable, use dovetailing on the indices~$e\in\N$
  to run $\TM_e$ on input~$3$.
  When a the machines halts, enumerate its~$e$ into the set.
  As to being not computable, we know from the prior section that this is not 
  a computable set.
  \item Computable.
  Given~$e$, decide if it is in the set by running~$\TM_e$ on~$3$ for the
  hundred steps.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
You read someone online who says, 
``every countable set~$S$ is computably enumerable because if
$\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ has range~$S$ then you have the enumeration 
$S$ as $f(0)$, $f(1)$, \ldots''\sentencespace
Explain why this is wrong.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The function is an enumeration but unless it is computable, it is not
  a computable enumeration.
  This can happen; an example of a countable set that is not 
  computably enumerable is $\setcomp{K}$. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The set $A_5=\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(5)\converges}$ is clearly not
computable.
Show that it is computably enumerable.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Briefly, we dovetail.
  First run $\TM_0$ on input~$5$ for one step.
  Then run  $\TM_0$ on input~$5$ for a second step
  and  $\TM_1$ on input~$5$ for one step.
  Then run  $\TM_0$ for a third step,
  along with $\TM_1$ for its second step
  and $\TM_2$ on input~$5$ for one step.
  Continue in this way.
  The enumerating function~$\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ is:~$f(0)$ is the index
  of the first such computation to halt, $f(1)$ is the index of the second, etc.
  (Note that $A_5$ is infinite since any finite set is computable, so 
  computation of any $f(i)$ will halt in a finite number of steps.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that the set 
$\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(2)=4}$
is computably enumerable.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Dovetail.
  Start by running $\TM_0$ on input~$2$ for one step.
  Then run  $\TM_0$ on input~$2$ for a second step
  and  $\TM_1$ on input~$2$ for one step.
  Next, run  $\TM_0$ for a third step,
  along with $\TM_1$ for its second step
  and $\TM_2$ on input~$2$ for one step.
  Continue in this way.
  Then~$f(0)$ is the index
  of the first such computation to halt and output~$4$, 
  $f(1)$ is the index of the second, etc.
  (This set is infinite since any finite set is computable
  and this set is clearly not computable.
  Thus 
  computation of any $f(i)$ will halt in a finite number of steps.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Name a set that has an enumeration but not a computable enumeration.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The complement of the \HP{} set, $\setcomp{K}$,
  is countable since it is a subset of~$\N$.
  Thus it has an enumeration, a function with domain~$\N$ whose
  range is this set.
  But \cref{cor:KCompNotCE} shows that it has no computable enumeration.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Name three sets that are computably enumerable but not computable.
\begin{answer}\verified
  There are of course many correct answers.
  One answer is to take~$K$ as the first set.
  For the second, take~$K$ with its smallest element omitted and
  for the third, take~$K$ with the smallest two elements omitted. 

  A perhaps less wiseguy-ish answer is to take~$K$ along with some sets
  that we used as examples in the prior section, such as 
  $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$
  and $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{there is $x\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_e(x)=7$}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Let $\definend{K_0}=\set{\sequence{e,x}\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input $x$}}$.\index{K0@$K_0$}
\begin{exparts}
\item
Show that it is computably enumerable.
\item
Show that the columns of $K_0$, the sets
$C_e=\set{\sequence{e,x}\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input $x$}}$
make up all the computable enumerable sets.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  Enumerate it by dovetailing.
  The difference from some of the dovetail examples is that here there are
  two numbers, $e$ and~$x$.
  So recall Cantor's pairing function.
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{r|ccccccc@{\hspace{6pt}}c}
      \tabulartext{Number}  
         &$0$    &$1$   &$2$  &$3$  &$4$  &$5$  &$6$ &\ldots   
     \\ \cline{2-9}
      \tabulartext{Pair} 
                 &$\sequence{0,0}$  
                 &$\sequence{0,1}$ 
                 &$\sequence{1,0}$ 
                 &$\sequence{0,2}$  
                 &$\sequence{1,1}$ 
                 &$\sequence{2,0}$ 
                 &$\sequence{0,3}$  
                 &\ldots 
       \end{tabular}
  \end{center}       
  To dovetail, first run $\TM_0$ on input~$0$ for one step.
  Then run $\TM_0$ on input~$0$ for a second step
  along with $\TM_0$ on input~$1$ for one step.
  Next, run $\TM_0$ on input~$0$ for its third step,
  and $\TM_0$ on input~$1$ for its second step,
  and $\TM_1$ on input~$0$ for one step.
  The enumerating function outputs $\sequence{e,x}$ when 
  $\TM_e$ halts on~$x$.
  \item
  This is immediate from the second item of 
  \cref{lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
We know that there are subsets of~$\N$ that are not computable.
Are the computably enumerable sets the rest of the subsets?
\begin{answer}\verified
  The two collections are not disjoint.
  Rather, the collection of computable sets is a subset of the 
  collection of computably enumerable sets.

  Further, clearly the collection 
  $\set{W_e\suchthat e\in\N}$ is countable.
  Since there are uncountably many subsets of~$\N$ (because
  the power set of~$\N$ has cardinality greater than~$\N$), the 
  computably enumerable sets do not include every subset of~$\N$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that the set 
$\textrm{Tot}=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(y)\converges$ for all $y$}}$
is not computable and not computably enumerable.
\hint~if this collection is computably enumerable then we can get a table
like the one that starts \cref{sec:Unsolvability}, on 
Unsolvability. 
\begin{answer}\verified
  The computable sets are also computably enumerable
  so we will have shown that $\textrm{Tot}$ 
  is neither computable nor computably enumerable if we have shown
  that it is not computably enumerable.

  So, assume that 
  $\text{Tot}=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(y)\converges$ for all $y$}}$
  is computably enumerable, enumerated by~$\map{f}{\N}{\N}$.
  Following the hint, that gives a table like the one starting
  \cref{sec:Unsolvability} on Unsolvability, because 
  the $i,j$ entry $\TMfcn_{i}(j)$ is sure to halt.
  Diagonalize:~consider $\map{h}{\N}{\N}$ given by $h(e)=1+\TMfcn_e(e)$.
  It is clearly effective, total, and unequal to any member of
  $\textrm{Tot}$, which is a contradiction. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Can there be a set such that the problem of determining membership in that
set is unsolvable, and also the set is computably enumerable?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Yes, the \HP{} set~$K$ fits both criteria.
  Three other examples are the set
  $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$,
  the set 
  $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{there is $x\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_e(x)=7$}}$,
  and $\set{e\in\N\suchthat\text{$\TMfcn_e(x)=2x$ for all $x\in\N$}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Sketch a program to enumerate each set.
% \begin{exparts*}
% \item the even numbers
% \item the perfect squares
% \item the primes
% \item the set $\set{5,7,11}$.
% \end{exparts*}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Prove that every finite set is computably enumerable.
\item
Sketch a program that takes as input a finite set and returns
a function that enumerates the set.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  The answer to the second effectively answers the first, but we will do both
  items anyway.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  Let $S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_{k-1}}$ for~$k\in\N$.
  Then this function enumerates~$S$.
  \begin{equation*}
    f(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      1  &\case{if $x=s_0$ or \ldots{} or $x=s_{k-1}$}  \\
      0  &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  It is clearly computable.
  \item
  The algorithm inputs a set of numbers $S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_{k-1}}$.
  It returns a function, the one in the prior item.
  This function is clearly computable.

  Another approach is to start with the number $k=\sizeof{S}$.
  Define a function with  $k+1$ inputs, $x_0,\ldots x_{k-1},y$ that 
  tests whether $y$ equals any of the $x_i$'s, and returns~$1$ if 
  it does or~$0$ if it does not.
  Where $S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_{k-1}}$, apply the \smn{} Theorem to freeze
  the value of each $x_i$ to be~$s_i$.
  That's the desired function.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that every infinite computably enumerable set has an infinite computable
