subset.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Let the computably enumerable set be $W$ 
  and fix a computable enumeration $\TMfcn(0)$, $\TMfcn(1)$, \ldots\sentencespace
  We will define an $S\subseteq W$ that is computable.
  The first element of~$S$ is~$s_0=\TMfcn(0)$.
  The second element~$s_1$ is the first member of the enumeration that
  is larger than $s_0$;~such a number must eventually appear
  because $W$ is infinite.
  In general~$s_i$ is the first element of the enumeration that is larger 
  than $s_{i-1}$ (as with $s_1$, one must eventually appear).

  The set~$S$ is computably enumerable because we have just described a 
  mechanical procedure to enumerate it.
  It is computable because given a number $n\in\N$, to determine
  whether it is an element of~$S$ just wait until either it is enumerated 
  into~$S$ 
  or a number larger than it is enumerated into~$S$, which shows
  $n\notin S$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let $f$ be a partial computable function that 
enumerates the infinite set~$R\subseteq \N$.
Produce a total computable function
that enumerates~$R$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Fix an $\hat{r}\in R$.
  Then this function
  \begin{equation*}
    g(i)=
    \begin{cases}
      f(i)    &\case{if $f(i)\converges$}  \\
      \hat{r} &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
   is total, computable, and enumerates~$R$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Suppose that there is a set $A$ and a computable function $f$ such that
% the set difference $A-\operatorname{range}(f)$ is finite.
% Show that $A$ is computably enumerable.
% \end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Write a program that will, given two enumerating programs, dovetail them.
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set is \textit{enumerable in increasing order}
if there is a computable function~$f$ 
that is increasing:~$n<m$ implies~$f(n)<f(m)$, and whose range is the set.
Prove that
an infinite set~$S$ is computable if and only if it is 
computably enumerable in increasing order.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Suppose that $S$ is computable, so that its characteristic function
  \begin{equation*}
    \charfcn{S}(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      1  &\case{if $x\in S$}  \\
      0  &\case{if not}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  is a computable function.
  To enumerate~$S$ in increasing order the idea is to run $\charfcn{S}(0)$, 
  $\charfcn{S}(1)$, \ldots{}\,, each of which must converge because the
  function is computable, and then the first element of~$S$ is the smallest, the
  second element is the second smallest, etc.

  More formally, we define the enumerating function by:~where $s_0$ 
  is the smallest element of~$S$ then $f(0)=s_0$, and where 
  we have values for $f(0)$, \ldots $f(k)$ then the value for
  $f(k+1)$ is the smallest number~$x$ such that $x>f(k)$ and 
  $\charfcn{S}(x)=1$.
  Because $S$ is infinite, there is always such an~$x$.

  For the other direction, assume that $S$ is an infinite set that is 
  enumerable in increasing order, by the computable function~$h$.
  Given~$x$, to decide whether $x\in S$, compute $h(0)$, $h(1)$, \ldots{}
  until one of the ouputs is~$x$
  (in which case $x\in S$) or one of the outputs is a number larger 
  than~$x$ (in which case $x\notin S$).
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set is \textit{computably enumerable without repetition} if it is
the range of a computable function that is one-to-one.
Prove that a set is computably enumerable and infinite if and only if
it is computably enumerable without repetition.
\begin{answer}\verified
One direction is easy.
If a set is computably enumerable without repetition then 
it must be infinite, simply because if it were finite then
the enumeration would run out of new elements to output.

The other direction is about memoizing.
Let the set~$S$ be infinite and computably enumerable with
enumerating function $f$.
We will produce an enumerating function~$g$ that is one-to-one.
Define $g(0)=f(0)$.
For each $i\in\N$ define $g(i+1)$ to be the first element of~$S$ enumerated
by~$f$ after it enumerates~$g(i)$ that is not an element 
of~$\set{g(0),\ldots\, g(i)}$. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set is \textit{co-computably enumerable} 
if its complement is computabily enumerable.
Produce a set that is neither computably enumerable nor co-computably 
enumerable.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The set of ordered pairs 
  $\set{\sequence{a,b}\suchthat 
       \text{$a\in K$ and $b\in\setcomp{K}$}}$ is not
  computably enumerable, or else $\setcomp{K}$ would be computably enumerable.
  Similarly it is not co-computably enumerable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Computability is a property of sets so we can consider its
interaction with set operations.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Must a subset of a computable set be computable?
\item
Must the union of two computable sets be computable?
\item
The intersection?
\item
The complement?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1024.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1025.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp1026.pdf}}
    \caption{Machines to decide union, intersection, and complement of computable sets.}
    \label{fig:ComputableSetsUnionInt}
  \end{figure}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item No.
  The set $\N$ is obviously computable.
  Its subset~$K$ is not.
  \item Yes.
  Let $S_0\subseteq \N$ and $S_1\subseteq\N$ be computable via
  the functions $\TMfcn_{e_0}$ and~$\TMfcn_{e_1}$.
  A sketch of the machine to compute the union is in
  \figureref{fig:ComputableSetsUnionInt}.
  \item Yes.
  A sketch of the machine to compute the intersection is in
  \figureref{fig:ComputableSetsUnionInt}.
  \item Yes.
  A sketch of the machine to compute the complement is in
  \figureref{fig:ComputableSetsUnionInt}.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Computable enumerability is a property of sets so we can consider its
interaction with set operations.
\begin{exparts*}
% \item
% Must a subset of a computably enumerable set be computably enumerable?
\item
Must the union of two computably enumerable sets be computably enumerable?
\item
The intersection?
\item
The complement?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Yes.
  Let $W_{e_0},W_{e_1}\subseteq\N$ be computably enumerable,
  via $\map{\TMfcn_{e_0},\TMfcn_{e_1}}{\N}{\N}$.
  Dovetail enumerations of the two sets.
  That is, run $\TM_{e_0}$ and~$\TM_{e_1}$ on input~$0$ for a step each.
  Then, run $\TM_{e_0}$ and~$\TM_{e_1}$ on input~$0$ for a second step,
  and also run $\TM_{e_0}$ and~$\TM_{e_1}$ on input~$1$ for a step each.
  Continue in this way, and when
  a number is enumerated into either of $W_{e_0}$ or~$W_{e_1}$,
  enumerate it into their union.
  \item Yes.
  As in the prior item, 
  dovetail enumerations of the two sets.
  When a number has been enumerated into both of $W_{e_0}$ or~$W_{e_1}$,
  enumerate it into their intersection.
  \item No.
  The \HP{} set~$K$ is computably enumerable but its complement is not.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Show that if $h$ is a partially computable function and
% $S$ is a computably enumerable set, then the inverse image
% $h^{-1}(S)$ is computably enumerable.
% \end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% A set $S\subseteq \N^k$ is a \textit{projection}
% of the set $T\subseteq\N^{k+1}$
% if $S$ is the set of $k$-tuples $\sequence{x_0,\ldots x_{k-1}}$ such that
% there is a $i\in\N$ so that $\sequence{i,x_0,\ldots x_{k-1}}\in T$.
% Prove that the projection of a computably enumerable set is also
% computably enumerable.
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}
Finish the proof of \cref{lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn} by showing that
the second and third items are equivalent.
\begin{answer}\verified
Let $S$ be the domain of a partial computable function~$f$.
We will produce a partial computable function~$g$ whose range is~$S$
by dovetailing.

First run the computation of~$f(0)$ for a step.
Then run the computation of~$f(0)$ for a second step
and the computation of~$f(1)$ for a step.
Next run the computation of~$f(0)$ for its third step,
run the computation of~$f(1)$ for its second step,
and run the computation of~$f(2)$ for a step.
Continue in this way.
Define $g(0)$ to be the first number~$i$ where the computation of~$f(i)$
halts, define $g(1)$ to be the next number~$j$ where the computation of~$f(j)$
halts, etc.
Clearly $g$ is partial computable and has range~$S$.

The other direction works much the same way.
We are given a partial computable~$g$ whose range is~$S$ and we will 
produce a partial computable~$f$ whose domain is~$S$ by
dovetailing computations of~$g$. 
First run~$g(0)$ for a step.
Then run the computation of~$g(0)$ for a second step
and the computation of~$g(1)$ for a step.
Next run~$g(0)$ for its third step,
$g(1)$ for its second step,
and~$g(2)$ for a step.
Continue in this way.
When a computation halts, $g(i)=k$, define $f(k)$ to have some nominal
value, such as~$42$.
Clearly $f$ is partial computable and has domain~$S$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{computably enumerable|)}\index{set!computably enumerable|)}












\section{Oracles}\index{oracle|(}\index{set!oracle|(} \label{sec:Oracles}
\margingraphic{0.18\textwidth}{background/pix/delphi.jpg}{\label{fig:yogi}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Collier_(painter)}{Priestess of Delphi (Collier 1891)}}
The problem of deciding whether a machine halts 
is so hard that it is unsolvable.
It this the absolutely hardest problem
or are there ones that are even harder?

% \margingraphic{0.23\textwidth}{background/pix/delphi.png}{\label{fig:yogi}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythia}{The oracle at Delphi}}
What does it mean to say that one problem is harder than another?
We have compared problem hardness already, 
for instance when we considered the problem
of whether a Turing machine halts on input~$3$.
There we proved that if we could solve the halts-on-$3$ problem
then we could solve the \HP.
That is, we proved that halts-on-$3$ is at least as hard as the \HP.
So, the idea is that
one problem is harder than a second
if solving the first would also give us a solution to the 
second.\footnote{%
  We can instead think that the first problem is more general than
  the second.
  For instance, the problem of inputting a natural number and
  outputting its prime factors is harder than the problem of
  inputting a natural and determining if it is divisible by seven.
  Clearly if we could solve the first then we could solve the second.}

Under Church's Thesis we interpret the unsolvability of the \HP{} to
say that no mechanism can answer all questions about
membership in~$K$.
So if we want to answer questions about sets that are harder than~$K$ then 
we need the answers to be supplied in some way
that
\citetext{won't be a physically-realizable discrete and deterministic
mechanism}{%
  Turing introduced oracles in his PhD thesis.
  He said, ``We shall not go any further into the nature of this oracle
  apart from saying that it cannot be a machine.''
  \autocite{TuringThesis}}.
Consequently, we posit a device,
an \definend{oracle},\index{oracle}
that we attach to the Turing machine box and
that acts as the characteristic function of a set. 
For example, to see what could be computed if we could solve
the \HP{} we can attach a $K$-oracle that
answers,
``Is $x\in K$?''
This oracle is a black box, meaning that we can't open it to 
see how it works.\footnote{%
  Opening it would
  let out the \citetext{magic smoke}{See \autocite{wiki:MagicSmoke}.}.}

\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{asy/share/machine01.pdf}
\end{center}

We could formally define computation with an oracle $X\subseteq\N$ by
extending the definition of Turing machines.
% One way is:~to the action
% symbols \str{L} and~\str{R} add one more, \str{O}
% (this is an `oh', not a `zero').
% When this symbol appears, where the read/write head is pointing to the
% leftmost \str{1} of a sequence of $n$-many \str{1}'s,
% if $n\in A$ then that sequence is replaced
% by a single~\str{1} followed by $n$-many \str{0}'s,
% while if $n\notin A$ then the sequence is replaced 
% by $n$-many \str{0}'s.
But \citetext{we will instead use Church's Thesis}{%
  For a full treatment see \autocite{Rogers}.}.
Imagine enhancing a programming language by introducing 
a Boolean function \codeinline{oracle}.
\begin{lstlisting}
(if (oracle(x))
    ... )
\end{lstlisting}
When $x\in X$
then this takes one branch, while if $x\notin X$ then it takes the other.

Most of what we have already developed about machines carries over.
For instance, program are strings, each program has an index,
and the index is source-equivalent, so that from an index we can compute the
program source
and from a source we can find the index.

In the setup above, the program code does not
change if we change the oracle\Dash
if we unplug the $X$ oracle box and replace
it with a~$Y$ oracle box
then the white box is unchanged.
Of course, the values returned by \codeinline{oracle(x)} may change,
resulting in changes to the outcome of running the program
with the oracle, the two-box system.
But the enhanced Turing machine stays the same.
Thus,
to specify a relative computation, in addition to specifying
which program we are using and which inputs,
we must also specify the oracle set.
This explains the notations for the
\definend{oracle Turing machine, $\TM_e^X$}\index{oracle Turing machine}\index{Turing machine!with oracle}
and for the outcome of the
\definend{function computed relative to an oracle, $\TMfcn^X_e(x)$}.\index{computation!relative to an oracle}

\begin{definition}
If a function computed from~$X$ is the characteristic function
of the set~$S$ then we say that 
\definend{$S$ is $X$-computable},\index{computable!relative to a set}
or that
$S$ is 
\definend{Turing reducible}\index{Turing reducible}\index{T reducible@$T$ reducible} 
to~$X$ or that $S$ \definend{reduces to}~$X$,\index{reduces to}\index{set!reduces to}
denoted \definend{$S\leq_T X$}.
\end{definition}

That is, $S\leq_T X$ if and only if $\TMfcn_e^X=\charfcn{S}$ for some $e\in\N$.
Think of the set $S$ as being a easier problem, or at least no harder, than~$X$.
For instance, where $E$ is the set of even numbers then $E\leq_TK$.
\begin{remark}
The terminology
`$S$ reduces to $X$' can at first seem reversed.
The idea is that we can solve problem~$S$ by
using a solution to~$X$.
This phrase also appears in other areas of Mathematics.
For instance, in Calculus 
we may say that finding the area under a
polynomial curve reduces to the problem of antidifferentiation.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}
\begin{thmparts}
\item A set is computable if and only if it is computable
relative to the empty set, or relative to any computable set.
\item \textsc{(Reflexivity)} Every set is computable from itself, $A\leq_T A$.
\item \textsc{(Transitivity)} If $A\leq_T B$ and $B\leq_T C$ then $A\leq_T C$.
\end{thmparts}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For the first, a set is computable if its characteristic function is computable.
If the characteristic function is computable without reference to an
oracle then it can be computed by an oracle machine, by ignoring the
oracle.
For the other direction, suppose that a characteristic function can be computed
by reference to the empty set or any other computable oracle.
Then it can be computed without reference to an oracle by replacing
the oracle calls with computations.

The second item is clear.
For the third, suppose that $\TM_e^B$ computes the characteristic
function of~$A$ and that
$\TM_{\hat{e}}^C$ computes the characteristic function of~$B$.
Then in the computation of $A$ from $B$ we can replace the
$B$-oracle calls with calls to $\TM_{\hat{e}}^C$.
That computes the characteristic function of $A$
directly from~$C$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
Recall the problem of determining, given~$e$, whether $\TM_e$ halts
on input~$3$.
It asks for a program that acts as the
characteristic function of the set
$A=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on~$3$}}$.
We will show that $K\leq_T A$.

This is a reprise of \cref{ex:HaltOnThree}.
There, we considerd the function $\map{\psi}{\N^2}{\N}$
that returns $42$ if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$ and that diverges otherwise.
Church's Thesis gave a Turing machine whose input-output behavior is $\psi$.
We let that machine have index~$e$, 
and applied the \smn{} theorem to parametrize~$x$, 
giving $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.
We finished by observing that $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$ if and only if
$\charfcn{A}(s(e,x))=1$, where $\charfcn{A}$ is the characteristic function.
That computes $K$ from an $A$~oracle.
\end{example}

\begin{lemma} \label{lem:ComputableOracleDoesNothing}
Any computable set, including $\emptyset$ or~$\N$,
is Turing reducible to any other set.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $C\subseteq\N$ be computable.
Then there is a Turing machine that computes 
the characteristic function of~$C$. 
Think of this as an oracle machine that never queries its oracle.
\end{proof}

The \HP{} is
to decide whether $\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$.
A person may perceive that  
a more natural problem is to decide whether $\TM_e$ halts on input~$x$.

\begin{definition} \index{K0, set of halting pairs@$K_0$, set of halting pairs}
  $\definend{K_0}=\set{\sequence{e,x} \suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input~$x$}}$
\end{definition}

However, we will argue that the two are equivalent,
that they 
are inter-solvable, meaning that if you can solve the one then
you can solve the other. 
Thus your choice is a 
matter of convenience and convention.\footnote{%  
  For the \HP{} definition we use $K$ 
  because it is the standard and because it has some technical advantages,
  including that it falls out of the diagonalization development done
  at the start of this subsection.}

\begin{definition}
Two sets $A,B$ are 
\definend{Turing equivalent}\index{Turing equivalent}\index{set!Turing equivalent} 
or 
\definend{$T$-equivalent},\index{T equivalent@$T$ equivalent}\index{set!T equivalent@$T$ equivalent} 
denoted \definend{$A\equiv_T B$}, if $A\leq_T B$
and $B\leq_T A$.
% For any set~$A$, the collection of sets Turing equivalent to $A$ is the
% \definend{Turing degree}\index{Turing degree}\index{set!Turing degree} 
% of~$A$.
% We denote
% the Turing degree of a set named~$A$ by using the 
% boldface lower case letter, here~\definend{$\textbf{a}$}.
\end{definition}

Showing that two sets are $T$-equivalent
shows that two seemingly-different problems are actually versions
of the same problem.
We will greatly expand on this approach in the chapter on Complexity.

\begin{theorem}
$K\equiv_T K_0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $K\leq_T K_0$ suppose that we have access to a $K_0$-oracle.
Since it can say whether $\TM_e$ halts on~$x$ for any input~$x$,
it can clearly say whether $\TM_e$ halts on~$e$.

For the $K_0\leq_T K$ half, 
% The problem we must solve is:~given a pair $\sequence{e,x}$,
% determine whether $\TM_e$ halts on input~$x$.
consider the flowchart on the left;
obviously this machine 
halts for all input triples exactly if $\sequence{e,x}\in K_0$.
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine implementing
it; let it be machine~$\TM_{\hat{e}}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp03.pdf}
  \hspace{2em plus 0.5fil}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp04.pdf}
\end{center}
Get the 
flowchart on the right by applying the \smn{} theorem
to parametrize $e$ and~$x$
(that is, this is a sketch of machine $\TM_{s(\hat{e},e,x)}$). 
That flowchart represents a family of machines, 
one for each pair $\protect\sequence{e,x}$.

Now suppose that we are given a pair $\sequence{e,x}$ and consider the 
right-side flowchart % $\TM_{s(\hat{e},e,x)}$ 
for that pair.
It either halts on all inputs~$y$ 
or fails to halt on all inputs, depending on whether $\phi_e(x)\converges$.
In particular, taking the input to be the number of this machine
$y=s(\hat{e},e,x)$, we have that
$\TM_{s(\hat{e},e,x)}$ halts on input $s(\hat{e},e,x)$
if and only if $\TMfcn_e(x)\converges$.
So give a question about membership in $K_0$,
about whether $\sequence{e,x}\in K_0$, we can answer it by 
determining whether $s(\hat{e},e,x)\in K$.  
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
The \HP{} is at least as hard as any computably enumerable
problem:~$W_e\leq_T K$ for all~$e\in\N$. 
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \cref{lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}
the computably enumerable sets are the columns of $K_0$.
\begin{equation*}
  W_e
  =\set{y\suchthat \TMfcn_e(y)\converges}
  =\set{y\suchthat \sequence{e,y}\in K_0}
\end{equation*}
So $W_e\leq_T K_0\equiv_T K$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
The Relativized \HP{},
the problem of determining membership in
$K^K=\set{x\suchthat \TMfcn_x^K(x)\converges}$,
is not computable from a $K$ oracle.
Thus, there is no~$e\in\N$ such that $\TMfcn_e^K$ is the 
characteristic function of $K^K$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
This is an adaptation of the proof that the \HP{} is unsolvable.
Assume otherwise, that there is a mechanical computation relative to a
$K$~oracle that acts as the characteristic function of $K^K$.
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_e^K(x)=
  \begin{cases}
  1  &\case{if $\TMfcn^K_x(x)\converges$}  \\
  0  &\case{otherwise} 
  \end{cases}
  \tag{$*$}
\end{equation*}
Then the function below is also computable relative to a $K$~oracle.
The flowchart illustrates its construction;
it uses the above function for the branch.
\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.3\textwidth}
  \begin{equation*}
  f^K(x)=
    \begin{cases}
    42    &\case{if $\TMfcn^K_x(x)\diverges$}  \\
    \uparrow  &\case{if $\TMfcn^K_x(x)\converges$}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
\end{minipage}
\hspace{2.5em}
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts03.pdf}}
\end{center}
Since $f$ is computable, it has an index.
Let that index be~$\hat{e}$, so that
$f^K=\TMfcn^K_{\hat{e}}$.

Now feed $f$ its own index\Dash consider
$f^K(\hat{e})=\TMfcn^K_{\hat{e}}(\hat{e})$.
If that diverges then the first clause in the definition of~$f$
gives that $f^K(\hat{e})\converges$,
which is a contradiction.
If it converges then $f$'s second clause 
gives $f^K(\hat{e})\diverges$, which is also impossible.
Either way, assuming that the function in~($*$) can be 
computed relative to a $K$~oracle leads to a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
Any set~$S$ is reducible to its relativized \HP{}, $S\leq_T K^S$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The flowchart on the left sketches a function that is intuitively
mechanically computable relative to an oracle~$X$.
So Church's Thesis gives that it is $\TM_e^X$ for some index~$e$.
Apply the \smn{} theorem to parametrize~$x$, giving the
uniformily computable family of
machines $\TM_{s(e,x)}^X$ charted on the right.
\begin{center}
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts04.pdf}}
\hspace{3em}
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts05.pdf}}
\end{center}
On the right 
$\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}^X(y)\converges$ if and only if $x\in X$.
Taking the oracle to be~$S$ and the input to be $s(e,x)$ gives that
$x\in S$ if and only if $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}^S(s(e,x))\converges$,
which holds if and only if $s(e,x)\in K^S$.
\end{proof}


\begin{corollary}
$K\leq_T K^K$, but $K^K\not\leq_T K$
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
This follows from the prior two results.
\end{proof}

That answers the question posed at the start of this section.
One problem strictly harder than the \HP{}
is to compute the characteristic function
of~$K^K$.



\begin{exercises}[Recall from page~\pageref{def:ComputableFcn} that a
  Turing machine is a decider for a set if it computes the characteristic
  function of that set.]

\recommended
\begin{exercise} 
\begin{credit}
  \url{http://people.cs.aau.dk/~srba/courses/tutorials-CC-10/t5-sol.pdf}
\end{credit}
Suppose that the set $A$ is Turing-reducible to the set~$B$. 
Which of these are true?
\begin{exparts}
\item A decider for $A$ can be used to decide~$B$.
\item If $A$ is computable then~$B$ is computable also.
\item If $A$ is uncomputable then $B$ is uncomputable too.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item This is wrong.
  For instance, the empty set is reducible to the \HP{} set, 
  $\emptyset\leq_T K$, but while a decider for 
  $\emptyset$ is trivial, there is no computable decider for~$K$.

  The correct statement is the opposite:~if $A\leq_T B$ then 
  a decider for~$B$ can be used to decide the set~$A$.
  \item This is wrong. 
  As in the prior item, if $A$ is the empty set then it is computable.
  Then $A\leq_TB$ where $B$ equals the \HP{} set~$K$,
  but $B$ is not decidable.
  The other way around is right:~if $B$ is decidable then $A$ is decidable also.
  \item True.
  If $A\leq_T B$ then $\TMfcn_e^B=\charfcn{A}$ for some index~$e\in\N$.
  If we could compute~$B$ then we could use that ability to compute~$A$.
  So if~$A$ is uncomputable then~$B$ is also uncomputable.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Both oracles and deciders take in a number and return, $0$ or~$1$, whether 
that number is in the set.
What's the difference?
\begin{answer}\verified
  % A decider is computable.
  % An oracle, typically, is not.
  A decider is a Turing machine 
  (whose computed function is the characteristic function of some set).
  An oracle is an addition to a Turing machine, a set that the machine 
  can query.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \href{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/40605/67754}{SE user Karolis Juodel\.{e}}
\end{credit}
Your friend says, ``Oracle machines are not real, so why talk about them?''
What do you say?
\begin{answer}\verified
  First of all, they are fascinating and fun.
  Is that not enough?

  Beyond that, they help us understand the relationships among problems.
  In the Complexity chapter we will expand on the idea to help understand
  how many everyday, practical, problems are related.
  This will include trying to understand which are easy and which are
  hard. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Your classmate says they answered a quiz question to define an oracle with,
``A set to solve unsolvable problems.''
Give them a gentle critique.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Their answer captures the spirit of the reason for considering oracle 
  computations.
  But it is lacking in two ways.
  The first is a technicality:~there are oracles such as the
  empty set, where computations relative to that oracle can only solve 
  problems that you could solve already.

  The second lack is that their answer doesn't refer to the mechanism at all.
  That is, the oracle set doesn't solve the problems.
  We might informally speak of an oracle as solving problems but for a 
  definition we should say that
  we give a machine access to answers about membership in the set and then
  the input-output behavior of the machine is the solution to those problems. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is there an oracle for every problem?
For every problem is there an oracle?
\begin{answer}\verified
  We take the first question to ask:~is there a most-powerful oracle, 
  one that solves every problem?
  The answer is no.
  Given an oracle~$X\subseteq \N$, the problem of deciding membership
  in $K^X$, the \HP{} in that oracle, is not solvable from~$X$.

  The second question asks whether, given a set~$S\subseteq N$,
  there is an oracle that suffices to determine membership in~$S$. 
  The answer is yes;~the oracle~$S$ will do.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \href{https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/96806/67754}{SE user Noah Schweber}
\end{credit}
There is this person in your class who keeps mouthing off
and your professor has to keep gently setting them straight.
This time its, ``Oracles can solve unsolvable problems, right?
And $K^K$ is unsolvable, right?
So an oracle like the~$K$ oracle should solve it.''
Help your prof out here.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Each oracle can only compute answers to some problems.
  But for each oracle there are problems that it cannot solve. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is the number of oracles countable or uncountable?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Any subset of~$\N$ can be an oracle, so there are uncountably many oracles. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Prove that $A\leq_T \setcomp{A}$ for all $A\subseteq\N$.
\begin{answer}\verified
Consider a machine with oracle~$X$ that takes input~$x$, 
accesses the oracle to determine if $x\in X$,
and then prints out the opposite.
So if it finds that $x\in X$ then it prints \str{0}, while if it finds that
$x\notin X$ then it prints~\str{1}.
This machine will, given oracle~$A$, compute the characteristic
function of~$\setcomp{A}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Your study partner confesses, ``I don't understand relative computation.
Any computation using an oracle must make only finitely many 
\codeinline{oracle} calls if it halts.
But a finite oracle is computable, and so by
\cref{lem:ComputableOracleDoesNothing} it is reducible to any set.''
Help them out.
\begin{answer}\verified
  In an oracle computation that halts, there are indeed only finitely many
  calls to the oracle.
  But the calls when the computation's input is~$0$ 
  may well differ from the calls when
  the computation's input is~$1$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let $A$ and~$B$ be sets.
Show that if $A(q)=B(q)$ for all $q\in\N$ used in the oracle computation
$\TMfcn^A(x)$
then $\TMfcn^A(x)=\TMfcn^B(x)$.
\begin{answer}\verified
The computation will proceed in exactly the same way, so it will come
to the same result.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that $K\nleq_T\emptyset$.
\begin{answer}\verified
If $K\leq_T\emptyset$ were true then
there would be an oracle machine whose computed function~$\TMfcn^\emptyset$
is the characteristic function of~$K$.
But because membership in~$\emptyset$ is itself computable we could 
replace oracle calls with the computation itself, so that any 
time the machine asks if $x$ is an element of the oracle, we replace
that with `no'.
That mechanically computes~$K$, which is impossible.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that the \HP{} set~$K$ reduces to each.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{x\suchthat \text{$\TM_x$ outputs a~$7$ for some input}}$
\item $\set{x\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_x(y)=2y$ for all input}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  We can do these just like the ones in the \HP{} section.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Let $A=\set{x\suchthat \text{$\TM_x$ outputs a~$7$ for some input}}$.
  Consider this function.
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      7         &\case{if $\TM_x$ halts on input~$x$}  \\
      \uparrow  &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1019.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1020.pdf}}
    \caption{Sketch of the machine for the function $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the family of machines for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:MachinesOutputSeven}
  \end{figure}
  We can produce this behavior with 
  a program that takes two inputs, $x,y\in\N$, simulates
  running $\TM_x$ on input~$x$, prints~$7$, and then stops.
  See \figureref{fig:MachinesOutputSeven}.
  Then by Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine that does that, so 
  fix an index~$e$ where $\TMfcn_e=\psi$.

  Apply the \smn~Theorem to get a family of functions $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$, 
  each of which 
  takes in one input~$y$ and outputs~$7$ if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$, or
  diverges otherwise.
  Then $x\in K$ if and only if $s(e,x)\in A$, and so $K\leq_T A$.  
  \item Let $D=\set{x\suchthat  \text{$\TMfcn_x(y)=2y$ for all inputs~$y$}}$.
  Consider this function.
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      2y         &\case{if $\TM_x$ halts on input~$x$}  \\
      \uparrow  &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1021.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1022.pdf}}
    \caption{The machine for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the family of machines for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:MachinesDoubler}
  \end{figure}
  We can produce this behavior with 
  a program that takes two inputs, $x,y\in\N$, simulates
  running $\TM_x$ on input~$x$, prints~$2y$, and then stops.
  See \figureref{fig:MachinesDoubler}.
  So Church's Thesis gives that there is a Turing machine, with 
  an index~$e$, so that $\TMfcn_e=\psi$.

  Apply the \smn~Theorem to get a family of functions $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$ that
  take in one input~$y$ and output~$2y$ if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$, or
  diverges otherwise.
  Then $x\in K$ if and only if $s(e,x)\in D$, and so $K\leq_T D$.  
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let $A$ and~$B$ be sets.
Produce a set~$C$ so that $A\leq_T C$ and $B\leq_T C$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  One is 
  $C=\set{\cantor(0,a)\suchthat a\in A}
      \cup\set{\cantor(1,b)\suchthat b\in B}$.
  Clearly from this set we can answer questions about membership in 
  each of~$A$ and~$B$. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Fix an oracle.
Prove that the collection of sets computable from that oracle is countable.
\begin{answer}\verified
  There are only countably many programs that can involve an
  \codeinline{oracle} call.
  That is, there are only countably many oracle-enhanced Turing machines.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The relation $\leq_T$ involves sets so we naturally ask how it interacts
with set operations.
\begin{exparts}
\item Does $A\subseteq B$ imply $A\leq_T B$?
\item Is $A\leq_T A\cup B$?
\item Is $A\leq_T A\cap B$?
\item Is $A\leq_T \setcomp{A}$?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item No.
    The \HP{} set~$K$ is a subset of the natural numbers~$\N$
    but $K\nleq_T\N$, because the \HP{} is unsolvable.
  \item No.
    The \HP{} set $K$ is not Turing reducible to~$\N$, 
    but $\N=K\cup\setcomp{K}$.
  \item No.
    The \HP{} set $K$ is not Turing reducible to~$\emptyset$, 
    but $\emptyset=K\cap\setcomp{K}$.
  \item Yes.
    We can write this oracle-using program:~read the input~$x$
    and if \codeinline{oracle(x)} then output~$0$, otherwise
    output~$1$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let $A\subseteq\N$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Define when a set is \definend{computably enumerable in an oracle}.\index{computably enumerable!in an oracle}\index{oracle!computably enumerable in}
\item
Show that $\N$ is computably enumerable in $A$ for all sets~$A$.
\item
Show that $K^A$ is computably enumerable in~$A$.
% \item
% Show that the relation $B\sim A$ if $B$ is computably enumerable in~$A$
% is not transtive.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item The natural definition is that a set~$B$ is 
    \definend{computably enumerable} in the set~$A$ if $B$~is the 
    range of a total $A$-computable function, or if $B$ is empty.
    That is, $B$ is c.e.\@ in $A$ if
    $B=\set{\TMfcn_e^A(x)\suchthat x\in\N}$ for some index~$e$ where the
    function is total, or if $B=\emptyset$.
  \item As in \cref{lem:ComputableOracleDoesNothing}, we can output the 
    elements of~$\N$ without even referring to the oracle.
    The function $\TMfcn^A(x)=x$ is computable, and does not need to 
    ever refer to the oracle.
  \item Dovetail.
    First run the computation of $\TM_0^A$ on input~$0$ for one step.
    Then run the computation of $\TM_0^A$ on input~$0$ for a second step,
    and the computation of $\TM_1^A$ on input~$1$ for one step.
    Next run the computation of $\TM_0^A$ on input~$0$ for its third step,
    the computation of $\TM_1^A$ on input~$1$ for its second step,
    and the computation of $\TM_2^A$ on input~$2$ for one step.
    As computations halt, enumerate the index into $K^A$.
  % \item
  % Hint: use $\emptyset$, $K$, and $\setcomp{K}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
% Instead, define re in A, and prove that if S is re in A and S comp
% is re in A then S is recursive in A?

% \begin{exercise}
% Prove that
%   $\text{Tot}=\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TMfcn_e(y)\converges$ for all $y$}}$
% is $T$-equivalent to $K^K$.
% \end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{oracle|)}\index{set!oracle|)}




% =====================================================
\section{Fixed point theorem}\index{Fixed point theorem|(}

%<*discussion:RecallCantorThm>
Recall our first example of diagonalization,\index{diagonalization} 
the proof that the
set of real numbers is not countable, on page~\pageref{th:RealsNotCountable}.
We assume that there is an $\map{f}{\N}{\R}$
and consider its inputs and outputs, as illustrated in this table.
\begin{equation*} \setlength{\arraycolsep}{0.75\arraycolsep}
  \begin{array}{c|r@{\hspace*{.35em}.\hspace*{.35em}}ccccccc@{\hspace*{1em}\ldots\;}}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\text{\small $n$\hspace*{0.25em}}} 
    &\multicolumn{8}{c}{\text{\hspace*{-1.00em}\small\tabulartext{$f(n)$'s decimal expansion}}}  \\ 
   \cline{1-9}
   \rule{0em}{2.15ex}% bit more vertical room 
   0  &42  &3  &1  &2  &7  &7  &0  &4  \\  
   1  &2   &0  &1  &0  &0  &0  &0  &0  \\  
   2  &1   &4  &1  &4  &1  &5  &9  &2  \\  
   3  &-20  &9  &1  &9  &5  &9  &1  &9  \\  
   % 4  &  0 &1  &0  &1  &0  &0  &1  &0  \\
   % 5  &-0  &6   &2  &5  &5  &4  &1  &8  \\[-.5ex]
   \multicolumn{1}{c}{\vdotswithin{$0$}} 
    &\multicolumn{4}{c}{\vdotswithin{$1$}}  
  \end{array}
\end{equation*}
Let a decimal representation of the number on row~$n$ be 
$d_n=\hat{d}.d_{n,0}\mskip 1mu d_{n,1}\mskip 1mu d_{n,2}\ldots$ % 
Go down the diagonal to the right of the decimal point to get 
the sequence of digits $\sequence{d_{0,0}, d_{1,1}, d_{2,2}, \ldots}$.
With that sequence, 
construct a number~$z=0.z_0\mskip 1mu z_1\mskip 1mu z_2\ldots$
by making its $n$-th decimal place be something other than~$d_{n,n}$.
In our example we took a transformation~$t$ of digits
given by~$t(d_{n,n})=2$ if $d_{n,n}=1$, and $t(d_{n,n})=1$
otherwise, so that the table above gives $z=0.1211\ldots$\sentencespace
Then the diagonalization argument culminates in verifying that~$z$ 
is not any of the rows. 
%</discussion:RecallCantorThm>



\subsection{When diagonalization fails}\index{diagonalization}
%<*discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFails>
But what if the transformed diagonal is a row, $z=f(n_0)$? 
Then the member of the array where the diagonal crosses that row is
unchanged by the transformation, 
$d_{n_0,n_0}=t(d_{n_0,n_0})$.
Conclusion:~if diagonalization fails then the transformation has
a fixed point.
%</discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFails>

We will apply this to sequences of
computable functions,
$\TMfcn_{i_0},\TMfcn_{i_1},\TMfcn_{i_2},\ldots$\sentencespace
We are interested in effectiveness so we don't consider
arbitrary sequences of indices but instead take the indices to be computable,
$i_0,i_1,i_2\ldots=\TMfcn_e(0),\TMfcn_e(1),\TMfcn_e(2)\ldots$
for some~$e$.
So a sequence of computable functions has this form.
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_{\phi_e(0)},\,\TMfcn_{\phi_e(1)},\,\TMfcn_{\phi_e(2)}\,\ldots
\end{equation*}
%<*discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsii>
Below is a table with all such sequences, that is,
all effective sequences of effective functions, $\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_e(n)}$.
\begin{center}\small \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
  \begin{tabular}{rr|ccccc}
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
    &\multicolumn{4}{c}{\tabulartext{Sequence term}}  \\
    &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
      &$n=0$ &$n=1$ &$n=2$ &$n=3$ &\ldots \\  \cline{3-7}
   \multirow{5}{*}{\tabulartext{Sequence}}
    &$e=0$ &$\phi_{\phi_0(0)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_0(1)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_0(2)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_0(3)}$  &\ldots \\
    &$e=1$ &$\phi_{\phi_1(0)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_1(1)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_1(2)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_1(3)}$  &\ldots \\
    &$e=2$ &$\phi_{\phi_2(0)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_2(1)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_2(2)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_2(3)}$  &\ldots \\
    &$e=3$ &$\phi_{\phi_3(0)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_3(1)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_3(2)}$ &$\phi_{\phi_3(3)}$  &    \\[-.5ex]
    &$\mathrel{\makebox[\widthof{$e=3$}]{\vdots}}$
      &$\mathrel{\makebox[\widthof{=}]{\vdots}}$
      &$\mathrel{\makebox[\widthof{=}]{\vdots}}$
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
Each entry $\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_e(n)}$ is a computable function.
If $\TMfcn_e(n)$ diverges then the function as whole diverges. 

The natural transformation is to use a computable function~$f$\!. 
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_x\mapsunder{t_f}\TMfcn_{f(x)}
\end{equation*}
The next result shows that under this transformation
diagonalization fails
% (because the transformed diagonal is
% a computable sequence of computable functions)
and thus the transformation~$t_f$ has a fixed point.
%</discussion:WhenDiagonalizationFailsii>

\begin{theorem}[Fixed Point Theorem, Kleene 1938]\hspace*{-0.5em}\footnotemark[1]  \label{th:FixedPointThm}
%<*th:FixedPointThm>
\index{Fixed point theorem}\index{Kleene's fixed point theorem}\index{Recursion theorem}
For any total computable function~$f$
there is a number~$k$ such that $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{f(k)}$.
%</th:FixedPointThm>
\end{theorem}\footnotetext[1]{%
  This is also known as the Recursion Theorem
  but there is another widely used result of that name.
  This name is more descriptive so we'll go with it.}
\begin{proof}
%<*pf:FixedPointThmi>
The array diagonal is
$\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_0(0)},\,\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_1(1)},\,\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_2(2)}\,\ldots$\sentencespace
The flowchart on the left below is a sketch of a function
$f(e,x)=\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_e(e)}(x)$.
Church's Thesis says that some Turing machine computes this function; let that
machine have index~$e_0$.
Apply the \smn{} theorem to parametrize~$e$ in the right chart, 
which describes the family of 
machines that compute $\TMfcn_{s(e_0,e)}$,
the $e$-th function on the diagonal.
%</pf:FixedPointThmi>
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts06.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts07.pdf}}
  \hspace{1em}
  \TMfcn_{s(e_0,e)}(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    \TMfcn_{\TMfcn_e(e)}(x)  &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$} \\
    \uparrow                &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
%<*pf:FixedPointThmii>
The index~$e_0$ is fixed so $s(e_0,e)$ is a function of one variable.
Let $g(e)=s(e_0,e)$, so that the diagonal functions are $\TMfcn_{g(e)}$.
This function $g$ is computable and total.

Under~$t_f$ those functions are transformed to
% $\TMfcn_{f\TMfcn_0(0)},\,\TMfcn_{f\TMfcn_1(1)},\,\TMfcn_{f\TMfcn_2(2)},\>\ldots$\sentencespace
$\TMfcn_{fg(0)},\,\TMfcn_{fg(1)},\,\TMfcn_{fg(2)},\>\ldots$\sentencespace
The composition $\composed{f}{g}$ is computable and total, since
$f$ is specified as total.
%</pf:FixedPointThmii>
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_{fg(e)}\;(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    \TMfcn_{f\TMfcn_e(e)}(x)   &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$}     \\
    \uparrow                 &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts08.pdf}}
\end{equation*}
%<*pf:FixedPointThmiii>
As the flowchart underlines, this is a computable sequence of
computable functions.
Hence it is one of the table's rows.
Let it be row~$v$, so that $\TMfcn_{fg(n)}=\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_{v}(n)}$ for all~$n$.
Consider where the diagonal sequence $\TMfcn_{g(e)}$ intersescts that row:
% \begin{equation*}
  $\TMfcn_{g(v)}=\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_v(v)}=\TMfcn_{fg(v)}$.
% \end{equation*}
The desired fixed point for $f$ is $k=g(v)$.
%</pf:FixedPointThmiii>
\end{proof}

So when we try to diagonalize out of the partial computable functions, we 
fail.
That is, 
\citetext{the notion of partial computable function seems to have an
in-built defense against diagonalization}{%
  \autocite{Odifreddi}, p~152.}.

The Fixed Point Theorem
applies to any total computable function.
Consequently, it leads to many surprising
results. 

\begin{corollary}
There is an index~$e$ so that $\TMfcn_e=\TMfcn_{e+1}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The function~$f(x)=x+1$ is computable and total.
So there is an $e\in\N$ such that $\TMfcn_{e}=\TMfcn_{f(e)}$.
\end{proof}

%<*cor:WeEqualsSete>
\begin{corollary} \label{cor:ExistsPhiNWithRanN}
There is an index~$e$ such that $\TMfcn_e$ halts only on $e$.
\end{corollary}
%</cor:WeEqualsSete>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:WeEqualsSetei>
Consider the program described by the flowchart on the left.
By Church's Thesis it can be done with a Turing machine,
$\TM_{e_0}$.
Parametrize to get the program on the right,
$\TM_{s(e_0,m)}$.
%</pf:WeEqualsSetei>
\begin{equation*}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts09.pdf}}
  \hspace{1em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts10.pdf}}
  \hspace{1em}
  \TMfcn_{s(e_o,m)}(x)=
  \begin{cases}
  42         &\case{if $x=m$}  \\
  \uparrow  &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
%<*pf:WeEqualsSeteii>
Since $e_0$ is fixed (it is the index of the machine sketched on the left),
this is a total computable function~$f$ of one variable
$f(m)=s(e_0,m)$ that halts only on elements in~$\set{m}$.
The Fixed Point Theorem gives a fixed point~$m=e$, so
the set of inputs on which $\TMfcn_e$ halts is $\set{e}$.
%</pf:WeEqualsSeteii>
\end{proof}

This says that there is a Turing machine that halts
only on one input, its index.
Rephrased for rhetorical effect, 
\citetext{this machine's name is its behavior}{%
  \definend{Nominative determinism}
  is the theory that a person's name has some influence over what they do
  with their life.
  Examples are: the sprinter Usain Bolt,
  the US weatherman Storm Fields,
  the baseball player Prince Fielder.
  and the Lord Chief Justice of England and Wales named Igor Judge,
  I~Judge.}.\footnote{%
  Here, `name' is used as an equivalent of `index' 
  that is meant to be evocative.}

\begin{corollary}
There is an $m\in\N$ such that  
$\TMfcn_m(x)=m$ for all inputs~$x$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Consider the function $\psi(x,y)=x$.
As the flowchart on the left illustrates, it is computable.\footnote{% 
  In this argument perhaps 
  the flowchart is overkill since the function 
  is obviously computable.
  But when it is not obvious, as in the prior result and in  
  some of the exercises, we need an outline of how to compute the function.}
So by Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine that computes it.
Let that machine have index~$e$, so that $\psi(x,y)=\TMfcn_e(x,y)=x$.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts17.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts18.pdf}}
\end{center}
Apply the \smn{} theorem to get a
family of uniformily computable functions parametrized by~$x$,
given by $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=x$.
Because~$e$ is fixed, as it is the number of the Turing machine that 
computes $\psi$, define $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ by $g(x)=s(e,x)$.
This function is total.
The Fixed Point Theorem says that there is a~$m\in\N$ with 
$\TMfcn_m(y)=\TMfcn_{g(m)}(y)=\TMfcn_{s(e,m)}(y)=m$ for all~$y$.
\end{proof}

% We close with an intriguing result.

% \begin{corollary}
% There is a program that exactly outputs its own source code; there is a
% Turing machine that outputs itself.
% \end{corollary}

% \begin{proof}
% Define a rewriter function that outputs its input;
% call it~$\operatorname{echo}$.
% If given the input of the source code of a Turing machine $\TM$ then
% its output is the source of $\TM$.
% By the Fixed Point Theorem, there is some Turing machine
% $\TM$ such that $\TM$ has the same behavior as $\operatorname{echo}(\TM)$.
% But that behavior is to output the source code of $\TM$.
% So this machine outputs its own source.
% \end{proof}

% The prior result gives a Turing machine that prints its own index.
% Since the index is source-equivalent, it raises the question of whether there
% is a program that self-reproduces, that prints its own source.
% The answer is that there is such a program; see \cref{sec:SelfReproduction}.

\begin{remark}
Every Turing machine has some index number but 
here the index is related to its machine's behavior.
Imagine finding that in our numbering scheme, 
machine~$\TM_7$ outputs~$7$ on all inputs.
This may seem to be an accident of the choice of scheme.
But it isn't an accident; the 
corollary says that something like 
this must happen for any acceptable numbering.

The Fixed Point Theorem is deep, 
showing surprising and interesting behaviors that
occur in any sufficiently powerful computation system.
For instance, since a Turing machine's index is source-equivalent, 
the prior result raises the question of whether there
is a program that prints its own source, that self-reproduces.
In addition to the discussion below, \cref{sec:SelfReproduction} has more.
\end{remark}




% ...........
\subsection{Discussion}\index{Fixed point theorem!discussion|(}
The Fixed Point Theorem and its proof are often 
\citetext{considered mysterious, or at any rate obscure}{%
  For example, ``The recursion theorem \ldots{} has one of the most
  unintuitive proofs where I cannot explain why it works, only that it
  does.'' \autocite{blog:ComputationalComplexityRecursionThm}}.
Here we will expand on a few points.

One aspect that bears explication is how it employs
the use-mention distinction.\index{use-mention distinction}
Compare the sentence \textit{Atlantis is a mythical city}  
to 
\textit{There are  two a's in ``Atlantis''}.
In the first, we say that `Atlantis' is
\definend{used} because it has a value, it points to something. 
In the second sentence `Atlantis' is not referring to something\Dash its
value is itself\Dash so 
we say that it is \definend{mentioned}.\footnote{%
  A version of this comes up in programming books.
  If such a book has the sentence, ``The number of players is \texttt{players}''
  then the first `players' refers to people while the second is a variable from
  the program.
  There the computer code is in a typewriter font, as is a standard
  practice, because
  quoting would be awkward and ugly.}
  % We have done something like the same thing with
  % the paragraph's single-quoted ``\,`Atlantis'\,'' mentions. 
  % (This is quoted because it mentions a mention.)}
% This difference is the reason for the second sentence's
% quotation marks.

\margingraphic{0.30\textwidth}{background/pix/xkcdpointers.png}{\label{fig:xkcdpointers}\href{https://xkcd.com/138/}{Courtesy \url{xkcd.com}}}
A version of the use-mention distinction happens in computer programming,
with pointers.\index{pointer, in C}
The C language program below illustrates.
The second line's 
asterisk means that \codeinline{x} and~\codeinline{y} are pointers.
While the compiler associates
\codeinline{x} and \codeinline{y} with memory cells, 
we are not so much interested in the contents of these cells
as in the contents of the memory cells that they name.
The first diagram
imagines that the compiler happens to associate~\codeinline{x}
with memory address~$123$ and~\codeinline{y} with~$124$.
It further imagines that the contents of cell~$123$ is the number~$901$
and the contents of cell~$124$ is~$902$.
We say that \codeinline{x} points to $901$ and \codeinline{y} points to~$902$.

The second diagram in the sequence shows the code running.
Because of the \codeinline{*x = 42}, the system 
puts $42$ where \codeinline{x} points:~it does not put $42$ in location~$123$, 
rather it
puts $42$ in the location referred to by the contents of~$123$,
namely, cell~$901$.\footnote{%
  Using the \codeinline{*} operator to access the value stored at a pointer
  is called dereferencing that pointer.
  There is a matching referencing operator, \codeinline{&},
  that gives the address of an existing variable.
  % Both allow a programmer to control the indirection that pointers provide.
  }
Then the code sets \codeinline{y} to point to the same
address as~\codeinline{x}, address~$901$.
Finally, it puts $13$ where \codeinline{y} points, which is at this moment
the same cell to which~\codeinline{x} points.
\begin{animatedfigure}{Pointers in a C program.}
\vcenteredhbox{\animategraphics[poster=last]{1}{background/asy/memory/memory}{00}{04}}
\end{animatedfigure}
\noindent The \codeinline{x} and~\codeinline{y} variables are being
considered at different levels of meaning than ordinary variables.
On one level,
\codeinline{x} refers to the contents of $123$,
while on another level it is about the contents of those contents,
what's in address~$901$.

As to the role played by the use-mention distinction in the
Fixed Point Theorem, 
the proof starts by taking 
$g(e)$ to be the name of this procedure.
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_{g(e)}(x)=
  \TMfcn_{s(e_0,e)}(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    \TMfcn_{\TMfcn_e(e)}(x)  &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$}  \\
    \uparrow                &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Don't be fooled by the notation; it is not the case that 
$g(e)$ equals $\TMfcn_e(e)$ but instead $g(e)$ is an index of the 
flowchart on the right in the proof, describing the procedure that
computes the function above.
Regardless of whether 
$\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$, we can nonetheless compute the index~$g(n)$
and from it the instructions for the function.
There is an analogy here with Atlantis\Dash despite that the referred-to
city doesn't exist we
can still sensibly assert things about its name.
  
Informally, what $g(e)$ names is,
``Given input~$x$, run $\TM_e$ on input~$e$ and if it halts with
output~$w$ then run $\TM_w$ on inpue~$x$.''
Shorter:~``Produce $\TMfcn_e(e)$ and then do $\TMfcn_e(e)$.''

Next, from~$f$ we consider the composition and give it a name
$\composed{f}{g}=\TMfcn_v$.
Substituting $v$ into the prior paragraph gives that $g(v)$ names, 
``Compute $\TMfcn_v(v)$ and then do $\TMfcn_v(v)$.''
That's the same as
``Compute $\composed{f}{g}\,(v)$ and then do $\composed{f}{g}\,(v)$.''
Note the self-reference; it may naively appear that to compute $g(v)$ we need 
to compute~$g(v)$,
that the instructions for $g(v)$ paradoxically
contains itself as a subpart.

Then $g(v)$ first computes the name of $\composed{f}{g}\,(v)$ and after that
runs the machine numbered $\composed{f}{g}\,(v)$.
So $g(v)$ and $\composed{f}{g}\,(v)$ are names for machines that
compute the same function.
Thus $g(v)$ does not contain itself; more precisely, the set of instructions for
computing $g(v)$ does not contain itself.
Instead, it contains a name for the instructions for computing itself.
\index{Fixed point theorem!discussion|)}

% ................................................
\begin{exercises}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Your friend asks you about the proof of the Fixed Point Theorem, 
\cref{th:FixedPointThm}.
``The last line says $\TMfcn_{g(v)}=\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_v(v)}$; isn't this just
saying that  $g(v)=\TMfcn_v(v)$?
Why the circumlocution?''
Help them out.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Saying that the two are the same function,  
  that $\TMfcn_{g(v)}=\TMfcn_{\TMfcn_v(v)}$, is very different than
  saying that the indices are the same,
  that  $g(v)=\TMfcn_v(v)$.
  (That is, we can easily write two different programs to compute the same
  function.)
  In particular, the Padding Lemma, \cref{lem:EveryCompFcnHasInfManyIndices}
  on page~\pageref{lem:EveryCompFcnHasInfManyIndices},
  says that every computable function (including the partial functions) has many
  indices.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show each.
\begin{exparts*}
\item There is an index~$e$ such that $\TMfcn_e=\TMfcn_{e+7}$.
\item There is an~$e$ such that $\TMfcn_e=\TMfcn_{2e}$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Apply the Fixed Point Theorem to the computable function~$f(x)=x+7$.
  \item Apply the Fixed Point Theorem to~$f(x)=2x$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
What conclusion can you draw about acceptable enumerations of Turing machines
by applying the Fixed Point Theorem to each of these?
\begin{exparts*}
\item the tripling function $x\mapsto 3x$
% \item the adds-five function $x\mapsto x+5$
\item the squaring function $x\mapsto x^2$
\item the function that gives $0$ except for~$x=5$,
  when it gives~$1$
\item the constant function $x\mapsto 42$  
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item No matter what numbering scheme we use for Turing machines,
  if it is acceptable then there is a Turing machine that computes the
  same function as the machine with twice its index; that is, there exists a
  $k\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{3k}$.
  % \item No matter what numbering scheme we use,
  % as long as it is acceptable, there is a Turing machine that computes the
  % same function as the machine whose index is five more, 
  % so that there exists
  % $k\in\N$ with $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{k+5}$.
  \item For any acceptable numbering scheme,
  there is a Turing machine that computes the
  same function as the machine whose index is the square.
  That is, there exists a
  $k\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{k^2}$.
  \item Consider this $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$.
  \begin{equation*}
    f(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      1  &\case{if $x=5$}  \\
      0  &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  For any acceptable numbering for Turing machines
  there will be a
  $k\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{f(k)}$.
  \item
  If the function is $f(x)=42$ then the Fixed Point Theorem gives
  that for any acceptable numbering there is a $k\in\N$ so that 
  $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{f(k)}=\TMfcn_{42}$.
  The number $k=42$ comes to mind, but the Padding Lemma shows that there are
  infinitely many such indices~$k$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \autocite{Rogers}, p~214.
\end{credit} 
We will prove that there is an~$m$ such that 
$W_m=\set{x\suchthat \TMfcn_m(x)\converges}=\set{m^2}$. 
\begin{exparts}
\item You want to show that there is a uniformily computable family of 
  functions like this.
  \begin{equation*}
    \TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=x^2$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}  
  \end{equation*}
  Define a suitable $\map{\psi}{\N^2}{\N}$, argue that it is intuitively
  mechanically computable, and apply the \smn{} Theorem to get the family
  of $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$. 
\item Observe that $e$ is fixed so that $s(e,x)$ is a function of one variable
  only, and name that function~$\map{g}{\N}{\N}$.
\item Apply the Fixed Point Theorem to get the desired~$m$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
  \item
  Consider the function on the left and observe that it is computed 
  by the program sketched on the left of
  \figureref{fig:WeContainsOnlyESquared}.
  Church's Thesis gives there is a Turing machine with this behavior.
  Let that machine's index be~$e$. 
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=x^2$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=x^2$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1027.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1028.pdf}}
    \caption{Flowcharts sketching the machine for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:WeContainsOnlyESquared}
  \end{figure}%
  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to get the family of functions on the right.
  \item  The number~$e$ is fixed because it is the index of the Turing machine
  that we chose to compute~$\psi$.
  So, define $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ by $g(x)=s(e,x)$.
  \item 
  The Fixed Point Theorem gives a $m\in\N$ with 
  $\TMfcn_m=\TMfcn_{g(m)}=\TMfcn_{s(e,m)}$. 
  Thus $W_m=\set{m^2}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
We will show there is an index~$m$ so that 
$W_m=\set{y\suchthat \TMfcn_m(y)\converges}$ is the set consisting
of one element, the $m$-th prime number.
\begin{exparts}
\item Argue that $\map{p}{\N}{\N}$ such that $p(x)$ is the
  $x$-th prime is computable.
\item Use it and the \smn{} Theorem to get that this family of 
  functions is uniformily computable: $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=42$ if 
  $y=p(x)$ and diverges otherwise.
  % \begin{equation*}
  %     \TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=
  %   \begin{cases}
  %     42       &\case{if $y=p(x)$}  \\
  %     \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
  %   \end{cases}
  % \end{equation*}
  \item Draw the desired conclusion.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item 
  The function $\map{p}{\N}{\N}$ such that $p(x)$ is the $x$-th prime number 
  is computable by Church's Thesis.
  \item
  We want to get the family of functions on the right side 
  below, computed by the machines sketched on the right of 
  \figureref{fig:WeContainsOnlyEthPrime}, as a uniformly computable
  family.
  By now this is a familar situation.
  Consider the function on the left, observe that it is computed 
  by the machine sketched on the left of the figure, cite
  Church's Thesis to get that it has an index, and let that index be~$e$. 
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=p(x)$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=p(x)$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1025.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1026.pdf}}
    \caption{Flowcharts outlining the machine for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:WeContainsOnlyEthPrime}
  \end{figure}
  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to get the uniformily computable 
  family of functions on the right.
  \item
  The number~$e$ is fixed so $s(e,x)$ is a function only of the variable~$x$.
  To emphasize that, define $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ by $g(x)=s(e,x)$. 
  The Fixed Point Theorem gives a $m\in\N$ with 
  $\TMfcn_m=\TMfcn_{g(m)}=\TMfcn_{s(e,m)}$, which halts only when the 
  input is the $m$-th prime.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \autocite{Rogers}, p~214.
\end{credit} 
Prove that there exists $m\in\N$ such that
$W_m=\set{y\suchthat \TMfcn_m(y)\converges}=10^m$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Consider the function on the left.
  By Church's Thesis it is computable and so has an index;~let that index be~$e$. 
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=10^x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y=10^x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to get the uniformily computable 
  family of functions on the right.
  The number~$e$ is fixed so define $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ by $g(x)=s(e,x)$.
  The Fixed Point Theorem gives $k\in\N$ with 
  $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{g(k)}=\TMfcn_{s(e,k)}$, 
  and so $W_k=\set{10^x}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show there is an index~$e$ so that 
$W_e=\set{\TMfcn_e(x)\converges}=\set{0,1,\ldots\, e}$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  We use \cref{cor:ExistsPhiNWithRanN} as a guide.
  Consider the function on the left below.
  It is computed by the machine sketched on the left in 
  \figureref{fig:WeEqualsZeroOneUpToE}, so Church's Thesis gives that
  there is a Turing machine index for this machine; let it be~$e$.
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y\leq x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y\leq x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \tag{*}
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1023.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1024.pdf}}
    \caption{A sketch of the machine for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$, and the machine for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:WeEqualsZeroOneUpToE}
  \end{figure}
  Apply the \smn~Theorem to get the 
  family of machines sketched on the right, which compute
  the family of functions given on the right of~($*$).

  Because~$e$ is the index of the machine on the left, it is fixed.
  So $s(e,x)$ is a function of the single variable~$x$.
  Let $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ be defined by $g(x)=s(e,x)$.
  By the Fixed Point Theorem \cref{th:FixedPointThm}, the function~$g$ has a
  fixed point so that there is a~$k\in\N$ with
  $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{s(e,k)}$.
  Observe that $W_k=\set{0,1,\ldots{} k}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The Fixed Point Theorem says that for all~$f$ (which are computable and total)
there is an~$n$ so that $\TMfcn_n=\TMfcn_{f(n)}$.
What about the statement in which we flip the quantifiers:~for
all $n\in\N$, does 
there exist a total and computable function~$\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ so that
$\TMfcn_n=\TMfcn_{f(n)}$?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Yes, we can take $f(x)=x$.
  Or, if we want $f(n)\neq n$ then this is the Padding Lemma.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Let $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ be the function whose effect on Turing machines is
% that $\TM_{f(e)}$ is the same as $\TM_e$ except that the final
% states and non-final states are interchanged.
% Give a fixed point.
% \begin{answer}
% A fixed point is a function, not a machine.
% Consider the function $\TMfcn_{e}$ given by $\TMfcn_{e}(x)\diverges$ for all
% inputs~$x$.
% For any machine associated with that function,
% whether a state is final or not has no effect on the machine's behavior
% because whether a state is final is only relevant when the machine halts.
% So the transformed machine will have the same behavior.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \autocite{Rogers}, p~214.
\end{credit} 
Prove or disprove the existence of the set. 
\begin{exparts*}
\item $W_m=\set{\TMfcn_m(y)\converges}=\N-\set{m}$
\item $W_m=\set{x\suchthat \text{$\phi_m(x)$ diverges}}$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  The function on the left below is computed 
  by the machine sketched on the left of 
  \figureref{fig:WeContainsAllButE}.
  Church's Thesis gives that it has an index; let that index be~$e$. 
  \begin{equation*}
    \psi(x,y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y\neq x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
    \qquad
    \TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=
    \begin{cases}
      42       &\case{if $y\neq x$}  \\
      \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1029.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1030.pdf}}
    \caption{Sketches for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:WeContainsAllButE}
  \end{figure}%
  The \smn{} Theorem parametrizes~$x$ to get the family of functions on the 
  right.
  The number~$e$ is fixed so define $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ by $g(x)=s(e,x)$. 
  Apply the Fixed Point Theorem to get a $k\in\N$ with 
  $\TMfcn_k=\TMfcn_{g(k)}=\TMfcn_{s(e,k)}$, 
  and so $W_k=\N-\set{k}$.
  \item
  No such computably enumerable $W_m$ exists.
  The set $M=\set{m\suchthat \TMfcn_m(x) converges}$ is computably
  enumerable, by dovetailing.
  It is the complement of the given set, so if the given set were computably
  enumerable then the two would be computable.
  But showing that they are not computable is routine, for instance
  with Rice's Theorem.   
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Prove that there is a program that outputs its own source code, that is, 
% there is a Turing machine that outputs a string representation of itself.
% \begin{answer}
% Define a machine that outputs its input, so that it computes
% $\TMfcn(x)=x$.
% If given the input of the source code of a Turing machine $\TM$ then
% its output is the source of $\TM$.
% By the Fixed Point Theorem, there is some Turing machine
% $\TM$ such that $\TM$ has the same behavior as $\TMfcn(\TM)$.
% But that behavior is to output the source code of $\TM$.
% So this machine outputs its own source.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\Cref{cor:ExistsPhiNWithRanN} shows that there is a computable function
$\TMfcn_n$ with domain~$\set{n}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Show that there is a computable function $\TMfcn_m$ with range~$\set{m}$.
\item
Is there a computable function $\TMfcn_m$ with range~$\set{2m}$?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Consider the function $\psi(x,y)=x$.
  By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior\Dash
  it is computed by the machine on the left of 
  \figureref{fig:RangeOfPhiMEqualsSetM}\Dash so let that be
  machine~$\TM_e$.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1031.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1032.pdf}}
    \caption{Flowcharts for $\psi=\TMfcn_{e}$ and for $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}$.}
    \label{fig:RangeOfPhiMEqualsSetM}
  \end{figure}%
  Apply the \smn{} Theorem to get the machine on the right, 
  which computes the family of functions $\TMfcn_{s(e,x)}(y)=x$.
  There, $e$ is fixed since it is just the index of the Turing machine on 
  the left, so let $\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ be given by $g(x)=s(e,x)$.
  By the Fixed Point Theorem there is an $m\in\N$ where
  $\TMfcn_m=\TMfcn_{g(m)}=\TMfcn_{s(e,m)}$, which has range~$\set{m}$.
  \item This is the same as the prior answer, but starting with
  $\psi(x,y)=2x$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Extend the Fixed Point Theorem to show that any total computable

% \end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Prove that if $\map{f}{\N^2}{\N}$ is a total computable function of
